Quizartinib
Vanflyta
Kinase inhibitor
Quizartinib dihydrochloride [1-(5-tert-butyl-1,2-oxazol-3-yl)-3(4-{7-[2-(morpholin-4-yl)ethoxy]imidazo[2,1b][1,3]benzothiazol-2-yl}phenyl)urea dihydrochloride]
Daiichi Sankyo, Inc.
Tablets
Vanflyta should be administered in combination with standard
chemotherapy at a dose of 35.4 mg once daily for two weeks in
each cycle of induction. See Section 6.2.2. General Dosing and
Therapeutic Individualization for additional information.
In combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline
induction and standard cytarabine consolidation
chemotherapy, and as continuation monotherapy following
consolidation, for the treatment of adult patients with newly
diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML) that is FMS-like
tyrosine kinase 3 internal tandem duplication (FLT3-ITD)
positive as detected by an FDA-approved test.
Regular Approval

Recommendation on
Regulatory Action
Recommended In combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline
Indication(s)/Population(s) induction and cytarabine consolidation, and as maintenance
(if applicable) monotherapy following consolidation chemotherapy, for the
treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed acute

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Proposed Trade Name:
Established Name:
Therapeutic Class:
Chemical Class:
Pharmacologic Class:
Dosage Forms:

Vanflyta
Quizartinib
Antineoplastic
Small molecule
Kinase inhibitor
Oral tablet (17.7 mg and 26.5 mg)

Quizartinib is a new molecular entity, an orally administered, small molecule kinase inhibitor.
Quizartinib was previously evaluated under NDA 212166 for the treatment of adults with
relapsed or refractory acute myeloid leukemia (AML) which is FMS-like tyrosine kinase 3
internal tandem duplication (FLT3-ITD) positive, as detected by an FDA-approved test. However,
that application received a Complete Response on June 14, 2019, due to several concerns,
including failure to demonstrate substantial evidence of effectiveness and serious risks
including fatal cardiac events. The current NDA 216993 was submitted for a newly proposed
indication in combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and standard
cytarabine consolidation chemotherapy, and as continuation monotherapy following
consolidation, for the treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed AML that is FLT3-ITD
positive as detected by an FDA-approved test. The proposed dosage is 35.4 mg (40 mg salt)
orally once daily on Days 8 to 21 of each cycle of induction (or Days 6 to 19 for Induction cycle
2) with cytarabine and anthracycline and on Days 6 to 19 of each cycle of consolidation with
high-dose cytarabine. Following induction and consolidation, the proposed dosage of
quizartinib as maintenance monotherapy is 26.5 mg (30 mg salt) once daily Days 1 through 14
of the 28-day cycle, and if the QT interval corrected by Fridericia’s formula (QTcF) is less than or
equal to 450 msec, the dosage is increased to 53 mg (60 mg salt) once daily for the remainder of
the 28-day cycle; maintenance therapy may be continued until disease progression or
unacceptable toxicity for up to thirty-six 28-day cycles.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
The review team recommends regular approval for quizartinib “in combination with standard
cytarabine and anthracycline induction and cytarabine consolidation, and as maintenance
monotherapy following consolidation chemotherapy, for the treatment of adult patients with
newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML) that is FLT3 internal tandem duplication (ITD)positive as detected by an FDA-approved test.” The recommendation is based on the findings of
improved overall survival (OS) in Study AC220-A-U302 (QuANTUM-First). Supportive evidence
24
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
was also demonstrated with data on complete remission (CR) and CR with partial hematologic
recovery (CRh) rates from Study AC220-007 (QuANTUM-R).
Quizartinib was evaluated in one randomized (1:1), double-blind, placebo-controlled Phase 3
study in adult patients ages 18 to 75 years with newly diagnosed AML with FLT3-ITD mutation.
Patients were randomized to quizartinib (n=268) or placebo (n=271) in combination with
standard induction and consolidation chemotherapy, followed by a maintenance monotherapy
phase per the initial randomization for up to 36 months. Randomization was stratified by age,
white blood count at diagnosis, and region. The demographic and disease characteristics of the
randomized patients were balanced between arms.
The final analysis of QuANTUM-First included a prespecified assessment of the primary
endpoint of OS, measured from the date of randomization until death from any cause. At the
time of analysis, there was an OS advantage in patients who received quizartinib in addition to
standard chemotherapy compared to those who received placebo in combination with standard
chemotherapy with HR 0.78 (95% confidence interval [CI] 0.62, 0.98; p=0.0324).
The improvement in OS on the quizartinib + chemotherapy arm versus the placebo +
chemotherapy arm was generally consistent across various disease subsets (e.g., intermediate
cytogenetics HR 0.76, unfavorable cytogenetics HR 0.45, FLT3-ITD variant allelic frequency [VAF]
≥3 to ≤25% HR 0.87, FLT3-ITD VAF >25% to ≤50% HR 0.77, and FLT3-ITD VAF >50% HR 0.53).
Patients with favorable cytogenetics and NPM1 wild-type patients, however, had HR 2.07 (95%
CI 0.71-5.97) and HR 1.01 (95% CI 0.73-1.41), respectively. Given the small sample size for the
favorable cytogenetic risk group (N=14 quizartinib + chemotherapy, N=19 placebo +
chemotherapy) and the post-hoc nature of these analyses, no firm conclusions can be drawn.
Secondary endpoints on QuANTUM-First included event-free survival (EFS) and CR rate. EFS was
defined as the time from randomization until the date of the earliest of refractory disease or
treatment failure determined at the end of the Induction Phase (ITF), relapse, or death from any
cause. ITF was defined as failure to achieve CR within 42 days of the start of the last cycle of
induction. At the time of the final analysis, EFS was not significantly improved on the quizartinib
+ chemotherapy arm (HR 0.92, 95% CI 0.75-1.11; p-value 0.2371).
FDA-adjudicated CRs were achieved by 147 (55%) patients on the quizartinib + chemotherapy
arm and 150 (55%) patients on the placebo + chemotherapy arm. The median duration of CR
(DOCR) was 38.6 (95% CI, 21.9 - not evaluable) months on the quizartinib + chemotherapy arm
and was 12.4 (95% CI, 8.8-22.7) months on the placebo + chemotherapy arm.
Following completion of consolidation therapy and/or hematopoietic stem cell transplantation
(HSCT), maintenance therapy (termed “continuation” therapy in the protocol) was initiated by
39% of patients on QuANTUM-First. FDA guidance has been to rerandomize prior to

QuANTUM-First and the contribution of treatment effects of quizartinib from different phases
of therapy cannot be certain, the clinical data demonstrated a later separation in the survival
curves and relatively compelling results on OS in patients who received maintenance postconsolidation chemotherapy, such that limiting the indication for quizartinib to induction and
consolidation therapy may limit the efficacy of the regimen in this patient population (see
Section 8.1).
The review team recommends a limitation of use that quizartinib “is not indicated as
maintenance monotherapy following allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation
(HSCT); improvement in overall survival with [quizartinib] in this setting has not been
demonstrated.” The applicant did not establish safety and effectiveness of quizartinib in
patients who received maintenance post-HSCT, a biologically distinct subgroup of patients with
different benefit-risk considerations. The post-HSCT population has received a known
potentially curative therapy, which may preclude any further benefit from a prolonged
maintenance regimen, and these patients are prone to additional risks, particularly infectious
risks, that make the benefit-risk considerations distinct in this patient subgroup. Furthermore,
the design and conduct of the trial was inadequate to support an evaluation of the benefit-risk
in this subgroup given the relatively small sample size and post-hoc exploratory nature utilizing
post-randomization subgroup comparisons. The limited data that are available from the
QuANTUM-First trial in this patient population suggest that use of quizartinib in the
maintenance setting may add toxicity without added benefit (see Sections 8.1 & 8.2). Recent
results also showed lack of benefit of gilteritinib, another FLT3-inhibitor, that was studied in a
large randomized, controlled maintenance therapy trial post-HSCT (Astellas and BMT CTN
Announce Topline Results from Phase 3 MORPHO Trial of Gilteritinib, 2023).
Efficacy of quizartinib was supplemented by data from Study QuANTUM-R. QuANTUM-R was an
open-label, randomized (2:1), controlled, multicenter clinical trial of quizartinib monotherapy
versus choice of salvage chemotherapy in patients with relapsed or refractory (R/R) FLT3-ITD
positive AML. The trial randomized 367 adult patients, including 245 patients on the quizartinib
arm, with a target dose of 53 mg orally once daily. The primary endpoint was OS, and the study
showed a statistically positive result with hazard ratio [HR] 0.77 (95% confidence interval [CI]:
0.59-0.99; p = 0.019; median OS 26.9 weeks vs 20.4 weeks). However, there was lack of internal
consistency in other efficacy endpoints (event-free survival HR 0.90, CR rate 4% quizartinib
versus 1% chemotherapy), an imbalance in use of HSCT favoring the quizartinib arm, and
differences between arms in patients randomized and not treated and early censored that
favored the quizartinib arm, leading to the lack of a robust analysis of OS. These uncertain
efficacy results contributed to the Complete Response action on NDA 212166. However, the
QuANTUM-R study demonstrated quantifiable single-agent activity of quizartinib compared to

Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
Quizartinib is an oral Class III receptor tyrosine kinase (RTK) inhibitor that binds to FLT3 and several other Class III RTKs, with a proposed
indication in combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and standard cytarabine consolidation chemotherapy, and as
continuation monotherapy following consolidation, for the treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed AML that is FLT3-ITD positive as
detected by an FDA-approved test. Quizartinib was previously evaluated under NDA 212166 for monotherapy treatment of adults with relapsed
or refractory FLT3-ITD positive AML and received a Complete Response on June 14, 2019. This new NDA is seeking an indication for quizartinib
in combination with standard chemotherapy in patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-ITD positive AML.
FLT3-ITD positive AML is an aggressive form of AML associated with increased relapse rates and inferior survival. Standard of care for eligible
patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-ITD positive AML includes treatment with induction and consolidation chemotherapy in combination with
the FDA-approved kinase inhibitor midostaurin. Midostaurin was approved based on a randomized, placebo-controlled trial comparing
midostaurin plus chemotherapy versus placebo plus chemotherapy in patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-mutated AML ages 18-60 years.
Although the indication was not restricted to adults ≤ 60 years, the lack of enrollment of older patients on the pivotal study led to some
uncertainty regarding treatment of older patients with chemotherapy plus midostaurin. Furthermore, long-term outcomes in patients with
FLT3-ITD mutated AML continue to be suboptimal, leaving this patient population with a significant unmet medical need.
The recommendation for regular approval is based on the efficacy results from one randomized, controlled trial evaluating quizartinib in
combination with standard chemotherapy in patients with newly-diagnosed FLT3-ITD positive AML, with supportive data from a randomized,
controlled trial evaluating quizartinib monotherapy in patients with relapsed or refractory FLT3-ITD positive AML. Study QuANTUM-First was a
randomized (1:1), double-blind, placebo-controlled trial of quizartinib in combination with chemotherapy (n=268) versus placebo in
combination with chemotherapy (n=271). Randomization was stratified by age, white blood count at diagnosis, and region. Patients
randomized to quizartinib plus chemotherapy had significantly longer survival compared to placebo plus chemotherapy (HR 0.78; 95% CI: 0.62 –
0.98; p-value = 0.032). The HR for EFS was 0.92 and CR rate was 55% on both arms. Median DOCR was 38.6 months (95% CI, 21.9 to not
evaluable) and 12.4 months (95% CI, 8.8 to 22.7), respectively. Analysis of OS in patients treated with post-consolidation maintenance
28
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the
positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
suggested a benefit to maintenance monotherapy with quizartinib (HR 0.68), but this was driven by patients who received maintenance postconsolidation chemotherapy (HR 0.40), with no apparent benefit in patients post-HSCT (HR 1.62).
Study QuANTUM-R was an open-label, randomized (2:1), controlled, multicenter clinical trial of quizartinib monotherapy versus choice of
salvage chemotherapy in patients with R/R FLT3-ITD positive AML. The trial served as the basis of the initial NDA 212166 submitted for
quizartinib. The trial randomized 367 patients, including 245 on the quizartinib arm, with a target dose of 53 mg daily. OS was statistically
positive with a HR 0.77 (95% CI 0.59, 0.99; p=0.019), but issues with the study’s conduct called into question the robustness of the OS results.
The CR+CRh rate for quizartinib was 11%, with TI achieved by 26% for at least 56 days post-baseline. Thus, quizartinib had measurable, albeit
low, single agent activity in patients with R/R FLT3-ITD positive AML.
Safety of quizartinib was demonstrated on the QuANTUM-First study, as well as in the Applicant’s Integrated Summary of Safety (ISS) AML
population, which included the quizartinib arm of QuANTUM-R and several single-arm studies of quizartinib in combination with chemotherapy
and as monotherapy in patients with AML. Common adverse reactions (> 30%) not related to a laboratory evaluation for the combination of
quizartinib plus chemotherapy from Study QuANTUM-First were febrile neutropenia, diarrhea, mucositis, and nausea. Frequent serious adverse
reactions (> 5%) included febrile neutropenia. QT interval prolongation occurred in 14% of patients on the quizartinib arm, with 3% being Grade
3-4 in severity. Of 1,081 patients in the ISS AML population, torsades de pointes occurred in 0.2%, cardiac arrest occurred in 0.6% (0.4% with a
fatal outcome), and 0.1% experienced ventricular fibrillation. Adverse reactions were mitigated by electrocardiogram (ECG) monitoring,
electrolyte monitoring and correction, anti-infectives, and transfusions as indicated. The seriousness of the QT prolongation and arrhythmia risk
warrants a boxed warning and specific instructions to patients regarding the risks and optimal management. Furthermore, it was determined
that a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS) with Elements to Assure Safe Use (ETASU) was necessary to educate prescribers of the
unique and potentially fatal risk of arrhythmias, as well as the risk factors for, and strategies to mitigate QT prolongation, torsades de pointe,
and fatal cardiac arrest.
In terms of safety in the maintenance phase on QuANTUM-First, common adverse reactions in quizartinib-treated patients during maintenance
included neutropenia, gastrointestinal toxicity (diarrhea, nausea, abdominal pain, vomiting, decreased appetite), thrombocytopenia, upper
respiratory infections, herpesvirus infections, mucositis, hypertransaminasemia, QTc prolonged, musculoskeletal pain, cough, and headache.
Most of the reported adverse reactions during this phase occurred during the first year of maintenance treatment. Notably, patients who
received maintenance therapy post-HSCT had a higher rate of adverse reactions to quizartinib (on average 8% higher) than for patients who
received quizartinib maintenance post-consolidation chemotherapy without HSCT. Thus, the safety of extended maintenance therapy with

quizartinib for 3 years post-HSCT was deemed unacceptable in the face of the possible OS detriment in this subgroup and lack of benefit of
gilteritinib, another FLT3 inhibitor, in a properly conducted randomized, controlled trial in the post-HSCT maintenance setting (Astellas and
BMT CTN Announce Topline Results from Phase 3 MORPHO Trial of Gilteritinib, 2023).
In conclusion, the primary endpoint used to assess efficacy in the pivotal QuANTUM-First trial was OS. The median OS was 31.9 months (95% CI
21.8, not evaluable) on the quizartinib + chemotherapy arm compared to 15.1 months (95% CI 13.2, 26.8) on the placebo + chemotherapy arm
with HR 0.78 (95% CI 0.62, 0.98); p = 0.0324. Analyses to discern the impact of the maintenance phase of quizartinib monotherapy on the OS
results suggested a positive effect overall (HR 0.68), but there was inadequate evidence to establish a favorable benefit-risk profile in the
subgroup of patients who received maintenance post-HSCT (HR 1.62 and added toxicity), leading to inclusion of only post-consolidation
chemotherapy maintenance in the indication statement and a limitation of use for maintenance monotherapy post-HSCT. These findings were
supported by external information from the results of the dedicated post-HSCT randomized placebo-controlled maintenance clinical trial of
gilteritinib. Results of QuANTUM-R showing HR for OS 0.77 for quizartinib monotherapy versus choice of salvage chemotherapy, CR+CRh rate of
11%, and transfusion independence 26% support the effectiveness of quizartinib in patients with FLT3-ITD positive AML.

in patients who received maintenance post-HSCT. Patients who

TI rate indicate some monotherapy activity of
quizartinib in patients with FLT3-ITD positive
AML.

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Acute myeloid leukemia (AML) is the most common acute leukemia in adults (Siegel, 2022; De
Kouchkovsky, 2016). The United States (US) Surveillance, Epidemiology and End Results (SEER)
Program of the National Cancer Institute (NCI) estimated that 20,240 people in the US would be
diagnosed with AML in the year 2021, and 11,400 of these patients would die from the disease
(accounting for 1.9% of all cancer deaths; SEER, 2021).
The incidence of AML increases with age, ranging from 1.8 cases per 100,000 people aged
<65 years to 17.6 cases per 100,000 people aged >65 years. More than half of the patients with
newly diagnosed AML in developed countries are over 65 years of age, with a median age at
diagnosis of 67 years (Heuser, 2020).
Overall, the outcome of patients with AML is poor, with a 5-year survival rate of 40%, which
rapidly declines with increasing age at diagnosis (Thein, 2013). Outcome is influenced by
multiple factors, both disease-specific factors (cytogenetic and/or molecular genetic
alterations, including FMS-like tyrosine kinase 3 [FLT3], nucleophosmin 1 [NPM1], and others)
and patient-specific factors, such as age, performance status (PS), organ function, and other
comorbidities (Döhner, 2017). Age is the most prominent patient-specific risk factor, while
chromosomal aberrations/genetic mutations have been considered the strongest
disease-specific risk factors (Kottaridis, 2001). The assessment of genetic mutations, including
FLT3, as prognostic factors has become increasingly important for risk assessment and in the
treatment of AML as recommended by both the European LeukemiaNet (ELN) and the National
Comprehensive Cancer Network (NCCN) guidelines (Döhner, 2017; Pollyea, 2021).
Overexpression of the FLT3 receptor occurs in nearly all cases of AML, and mutations in FLT3
represent one of the most common genetic alterations occurring in approximately 30% of adult
patients with newly diagnosed AML (Papaemmanuil, 2016; Kennedy, 2020).
There are 2 types of FLT3 mutations: internal tandem duplication (ITD) mutations in the
juxta-membrane domain (FLT3-ITD) and point mutations or deletion in the tyrosine kinase
domain (FLT3-TKD). The FLT3-ITD mutation is more common than the TKD mutation, and these
are found in 20% to 25% and 7% to 10% of all AML cases, respectively. Two methods are used
to define FLT3-ITD positivity: the allelic ratio (AR), which calculates the ratio of the area under
the plasma concentration-time curve (AUC) of mutant to wild-type (WT) alleles
(FLT3-ITD/FLT3-WT), and variant allele frequency (VAF), which is defined as the number of
mutant alleles divided by the number of total alleles, reported as a percentage (FLT335
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
ITD/FLT3-WT + FLT3-ITD). Patients harboring a FLT3-ITD mutation, particularly those with a
high AR defined as a FLT3-ITD to FLT3-WT ratio of ≥0.5, show increased relapse rates and
inferior survival (Papaemmanuil, 2016; Patel, 2012; Thiede, 2002; Kottaridis, 2001). FLT3-ITD
mutations are associated with a higher leukemic burden with marked leukocytosis and higher
blast percentage. Females are affected more frequently and there is evidence that the
incidence of FLT3-ITD mutations decreases with age, with an incidence of up to 35% in patients
between 20 and 59 years compared with 16% to 20% in patients older than 60 years (Konig,
2015). In contrast, FLT3-TKD mutations have not been associated with a consistent prognostic
impact (Mead, 2007).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that AML is a serious disease that still presents
therapeutic challenges since 5-year survival remains poor. FDA also agrees that FLT3-ITD is one
of the most common genetic subtypes of AML.

Analysis of Current Treatment Options for Newly Diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+)
AML
For subjects who are eligible to receive chemotherapy, standard chemotherapy is the mainstay
of treatment for newly diagnosed AML, and cytarabine in combination with an anthracycline
remains the standard induction therapy, regardless of cytogenetic or molecular abnormalities,
with complete remission (CR) achieved in 60% to 85% of adults ≤60 years of age and 40% to
60% in adults >60 years of age (Döhner, 2015). For subjects who achieve a CR, the current
guidelines recommend that patients must be offered post-induction therapy to eradicate
residual disease and prevent relapse. Available options for consolidation include chemotherapy
and/or allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (allo-HSCT) depending on the risk
group. Allo-HSCT has improved the prognosis of patients with AML, but relapse remains high
and non-relapse mortality associated with allo-HSCT is as high as 20% (Styczyński, 2020).
Overall, cure rates following chemotherapy with or without HSCT are only 35% to 40% in
patients under age 60 and 5% to 15% in patients over age 60 (Döhner, 2015).
Per NCCN and ELN guidelines (Pollyea, 2021; Döhner, 2017), for AML patients with FLT3
mutations (ITD or TKD) <60 years of age, midostaurin should be added to standard
chemotherapy as part of frontline treatment. This recommendation is based on the results of
the RATIFY study in newly diagnosed AML subjects with FLT3 mutations (ITD and/or TKD)
<60 years of age (Stone, 2017). For AML patients with FLT3 mutations ≥60 years benefiting
from intensive cytarabine-based induction therapy, the NCCN recommends also that
midostaurin be added to standard chemotherapy (Pollyea, 2021). For those who are not
candidates for intensive chemotherapy, low-intensive regimens including venetoclax with
hypomethylating agents such as azacitidine or decitabine are recommended (Pollyea, 2021;
36
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Döhner, 2017).
Further, the safety and efficacy of midostaurin were evaluated in a single-arm Phase 2 study,
AMLSG 16-10, in which midostaurin was added to intensive chemotherapy followed by
allo-HSCT and single-agent midostaurin maintenance therapy in adult patients with FLT3-ITD (+)
AML (Schlenk, 2019). In this study, the authors reported that while older patients (61 to 70
years old) may benefit from midostaurin, outcomes were inferior to those observed in younger
patients (18 to 60 years old) in terms of total CR rate (including CR with incomplete neutrophil
or platelet recovery [CRi]), event-free survival (EFS), and 2-year OS. Less than 50% of patients
finished the 1-year intended maintenance therapy, and the median time of maintenance
therapy was 9 months after allo-HSCT and 10.5 months after high-dose cytarabine (HDAC) with
premature termination mainly a result of causes other than relapse (gastrointestinal toxicity
and infections).

The Applicant’s Position:
In the newly diagnosed FLT3-mutated AML treatment setting, midostaurin, a multitargeted
kinase inhibitor, is the only drug that has been approved thus far based on the RATIFY study
results. Given the poor prognosis of patients with FLT3-ITD (+) AML and the high risk of relapse,
the unmet medical need remains high and new treatment options are urgently needed.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that midostaurin added to induction and
consolidation chemotherapy is the only treatment specifically approved for newly diagnosed
FLT3-mutated AML. We also agree that despite improvement in overall survival seen with the
addition of midostaurin to induction and consolidation chemotherapy, that long-term overall
survival remains guarded and represents continued unmet medical need.

3 Regulatory Background
U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
The Applicant’s Position:
Quizartinib is not currently registered or approved in the US. In September 2018, a new drug
application (NDA 212166) for quizartinib was submitted for the treatment of patients with
relapsed/refractory (R/R) FLT3-ITD (+) AML based on Study AC220-007 (QuANTUM-R). The
application was the subject of an Oncologic Drug Advisory Committee (ODAC) meeting in May
2019, and the FDA subsequently issued a Complete Response Letter in June 2019.
Quizartinib was approved by the Ministry of Health, Labor and Welfare (MHLW) in Japan in
Jun 2019 for the treatment of R/R FLT3-ITD (+) AML and is currently marketed in Japan under
the trade name VANFLYTA®.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with Applicant’s position. Please refer to the FDA May 2019 ODAC materials
(https://public4.pagefreezer.com/browse/FDA/04-032022T19:30/https://www.fda.gov/advisory-committees/advisory-committee-calendar/may-142019-meeting-oncologic-drugs-advisory-committee-meeting-announcement-0514201905142019 )

4 Significant Issues from Other Review Disciplines Pertinent to Clinical
Conclusions on Efficacy and Safety
Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
The Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI) conducted inspections for QuANTUM-First at a
clinical site in Valencia, Spain (Site 3402). This site was selected given high subject enrollment
with remarkable OS results. Inspection review of the site led to a classification of No Action
Indicated. The Applicant (Daiichi Sankyo, Inc.) was also audited. The classification for the
Applicant inspection was also No Action Indicated. Based on these inspection results, the study
data derived from the inspected clinical site was considered reliable in support of the requested
indication.

Product Quality
Quizartinib drug product (Vanflyta) is presented as round, film-coated tablets for oral use
containing 17.7 or 26.5 mg quizartinib. The capsules are debossed with “DSC511” and
“DSC512,” respectively. Inactive ingredients include hydroxypropyl betadex, microcrystalline
cellulose, and magnesium stearate. The tablet coating contains hypromellose, talc, triacetin and
(b) (4)
titanium dioxide. All excipients, except for
are comprised of
(b) (4)
compendial components, but the
are comprised of compendial
components. The CMC reviewer noted that the quantity of each excipient used in the
manufacture of quizartinib tablets is below the “maximum potency” for oral administration as
reported in the FDA Inactive Ingredient Database. Individual unspecified impurities are
proposed to be controlled at a limit consistent with ICH Q3B (R2). The drug product is supplied
in bottles of 14 or 28 tablets with an expiry of 60 months when stored at USP controlled room
temperature.
There were no outstanding safety issues identified for the manufacturing process or from the
facilities inspections. The Applicant claimed a categorical exclusion from the requirement for an
environmental assessment and the claim was accepted. Approval of the NDA was
recommended by the Product Quality review team.
Lastly, there are no impurity issues from a Pharmacology/Toxicology perspective. The proposed
specifications for impurities in the drug substance and drug product that exceed the ICH
qualification thresholds are justified. The Applicant conducted a 28-day impurity qualification
toxicology study in rats to justify the higher limits.

Devices and Companion Diagnostic Issues
The Applicant is seeking an indication for patients with newly-diagnosed AML limited to those
who have a FLT3-ITD mutation, which is the target of quizartinib. In Study QuANTUM-First,
patients were selected based on detection of a FLT3-ITD mutation with a clinical trial assay, and
the results were confirmed retrospectively using the companion diagnostic LeukoStrat® CDx
FLT3 Mutation Assay. It was determined that a device to select patients for therapy would be
required for safe use of quizartinib when marketed. The Applicant cross-referenced PMA
P160040 for the LeukoStrat® CDx FLT3 Mutation Assay, which identifies the FLT3-ITD mutation.
The Center for Devices and Radiological Health (CDRH) reviewed the LeukoStrat® CDx FLT3
Mutation Assay for its intended use as a companion diagnostic to identify AML patients with a
FLT3-ITD mutation for treatment with quizartinib under P160040/S011 (MDUFA date
4/12/2023). Following review, CDRH decided that the information provided was acceptable and
the sPMA is approvable pending approval of quizartinib.

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
Quizartinib (AC220) is a receptor tyrosine kinase (RTK) inhibitor that binds to Fms Related
Receptor Tyrosine Kinase 3 (FLT3) and several other RTKs. Kinase inhibition is mediated through
competition with the adenosine triphosphate (ATP) binding pocket of the kinase. Quizartinib
binding is relatively specific for FLT3 (Kd = 1.3 nM) and KIT (Kd = 4.9 nM), as is the active
metabolite AC886, with Kd values of 0.54 nM for FLT3 and 0.97 nM for KIT. In binding assays,
quizartinib and AC886 showed approximately 10-fold lower affinity towards the FLT3 internal
tandem duplication (FLT3-ITD) mutation (Kd= 9.4 nM and 5.8 nM for quizartinib and AC886,
respectively) compared to FLT3 (Kd= 1.3 nM and 0.54 nM for quizartinib and AC886,
respectively).
Quizartinib pharmacology was characterized in vitro and/or in vivo in MV4-11 cells and RS4;11
cells. MV4-11 cells are a human leukemia cell line that expresses FLT3 with an internal tandem
duplication (ITD) activating mutation. The RS4;11 cell line is a leukemia cell line that expresses
wild-type FLT3 and is not dependent on FLT3 activity for proliferation. Western blot analysis
showed quizartinib induces concentration-dependent inhibition of autophosphorylation of FLT
protein in MV4-11 cells in vitro. Quizartinib inhibited proliferation of cultured MV4-11 cells
with an IC50 of 0.3 nM, whereas it had more than 1000-fold weaker activity against the RS4;11
control cell line (IC50 = 990 nM). Using the MV4-11 cell line, quizartinib also displayed in vivo
anti-tumor activity in a mouse tumor xenograft model and increased the lifespan of animals.
Quizartinib was tested for inhibitory activity against a panel of 118 non-kinase enzymes,
receptors, channels, and transporters. Quizartinib produced greater than 50% inhibition in five
of the 118 targets, but was more than 1000-fold less potent against those targets than for FLT3.
Standalone in vitro and in vivo safety pharmacology studies were conducted to assess the
cardiovascular risks of quizartinib and AC886, whereas endpoints to assess the effects of
quizartinib on the central nervous and respiratory systems were incorporated into repeat-dose
general toxicology studies. To assess the cardiovascular risks, in vitro studies examined the
effects of quizartinib and its active metabolite on cloned human ether-a-go-go-related gene
(hERG) potassium, L-type calcium, hKvLQT1/mink potassium, and early and late sodium
channels. Additionally, ex vivo studies were conducted using isolated rabbit hearts and
ventricular tissues and in dispersed canine cardiomyocytes, while in vivo cardiovascular data
was collected in the repeat-dose toxicology studies in dogs and monkeys, as well as in a
44
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
dedicated study in telemetered monkeys. Overall, these studies demonstrated quizartinib
exposure was associated with inhibition of potassium channel currents, prolongation of QT
intervals, and transient increases in blood pressure.
The pharmacokinetics of quizartinib were characterized in the mouse, rat, dog, and monkey.
Following oral administration of quizartinib, the plasma exposures were approximately dose
proportional in the range of doses tested in nonclinical species. Female exposures were 1.4- to
2-fold higher than males in rats and dogs. Bioavailability was lower in monkeys than in rats and
dogs (15% versus 30% to 40%). Both quizartinib and AC886 are highly bound to plasma
proteins (≥99%) in animals and humans. Quizartinib is well distributed in all species. Following
oral administration of radioactive quizartinib in rats, the highest levels of radioactivity observed
at Tmax were found in the GI tract, pancreas, kidney, liver, lymphoid tissues (spleen, thymus,
and lymph nodes), adrenal gland, pituitary, thyroid gland, adipose tissues, myocardium, lung,
bone marrow, and male and female reproductive organs (such as prostate and ovary). The
lowest levels were in the central nervous system and bone. In animals, the excretion was
primarily hepatobiliary, with low levels excreted in the urine.
Repeat-dose toxicology studies were conducted in Sprague-Dawley rats, beagle dogs, and
cynomolgus monkeys given quizartinib dihydrochloride for up to 13 weeks. All dose levels
employed were adequately justified. Hematologic and lymphoid toxicities were common and
attributable to exaggerated pharmacology. Target organs of toxicity included the bone marrow
(rats, dogs, and monkeys), liver (dogs and monkeys), kidney (rats and dogs), thymus (rats, dogs,
and monkeys), ovary, vagina, and testes (rats and monkeys). Toxicologically significant
microscopic findings included bone marrow hypocellularity, extramedullary hematopoiesis,
liver crystals, single cell necrosis of the renal tubule and renal tubular basophilia and crystals,
and thymic lymphoid atrophy/necrosis. When quizartinib was administered to juvenile rats, the
toxicities were similar to those in adult rats. A 28-day repeat-dose toxicology study conducted
in rats supports the proposed related substance impurity specifications, which are higher than
ICH qualification thresholds.
In an embryo-fetal development (EFD) study in rats, quizartinib at doses resulting in exposures
approximately 3 times the exposure at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) was
associated with adverse developmental outcomes including structural abnormalities (anasarca
and edema) and alterations to growth (lower fetal weights and effects on skeletal ossification).
The embryofetal toxicities occurred in the absence of maternal toxicities. Because of the
potential for serious adverse reactions in a breastfed child, patients will be advised that
breastfeeding is not recommended during treatment and for 1 month after the last dose; the
recommendation takes into consideration the elimination half-life of quizartinib and the active
metabolite, AC886. Based on the EFD study and genotoxicity findings, female patients of
reproductive potential will be advised to use effective contraception during treatment and for
45
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
at least 7 months after the last dose, and male patients with female partners of reproductive
potential will be advised to use effective contraception during treatment and for at least 4
months after the last dose.
In genotoxicity studies, quizartinib was mutagenic in a bacterial reverse mutation assay.
Quizartinib was not genotoxic in the following assays: mouse lymphoma thymidine kinase
mutation assay, chromosome aberration assay in human peripheral blood lymphocytes, and rat
bone marrow micronucleus assay (tested to the maximum tolerated dose, MTD). Of note, an
equivocal result (positive genotoxicity but within the range of historical control data) at a dose
approximating the MTD was obtained in the micronucleus assessment in a 28-day rat toxicology
study. An additional genotoxicity study was conducted in transgenic F344 homozygous Big Blue
rats, and the results were negative. No carcinogenicity studies were conducted or are required
to support the marketing of quizartinib for the current indication.
Fertility studies in animals have not been conducted with quizartinib; however, adverse findings
in male and female reproductive systems were observed in repeat-dose toxicity studies in rats
and monkeys. In female rats, ovarian cysts and vaginal mucification modifications were
observed at an exposure comparable to the MRHD based on area under the curve (AUC).
Findings in female monkeys included atrophy of the uterus, ovary, and vagina at approximately
0.2 times the MRHD. In male rats, testicular seminiferous tubular degeneration and failure of
sperm release were observed at approximately 6 times the MRHD. Findings in male monkeys
included germ cell depletion in the testes at approximately 0.4 times the MRHD. Findings were
reversible after a recovery period of about one month, except the vaginal mucosal
modifications in the female rats.
The nonclinical pharmacology and toxicology data submitted to this NDA are adequate to
support the approval of quizartinib for the proposed indication.

for quizartinib.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA acknowledges the tabulated information above. The following provides a more detailed
review of the in vitro and in vivo studies.
Research has identified molecular targets for treating AML. Among molecular abnormalities,
FLT3 mutations are frequent and well characterized actionable mutations in AML. FLT3
encodes a receptor tyrosine kinase expressed on normal hematopoietic stem/progenitor cells.
FLT3 dimerizes and autophosphorylates upon the binding of the FLT3 ligand (FLT3L), activating
the intracellular tyrosine kinase domain (TKD), which causes phosphorylation of downstream
molecules and activation of signaling cascades that promote the transcription of genes
47
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
regulating survival, proliferation and differentiation. Mutations of FLT3 resulting in constitutive
signaling are common in AML, including internal tandem duplications in the juxtamembrane
domain (FLT3-ITD) and point mutations. Patients with AML with FLT3-ITDs may have a poor
prognosis.
Using an array of in vitro and in vivo studies, the binding affinity and kinetics of quizartinib and
its metabolite AC886 have been characterized, as well as the inhibition of FLT3 activity and antiproliferative effects in leukemia cells.
In Vitro Studies
Binding assay (cell-free studies)
The binding affinity and kinetics of quizartinib and AC886 were evaluated for RTKs. KinomeScan
technology was used to determine the biochemical potency and selectivity of quizartinib and
AC886 against panels of 441 and 391 kinases, respectively (Study #NR0119 and #NR0118). The
assay measured the ability of the test article to compete with the interaction between the
target kinase and a known ATP site binding ligand. Quizartinib and AC886 displayed high
binding affinity to RTKs (with Kd values <100 nM) with similar characteristics and bound to a few
closely related RTKs with comparable Kd values.
Quizartinib had lower affinity towards the FLT3 internal tandem duplication, FLT3-ITD (Kd= 9.4
nM), compared to FLT3 (Kd= 1.3 nM). This trend was also noted for the AC886 metabolite (Kd=
5.8 nM for FLT3-ITD and 0.54 nM for FLT3).

The total (bound + unbound) Cmax values of quizartinib and AC886 are 0.94 µM and 0.45 µM,
respectively, in subjects taking an oral dose of 53 mg quizartinib (free base; Study No. 2689-CL2004).
Intracellular inhibition of FLT3 activity
The MV4-11 cell line is derived from human leukemia that expresses the FLT3-ITD activating
mutation that results in constitutive activation of FLT3 (Yee et al., Blood, 100: 2941-2949,
2002). Quizartinib appears to bind within the catalytic domain of the FLT3 protein, competing
with ATP, thereby inhibiting the ability of the enzyme to phosphorylate proteins. Quizartinib
induced concentration-dependent inhibition of FLT protein autophosphorylation (Study
#NR0006).
The effect of quizartinib on the proliferation of MV4-11 cells was also evaluated. In study
#NR0007, the RS4;11 cell line served as the negative control. The RS4;11 cell line expresses
wild-type FLT3 and is not dependent on FLT3 activity for proliferation. The leukemia cells were
49
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
incubated with quizartinib (0.169 nM to 3 µM) for 72 h (MV4-11 cells) or 48 h (RS4;11 cells).
Quizartinib inhibited FLT3-dependent cell proliferation in the MV4-11 cell line (IC50 = 0.3 nM),
while it exerted 1000-fold weaker activity against FLT3-independent proliferation of the RS4;11
cells (IC50 = 990 nM).
In Vivo Studies
Pharmacodynamic analysis: temporal effect of FLT3 inhibition
The inhibitory effect of quizartinib on the phosphorylation of FLT3 was assessed in vivo (Study
#NR0112). MV4-11 tumor bearing female C.B-17-SCID mice were treated with a single oral
(b)
dose of either vehicle ( (4)% 2-hydroxypropyl-β-cyclodextrin [HBCD]) or quizartinib (10 mg/kg, n =
4 mice per timepoint) and tumors were collected at 0.25, 1, 2, 6, 16, 24, 48, and 96 hours after
dosing. The tumors were harvested for FLT3 analysis, and plasma samples were collected for
pharmacokinetic (PK) analysis of quizartinib and AC886. Phosphorylated FLT3 (p-FLT3) and total
FLT3 protein were measured in tumor lysates with Meso Scale Discovery (MSD) analysis. A
normalized percent p-FLT3 to total FLT3 ratio was calculated using the values for the vehicle at
the 0.25-hour timepoint.
A time-dependent reduction of p-FLT3 was observed following a single dose of 10 mg/kg
quizartinib. The maximal effect was observed at 1-, 2-, and 6-hours post-dose with a percent pFLT3 value of 6%, 4%, and 7%, respectively. The p-FLT3 leveled off, i.e., ~60%, at 48-96 hours
post-dose for quizartinib treatment, while vehicle treated animals remained stable over time
and p-FLT3 ranged from 70%-126%. The results demonstrated that quizartinib treatment
induced a temporal effect on the inhibition of auto-phosphorylation of FLT3. Exposure levels of
both quizartinib and AC886 in the tumor and plasma samples over 48 h were comparable.
Anti-tumor activity in xenograft models
MV4-11 cells were implanted subcutaneously (SC) in mice to form localized tumors or injected
intravenously (IV) to produce disseminated disease. In the latter case (Study #NR0116), the
leukemia cells infiltrated the bone marrow. Quizartinib (0.1 to 10 mg/kg) was administered
orally in mice after disease was established. In the SC tumor model (Study #NR0009), the antitumor activity of quizartinib treatment was both dose-dependent and treatment durationdependent. In the disseminated graft model (Study #NR0116), quizartinib treatment prolonged
the lifespan of animals in a dose-dependent fashion. Treatment with 1 mg/kg quizartinib once
daily for 31 days produced an increased lifespan of 55%.

Report Title: A Cardiovascular Telemetry Study in the Unrestrained Conscious Non-Naïve Cynomolgus
Monkey
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:
• Dose-dependent ↑ QTc at ≥10 mg/kg which were observed 1 hr postdose and lasting to 24 hr
postdose; mean peak changes were 33 to 135 ms (13% - 53%) vs baseline
•

There were no changes in heart rate at any dose

•

Dose-dependent ↑ systemic BP at ≥100 mg/kg (1 - 8 hr postdose) primarily due to transient
elevations in systolic pressure.

In summary, quizartinib showed IKs current inhibition at clinically relevant physiologic
concentrations based on free drug concentration. However, quizartinib and the major
metabolite AC886 did not inhibit hERG, INa, INa-L, or ICa-L currents at clinically relevant
concentrations. In vivo, a single dose of quizartinib administered to monkeys induced a dosedependent increase in corrected QT interval (QTc) with sustained prolongation over 24 hours.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Overall, the FDA concurs with the Applicant’s cardiovascular safety pharmacology assessment
for quizartinib and the metabolite AC886.
The Applicant’s assessment contains typographical errors consisting mainly of mM to be
corrected to microM (µM) in the summary table of In Vitro Ion Channel Currents, Table 5 and
the summary of ex vivo assays.

The Applicant’s Position:
Type of Study

Major Findings

Absorption
Pharmacokinetics and Dose
Proportionality of Quizartinib

(Study NR0016)
Pharmacokinetics and Dose
Proportionality of Quizartinib
After a Single Dose in Dogs
(Study NR0014)

In male rats, quizartinib levels were approximately proportional
to dose. Quizartinib has good bioavailability (F) in male and
female rats (36% to 46% in male and 41% to 47% in female rats
over dose range of 3 to 30 mg/kg). In fed rats (male) the
bioavailability increased to approximately 100%, indicating that
the presence of food enhanced the absorption of quizartinib.
In male dogs, PK characteristics after oral dosing appeared to
be relatively dose proportional over the dose range of 1 to 10
mg/kg. Oral bioavailability was good at approximately 30% to
40%.

Clearance after IV administration was high (approximately
30 mL/min/kg; similar to hepatic blood flow) and bioavailability

The high concentrations of radioactivity observed in tissues at
plasma Tmax were found in the stomach, small intestine,
adrenal gland, liver, kidney medulla, pituitary gland, kidney
cortex, pancreas, spleen, thyroid gland, brown adipose,
mandibular salivary gland, stomach wall, myocardium, ovary,
large intestine, lung, thymus, white adipose, bone marrow,
lymph nodes, uterus wall, and prostate gland. The lowest
radioactivity in tissues at Tmax was in the central nervous
system and bone.
Quizartinib and AC886 exhibited greater than or equal to 99%
plasma protein binding in all of the species examined.

Metabolite profiling in plasma and feces from rats given
[14C]quizartinib dihydrochloride revealed that AC886 was the
major metabolite in rat plasma. No other circulating
metabolite was present at a fraction greater than 6.1% at any
time point (2, 4, or 24 h).
AC886 was the major circulating metabolite at later time
points, but another major metabolite at a fraction greater than
15%, identified as a morpholino oxidation product, was also
found. This metabolite was not detected as a major plasma
component in rats or humans, indicating species differences in
oxidation of the morpholino side chain. No other circulating
metabolite was present at a fraction greater than 8.2% at any
time point (2, 4, or 24 h).

TK data from general toxicology studies
4-week and 13-week repeat-dose toxicology studies in: rats (Study 70040 and 802411); dogs (Study
60024 and 802412); monkeys (Study 1008-2493 and 803726)
In rats, plasma exposure (AUC) to quizartinib increased greater than dose increment with oral doses
up to 60 mg/kg/day. After 3 months repeated dosing, exposures were greater than on day 1 by
approximately 3-fold. No apparent sex difference was observed.
In dogs, Cmax and AUC increased greater than dose increment at doses up to 150 mg/kg/day after 4
weeks, and almost with dose increment up to 15 mg/kg/day after 3 months. After 3 months
repeated dosing, there were decreases in Cmax at all doses up to 15 mg/kg/day and decreases in
AUC at 15 mg/kg/day only. Gender effects were not observed after 4 weeks of dosing, however, at
15 mg/kg/day over 3 months, Cmax and AUC were lower in males than females.
In monkeys, systemic exposure to quizartinib was generally with dose increment up to 3 months
dosing and at doses up to 60 mg/kg/day. Plasma exposures for quizartinib and the major
metabolite, AC886, following repeated quizartinib dosing were higher than Day 1 exposures.
Quizartinib and AC886 displayed no consistent gender effects in monkeys.
TK data from reproductive toxicology studies
Definitive oral (gavage) study for effects on embryo-fetal development in the rat (Study 901985)
Exposure to quizartinib was greater than dose increment, whereas exposure to AC886 increased
with dose increment. There was no relevant accumulation of either analyte after 12 days of
administration, except for a slight increase in exposure of quizartinib at 6 mg/kg/day. AUC0-t values of
AC886 relative to quizartinib were 42% to 83% on Gestation Day (GD) 6 and 32% to 75% on GD 17

Toxicology
General Toxicology
Single-dose and repeated-dose toxicity studies for up to 13 weeks of daily quizartinib
administration have been conducted in rats, dogs, and monkeys. At the early phase of
development, the dog was used to characterize the toxicity of quizartinib as a nonrodent
species. However, the dog showed a unique major circulating metabolite (morpholino oxidation
product) that has not been detected in human, rat, and monkey, despite achieving higher
exposure of those species than the dog to quizartinib, and may possibly contribute to a dogspecific liver toxicity due to birefringent crystal deposition. Based on pharmacokinetic (PK) and
toxicology data, the cynomolgus monkey was selected as a potential alternate nonrodent
model for evaluating the toxicity profile of quizartinib to predict human safety risk.
In all animal species studied, the principal target organs of toxicity were the bone marrow and
lymphoid organs. In addition, toxicological effects were observed in the kidney, liver, ovary,
vagina, and testis. Target-organ toxicity appeared to be dose and time dependent, and most
toxicological findings were reversible following a 28-day or 30-day recovery period; however,
slight decreases in red blood cell (RBC) and white blood cell (WBC) parameters, minimal bone
marrow hypocellularity, and liver crystal deposition (only in dogs) persisted through the post
recovery period. In 13-week studies, toxicological findings were present among rats and dogs
at 10 mg/kg/day and 15 mg/kg/day, respectively, and these findings included decreases in
hematology parameters, increased liver enzymes, and microscopic changes in bone marrow
and lymphoid organs. In a 13-week study in monkeys, mortality and adverse clinical signs were
reported at 10/6 mg/kg/day, with the findings consistent with those reported in the other
species. NOAELs in the 13-week repeated dose toxicity studies were 3 mg/kg/day in rats,
5 mg/kg/day in dogs, and 3 mg/kg/day in monkeys.

Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:
• All animals in the 60/30 mg/kg/day dose group were found dead or prematurely euthanized
from Study Days 3 to 15. Cause of death/moribundity was considered to be due to multi-organ
toxicity related to deteriorated condition. Gross and microscopic histopathologic changes

nodes, liver, adrenals, testes, epididymides, heart, pancreas, stomach, and small and large
intestines (data tables not shown).

toxicities noted in bone marrow, kidney and thymus.

Report Title: AC220: A 28-Day Oral Toxicity and Toxicokinetics Study in Beagle Dogs
Study Number: 60024 (eCTD 4.2.3.2)
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:
• A male given 150 mg/kg/day was prematurely euthanized on Day 8 in moribund condition. Due to severe adverse effects in surviving
animals administered 50 or 150 mg/kg/day, all dose levels were reduced from 10, 50 and 150 mg/kg/day to 5, 25 and 40 mg/kg/day,
respectively.

spleen and thymus.
Methods

Route and Method of administration: oral gavage
Number/Sex/Group: 3M, 3F
Dose: 10/5, 50/25, 150/40 mg/kg, once daily
(b)
Initial Age: 5-7 months
Vehicle/Formulation: (4)% HPβCD/Suspension

Toxicokinetics
Both Cmax and AUC0-t increased greater than dose proportional. No consistent gender effects were noted.

Report Title: A 90 Day Oral Gavage Toxicity Study of AC220 (With a 30 Day Recovery) in the Beagle Dog

Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:
• No treatment-related mortalities occurred
• Treatment-related changes were limited to the 15 mg/kg/day dose and included skin pallor, reductions in body weight, hematologic
changes in RBC and WBC parameters, increases in liver chemistry-related parameters

Methods

Number/Sex/Group: 4

(b)

Toxicokinetics
Cmax and AUC0-24 generally increased with dose increment. Between Days 1 and 90, there were decreases in Cmax and increases in
time to reach maximum plasma concentrations (Tmax). In the high-dose group, mean AUC0-24 was decreased between Days 1 and
90, and Cmax and AUC0-24 were lower for males than females.

28-day Repeat-dose Toxicity Study in Monkeys

Report Title: AC220: A 28-Day Oral Gavage Toxicity Study with a 28-day Recovery Period in the Cynomolgus Monkey
Study Number: 1008-2493 (eCTD 4.2.3.2)
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:
• Oral administration of quizartinib was generally well tolerated up to 30 mg/kg/day; several monkeys in the 100/60 mg/kg/day dose
group died prematurely due to moribundity
• Treatment-related adverse effects were observed at doses ≥30 mg/kg/day and included reductions in body weight, clinical signs and
changes in clinical pathology parameters
• Target organs included bone marrow, lymphoid tissue, kidney, and GI tract
Methods
Species/Strain: Cynomolgus Monkey
Route and Method of administration:
Number/Sex/Group: 3
Dose: 10, 30, 100/60 mg/kg, once daily
(b)
Initial Age: 2-3 years
Vehicle/Formulation: (4)% HPβCD/Suspension
Satellite groups: recovery: 2M, 2F

Toxicokinetics
Systemic exposure to quizartinib increased less than the dose increment on Day 1 and generally with dose increment at steady-state
(Day 28). There was no apparent sex difference in the systemic exposure to either quizartinib or AC886. After multiple dosing, there
was a potential for plasma accumulation of quizartinib and AC886.

13-week GLP Toxicology Study in Cynomolgus Monkeys

Report Title: A 13-Week Oral Gavage Toxicity Study of AC220 in the Cynomolgus Monkey With a 4 Week Recovery Period
Study Number: 803726 (eCTD 4.2.3.2)
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:
• All monkeys in the 30 mg/kg/day group were prematurely euthanized due to moribundity; doses for mid and high doses were reduced

Toxicokinetics
Cmax and AUC0-24 of quizartinib and AC886 increased as a function of quizartinib dose in both sexes. The plasma exposures for both
analytes following repeated quizartinib dosing were higher than Day 1 exposures. Quizartinib and AC886 displayed no consistent
gender effects in monkeys.

Observations and Results: Changes From Control
Parameters
Mortality

Major findings
5 animals found dead or prematurely euthanized between Days 6 and 36, including 2F/1M at 30/12 mg/kg/day and 2F
at 10/6 mg/kg/day. Two deaths were due to anemia, one was likely secondary to marked bone marrow hypocellularity.
A cause of death could not be determined for the others.
Clinical Signs
≥6 mg/kg/day: reduced appetite, emesis, prominent backbone, decreased activity
≥10 mg/kg/day: severe dehydration, hunched posture, decreased respiratory rate, labored breathing, partly closed eyes,
severe dehydration, soft/liquid feces, severe emesis
≥12 mg/kg/day: moderate to severe lack of coordination, severe dehydration; dosing discontinued on Study Days 61 (M)
and 60 (F) and all animals prematurely euthanized
≥30 mg/kg/day: dosing suspended for 10 days due to moribundity/premature death in 1M/2F and severity of clinical
signs in all animals. Dosing resumed at a reduced dose of 12 mg/kg/day until dosing discontinued as noted above.
Body Weights
≥6 mg/kg/day: decreased body weight
Ophthalmoscopy
No treatment-related changes
Electrocardiography No treatment-related effects
Hematology
≥3 mg/kg/day: decreases in RBC, hemoglobin, hematocrit
10/6 mg/kg/day: decreases in reticulocytes; increases in platelets, MCV, RDW
30/12 mg/kg/day: increases in neutrophils; decreases in lymphocytes and eosinophils
Clinical Chemistry
3 mg/kg/day: decreases in total protein and globulin (M only)
10/6 mg/kg/day: increases in AST, ALT, BUN (M only); decreases in total protein, albumin, globulin and phosphorus
30/12 mg/kg/day: increases in AST, ALT, TBL (M only), BUN, creatinine, glucose (M only), triglycerides; decreases in total
protein, albumin, globulin, phosphorus, sodium, potassium and chloride (M only)
Urinalysis
No treatment-related changes
Gross Pathology
10/6 mg/kg/day: small thymus (F only)
30/12 mg/kg/day: small thymus (M only); enlarged adrenals; small spleen in 1 female
Organ Weights
≥3 mg/kg/day: decreased uterus, ovaries, spleen
≥10/6 mg/kg/day: increased liver, adrenals; decreased thymus (F only)
30/12 mg/kg/day: decreased thymus

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the Applicant. See additional information below from the 13-week studies in rats and monkeys related to
findings in the reproductive organs to assist with information presented under section 13.1 of the product label.
A 90 Day Oral Gavage Toxicity Study of AC220 (with a 30 Day Recovery) in the Albino Rat (#802411; GLP)
•
•
•
•

Doses were 1 (Low dose. LD), 3 (mid dose, MD), and 10 mg/kg (high dose, HD).
Adverse reproductive effects in females included ovarian cyst, abnormal vaginal epithelia and mucification. Those in males
included small testis and epididymis, oligo/aspermia, and failure of sperm release. Findings were reversible.
The findings in females started at the mid dose (AUC of 12,500 ng•h/mL) and the findings in males occurred at the high dose
(10 mg/kg; AUC of 65,400 ng•h/mL).
Based on the human AUC of quizartinib (10,200 ng•h/mL at the MRHD), the adverse reproductive effects in female rats
occurred at an exposure comparable to human exposure. That in male rats occurred at ~6-fold the human exposure.

A 13 Week Oral Gavage Toxicity Study of AC220 in the Cynomolgus Monkey with a 4 Week Recovery Period (#803726; GLP)
•
•
•
•

Doses were 3 (LD), 10/6 (MD), and 30/12 mg/kg (HD).

0.2 (females)- to 0.4 (males)- fold human exposures.

{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Genetic Toxicology
The Applicant’s Position:
Quizartinib demonstrated potential for mutagenicity in a bacterial reverse mutation assay (Ames test), but not in a mammalian cell
mutation assay (mouse lymphoma thymidine kinase) or transgenic rodent gene mutation assay with Big Blue® rats. Quizartinib was
negative in a chromosome aberration assay and in a single-dose rat bone marrow micronucleus assay although a rat micronucleus
assay conducted in conjunction with the 28-day general toxicology study had an equivocal result.
Genotoxicity Assessments
Report Title: AC010220 Bacterial Reverse Mutation Assay
Study Number: 961163 (eCTD 4.2.3.3.1)
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
• Following exposure to quizartinib, increases in revertant colony counts with TA98 in the absence and presence of S9 mix, and TA100 in the
presence of S9 mix.
• Exposure to quizartinib in the presence of S9 mix caused partial absence of the background lawn of non-revertant bacteria indicating that
quizartinib was toxic to the bacteria at high-dose concentrations.
Test system: S. typhimurium (TA98, TA100, TA1535, TA1537) and E. coli (WP2uvrA)
Test validity: Mean revertant colony counts for the vehicle controls were close to or within the laboratory historical control range. Positive
controls induced increases in revertant colony counts.
Report Title: AC010220 Mammalian Cell Mutation Assay
Study Number: 962099 (eCTD 4.2.3.3.1)
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
• No substantial increases in mutant frequency were noted for quizartinib in the absence or presence of S9 mix at dose levels up to the limit
of toxicity.
• Quizartinib did not show any evidence of genotoxicity in this assay.
Test system: Mouse lymphoma L5178Y TK+/- (clone 3.7.2) cells
Test validity: Plating efficiencies for vehicle control cultures were within acceptable limits (65-120% for Day 2/viability). Positive controls
caused increases in the small colony mutant frequency in each treatment regime.

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Genotoxicity Assessments
Report Title: AC010220 Chromosome Aberration Assay
Study Number: 961164 (eCTD 4.2.3.3.1)
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
• Cultures treated with quizartinib did not show any significant increases in the incidence of cells with aberrant metaphases at levels up to
the limit of toxicity
• A substantial reduction in the absolute number of analyzable metaphases was observed at the high dose for the 4-hour treatments.
Test system: Human peripheral blood lymphocytes
Test validity: Positive controls caused significant increases in the proportion of cells with chromosome damage.
Report Title: AC010220 Bone Marrow Micronucleus Assay in Rats Following a Single Dose of Quizartinib
Study Number: 961901 (eCTD 4.2.3.3.2)
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
• At quizartinib doses up to 100 mg/kg, no significant increases in the incidence of micronucleated immature erythrocytes were noted.
• Quizartinib showed a marginal but statistically significant decrease in the proportion of immature erythrocytes at the 48-hour sampling
time for animals in the highest dose group.
Test system:
Rat (Hsd:SD): (Males - 7 weeks; Females - 8 weeks); Single oral dose administration at 15, 50 and 100 mg/kg; positive control
(cyclophosphamide) at 20 mg/kg; cells evaluated from bone marrow smears included micronucleated immature and mature erythrocytes, and
proportion of immature erythrocytes in the total population (immature and mature erythrocytes).
Test validity:
Data for concurrent vehicle control were within ranges determined from laboratory historical data. Positive control induced clear,
unequivocal increases in micronuclei.
Report Title: AC010220: Micronucleus Test Component of the Rat 28-Day Oral Toxicity Study
Study Number: 961165 (eCTD 4.2.3.3.2)
GLP Compliance: Yes

86
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the
positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Genotoxicity Assessments
Key Study Findings:
At 15 mg/kg/day only, significant increases in the incidence of micronucleated immature erythrocytes were observed, although none of the
individual values or group means for quizartinib fell outside the historical control range. As a result, it cannot be concluded whether the small
apparent increase was treatment-related or due to variation between animals/groups. Therefore, the results of the study are considered
equivocal.
Test system:
Rat (Crl:CD [SD]): (Males/Females; 9-10 weeks); oral daily dose administration at 5 and 15 mg/kg/day for 28 days; bone marrow samples were
taken for bone marrow smears and 2000 immature erythrocytes per animal were examined for presence of micronuclei. The proportion of
immature erythrocytes was also assessed for potential bone marrow toxicity. Positive control slides from 3 male and 3 female rats dosed
orally with cyclophosphamide monohydrate at 20 mg/kg in a separate group were included.
Test validity:
Positive control slides showed significant increases in the incidence of micronucleated immature erythrocytes.
Report Title: Transgenic Rat Gene Mutation Assay with Quizartinib
Study Number: AF84WP.171.BTL (eCTD 4.2.3.3.2)
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
• All animals survived in the main study
• Quizartinib did not cause elevated mutant frequencies at the cII gene in the liver, bone marrow or glandular stomach of treated animals
and therefore was considered negative for induction of mutagenicity
Test system:
Rat (F344 homozygous Big Blue® transgenic): based on 7-day RF study in non-transgenic F344 rats at 15, 30 and 45 mg/kg/day which resulted
in premature death at doses ≥30 mg/kg/day, 6 males/group (8-12 weeks) were administered quizartinib at 3.8, 7.5 and 15 mg/kg/day for 28
days daily dosing; positive control (N-ethyl-N-nitrosourea [ENU]) at 20 mg/kg/day was given for 6 non-consecutive days; bone marrow, liver
and glandular stomach tissues were collected at necropsy and evaluated for mutation analysis via DNA isolation from the first 5 animals/group
and analysis of cII mutants.
Test validity:
Vehicle control did not cause statistically elevated mutant frequency at the cII gene in the liver, bone marrow, or glandular stomach, and
positive control induced a statistically significant increase in mutant frequency for all tissues tested.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s conclusions on the genotoxicity assessment of quizartinib.
Quizartinib was positive in the Ames test and negative in both in vitro (mouse lymphoma thymidine kinase) and in vivo (transgenic
rat model) mammalian cell mutation tests. FDA also agrees that quizartinib was not clastogenic and did not cause polyploidy in a
chromosomal aberration assay (human peripheral blood lymphocytes). While quizartinib was negative in bone marrow
micronucleus assays in a single dose study in rats, the in vivo micronucleus assay conducted in conjunction with the 28-day general
toxicology study in rats was considered equivocal (positive results compared to concurrent control but within the historical control
range).
Carcinogenicity
The Applicant’s Position:
In accordance with International Conference on Harmonisation (ICH) S9, no studies have been conducted.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA concurs with the Applicant’s statement.
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
Embryo-fetal toxicity studies in rats showed no maternal toxicity and no evidence of quizartinib-related embryolethality up to
6 mg/kg/day. Fetotoxicity and teratogenicity were evident at 6 mg/kg/day, primarily by lower fetal weights, with effects on skeletal
ossification, and fetal abnormalities (anasarca). The no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) for embryo-fetal development was
2 mg/kg/day.

Report Title: An Oral Range-finding Teratology Study of AC220 in the Rat
Study Number: 901971 (eCTD 4.2.3.5.2)
GLP Compliance: No
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:
Dams:
• Treatment-related maternal toxicity included clinical signs (dehydration, skin pallor, thinness, swollen cranium, increased incidence of
fur staining, and liquid vaginal discharge) at 20 mg/kg/day. One female at 10 mg/kg/day had brown liquid vaginal discharge and
delivered dead fetal material on GD21.
• Slightly lower body weight, body-weight gain and food consumption in dams at 10 mg/kg/day as well as reduced litter size and fetal
body weights.
• At 20 mg/kg/day embryo-fetal lethality as evidenced by total resorption noted in all animals and at 10 mg/kg/day in 1 of 5 animals.
• At 10 mg/kg/day, a high number of late resorptions resulting in higher post-implantation loss occurred. Lower gravid uterus weight
was observed due to smaller litters and lower fetal weights.
Fetus:
• At 10 mg/kg/day, lower fetal weight were noted. In all live litters from this group, fetuses had anasarca. Some showed generalized
skin pallor an micrognathia. One live fetus had microcaudia.

Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:

Other Toxicology Studies
The Applicant’s Position:
Quizartinib was not considered to be a contact sensitizer and was not phototoxic. An impurities study to assess potential toxicity of
five impurities of quizartinib showed no adverse effects in a 28-day rat study.
Antigenicity
Report Title: A Sensitization Study of AC220 by Dermal Administration in Guinea Pigs-Modified Buehler Design
Study Number: 20079155 (eCTD 4.2.3.7.1)
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
• No dermal reactions to 100% quizartinib in the test and challenge control animals occurred. Dermal scores were 0.
• Following challenge with 2.5% w/v HCA, dermal scores of 1 or 2 were noted in 5/10 HCA test animals at the 24- and 48-hour scoring
intervals. Dermal reactions in the remaining HCA test and HCA control animals were limited to scores of 0 or ±. Group mean dermal
scores were higher in the HCA test animals compared to the HCA challenge control animals.
• Following challenge with 1.0% w/v HCA, dermal scores of 1 were noted in 2/10 HCA test animals at the 24- and 48-hour scoring
intervals. Dermal reactions in the HCA challenge control animals were limited to scores of 0. Group mean dermal scores were higher in
the HCA test animals compared to the HCA challenge control animals.
• Based on these results, quizartinib is not considered to be a contact sensitizer in guinea pigs.
Species/Strain: Hartley-derived albino guinea pig
Route of administration: dermal
Number/Sex/Group: 10
Method of administration: topical
Vehicle/Formulation: Suspension - Reverse Osmosis Deionized (RODI)
Initial Age: 6-7 Weeks
water
Positive control: α-Hexylcinnamaldehyde
Method: Guinea pigs were topically treated with 100% quizartinib, 0.3 g/site, once per week, for 3 consecutive weeks. Following a 2-week
rest period, a challenge was performed; 20 test and 10 previously untreated (naïve) challenge control guinea pigs were topically
treated with 100% quizartinib. Challenge responses in the test animals were compared with those of the challenge control animals.
An α-Hexylcinnamaldehyde (HCA) positive control group consisting of 10 HCA test and 10 HCA control guinea pigs was included. The
animals were treated as above with the HCA test animals receiving 5% w/v HCA in ethanol for induction and 2.5% and 1.0% w/v HCA
in acetone for challenge.

Impurity Qualification
Report Title: A 28-day oral gavage toxicity Study of AC220 Impurities in Rats
Study Number: 1258-023 (eCTD 4.2.3.7.6)
GLP Compliance: Yes
Key Study Findings:
• Hematologic changes including reduced red cell parameters (RBC, hemoglobin and/or hematocrit) and reticulocytes, neutrophils and
eosinophils were observed in all quizartinib-treatment groups, and correlated with decreased bone marrow cellularity. These changes
were considered related to quizartinib and not the added impurities.
(b)
• Hepatocellular effects including increased AST and ALT were noted at 5 mg/kg/day of quizartinib with (4)% of impurities. These increases
were likely related to the impurities as similar increases were not observed in animals receiving quizartinib alone; however, these effects
were not considered adverse due to the small magnitude of change and lack of microscopic correlates.
• Statistically significant organ weight changes of the testes, spleen, and thymus were observed in males and/or females. Although these
changes were seen only in groups given quizartinib with impurities, they were not considered adverse due to the small magnitude of
change and lack of microscopic correlates.
(b)
(b)
• In summary, no adverse effects were noted for any parameter examined for quizartinib impurities concentrations of (4)% or (4)% after oral
gavage administration for 28 days.
Methods
Species/Strain: Rat/Crl:CD(SD)
Route of administration: oral
Number/Sex/Group: 10
Method of administration: gavage
(b)
Initial Age: 6 Weeks
Vehicle/Formulation: (4)% HPβCD/Suspension
(b) (4)
Impurities included:
(b)
(b)
(b) (4)
(b) (4)
Impurity levels:
mg/kg/day,
mg/kg/day representing (4)% and (4)% of 5 mg/kg/day quizartinib dose, respectively.
Justification: In a 28-day rat toxicology study, quizartinib was administered at 5 and 15 mg/kg/day by oral gavage. The primary treatmentrelated findings included a dose-dependent decrease in RBC, hemoglobin, hematocrit, neutrophil, monocyte and eosinophil
counts. Minimal bone marrow hypocellularity was observed at 5 mg/kg/day. Slight elevations in AST, ALT and ALP levels were
noted only at 15 mg/kg/day. Thus, a level of 5 mg/kg/day represents a low dose where minimal drug-related changes were
observed and measurable. The goal of this study was to evaluate the potential additional toxicity of two concentrations of
impurities spiked into the parent compound quizartinib.

Ocular Irritation Toxicity
Report Title: An Acute Irritation Study of AC220 by Ocular Administration in Rabbits
Study Number: 20079153 (eCTD 4.2.3.7.7)
GLP Compliance: Yes
Test system: Rabbit/NZW; 3 females administered a single ocular dose of 0.0354 g/eye with examination for irritation at 1, 24, 48, and 72
hours post administration
Key Study Findings:
• Exposure to quizartinib produced conjunctivitis in 3/3 test eyes at the 1-h scoring interval. Complete resolution of conjunctivitis occurred in
2/3 test eyes by the 48-h scoring interval and in the remaining test eye by the 72-h scoring interval. An additional ocular finding of
quizartinib remaining in the eye was noted in all test animals.
• Quizartinib is considered to be a mild irritant based on Kay and Calandra Evaluation Criteria
• Classified as a nonirritant based on EEC Ocular Evaluation Criteria
• Classified as equivocal based on FDA Evaluation Criteria due to a single animal with conjunctival redness of 2 at the 24- and 48-h scoring
intervals

6 Clinical Pharmacology

Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
The proposed indication is for use of quizartinib (VANFLYTA®) in combination with standard
cytarabine and anthracycline induction and standard cytarabine consolidation chemotherapy,
and as continuation monotherapy following consolidation, for the treatment of adult patients
with newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML) that is FMS-like tyrosine kinase 3 internal
tandem duplication (FLT3-ITD) positive as detected by an FDA-approved test.
The marketed formulation of quizartinib is as the salt in tablets; however, doses in the label are
described as free base content. Quizartinib is available as 20 mg and 30 mg (salt form) tablets.
In the assessment aid, doses are generally described as free base; however, there are instances
where the applicant has described the dose in the salt form. Table 9 provides the translation
between the dose descriptions. Quizartinib dosing regimen is proposed as described in Table
10.

The evidence of efficacy for the proposed indication comes from the pivotal study AC220-AU302: A Phase 3, Randomized (1:1), Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled Study: Quizartinib vs
Placebo with Standard Chemotherapy in FLT3-ITD(+) ND AML (QuANTUM-First) demonstrating
median (95% CI) OS was 31.9 (21.0, not estimable (NE)] months) in the quizartinib arm
compared to 15.1 (13.2, 26.2) months in the standard chemotherapy arm.
In the safety cohort of 265 patients in the quizartinib arm, quizartinib at the proposed dosage
resulted in a Grade ≥3 TEAE rate of 47.5%, and a serious TEAE rate of 54.0%. Dose reduction
due to an AE occurred in 18.9% of patients. Dose interruption occurred in 34.0% of patients
and dose discontinuation due to AE occurred in 20.4% of patients.
Quizartinib prolongs the QT interval in a concentration-related manner. In study AC220-AU302, 34.3% of patients on the quizartinib arm had new occurrence of a QTcF >450 msec,
compared to 17.9% in the placebo arm. In 7.5% of these patients, the QTcF >480 msec, and in
2.3% of patients it >500 msec. A >30 msec increase from baseline was observed in 55.1% of
patients. The increase from baseline was >60 msec in 10.2% of patients.
The clinical pharmacology review focused on the evaluation of the proposed dosage, drug-drug
interactions, QT interval prolongation, the impact of organ impairment, and the impact of
FLT3-ITD VAF and NPM1 mutation status. Population PK (PPK), exposure-response (E-R), and
physiologically-based PK (PBPK) modeling analyses were also conducted and reviewed.
