Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Data:
Data are included in other sections of this document.
The Applicant’s Position:
A comprehensive clinical pharmacology program characterizing the PK of quizartinib, intrinsic
and extrinsic factors on quizartinib PK, drug-drug interactions (DDIs), and exposure-response
(E-R) relationships was conducted in both AML subjects and healthy subjects and provides
adequate understanding of quizartinib disposition and support for the intended dosing regimen
of quizartinib for the indication of treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed
FLT3-ITD (+) AML.
The commercial tablet formulation of quizartinib provides adequate exposure levels for once
daily (QD) dosing with linear PK over the intended doses. Food intake does not significantly
impact the clinical PK, which is characterized by extensive metabolism and formation of the
major circulating active metabolite AC886, formed via cytochrome P450 (CYP) 3A mediated
oxidative metabolism. Population PK analysis showed that quizartinib PK varied during the
different treatment phases in newly diagnosed AML subjects which was captured by including a
phase effect on the relative bioavailability in the final model. The biological reasons for this are
unknown. Quizartinib and AC886 are not inhibitors or inducers for CYP3A therefore
autoinhibition or autoinduction is not anticipated.
Clinically relevant E-R relationships for efficacy and safety as well as the impact of intrinsic and
extrinsic factors have been characterized. They indicate a need for cardiac monitoring based on
a dose-dependent increase in QT intervals, the need to avoid coadministration of moderate or
strong inducers of CYP3A, and requiring a dose reduction in the case of coadministration of
strong CYP3A inhibitors. Quizartinib and AC886 are not anticipated to affect the PK of other
drugs through CYP enzymes. Quizartinib is a weak inhibitor of intestinal P-gp and predicted to
have no to weak effect on UGT1A1 substrates. Quizartinib and AC886 have minimal potential
to affect other transporters at therapeutic concentrations. Mild and moderate renal or hepatic
impairment have no clinically meaningful impact on the PK of quizartinib and AC886, except
that no data is available for individuals with severe renal or hepatic impairment and use in
these populations is not recommended. Therapeutic individualization is not indicated based on
107
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
age, sex, or race.
The dosing regimen used in Study AC220-A-U302 of subjects with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+)
AML is appropriate and adequately supported by the observed improvement in OS, the
observed safety profile, and the data from a comprehensive clinical pharmacology program.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that quizartinib (free base) showed linear PK in the
dose range of 26.5 mg to 79.5 mg in healthy subjects and 17.7 mg to 53 mg in patients with
AML. The highest dose proposed for use in patients with AML is 53 mg and falls within the
linear range.
PPK analysis determined that the median quizartinib exposure (AUCss, Cmax,ss, Cmin,ss) was 71 to
73% higher in healthy subjects compared to subjects with AML following the same dosage.
In study AC220-A-U302, the PK of quizartinib in patients with newly diagnosed AML differed
across the three treatment phases (induction, consolidation, and maintenance). Quizartinib
clearance was slightly lower during consolidation treatment compared to induction treatment.
Following the proposed dosage of 35.4 mg once daily (QD), median quizartinib steady state
exposures (AUCss and Cmax,ss) were 40% higher during consolidation compared to induction
(AUCss = 4290 versus 3086 ng∙hr/mL; Cmax,ss = 210 versus 151 ng/mL).
Quizartinib exposure during maintenance treatment is expected to be significantly higher than
exposure during induction or consolidation treatment due to two factors: 1) proposed
maintenance dosage of 53 mg QD which is higher than the 35.4 mg QD dosage for induction
and consolidation, and 2) significantly lower quizartinib clearance during maintenance
treatment compared to induction or consolidation treatment. Following the proposed dosage
in study AC220-A-U302, quizartinib median steady state exposures (AUCss and Cmax,ss) were
266% higher during maintenance treatment compared to induction treatment
(AUCss = 11286 versus 3086 ng∙hr/mL; Cmax,ss = 553 versus 151 ng/mL). This “phase effect” on
quizartinib PK was not observed in other studies, which may be due to the design of other
studies, use of quizartinib monotherapy versus combination therapy, differences in disease
status over time and across studies, lower patient numbers, and limited PK samples. See
Section 19.4.1.2 for additional details.
See Section 6.3.1 for FDA’s assessment of general pharmacology and clinical PK.
See Section 6.2.2 for FDA’s assessment of the proposed dosing regimen, E-R relationships of
quizartinib, and therapeutic individualization.

General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
6.2.2.1.

General Dosing

Data:
A 40-mg dose during the Induction and Consolidation Phases was selected based on the results
of the Phase 1 Study 2689-CL-0005 (Altman, 2018) and Study AML18 (Burnett, 2013) in subjects
with newly diagnosed AML in combination with standard chemotherapy. Both studies
identified 40 mg QD for 14 days as a safe recommended dose with the potential for clinical
efficacy when given in conjunction with standard chemotherapy. The 40 mg dose was further
supported by data from an ex vivo plasma inhibitory activity (PIA) assay utilizing FLT3-ITD (+)
cell lines with data from Study 2689-CL-2004 in R/R AML indicating that 30 mg is the minimum
dose for complete and rapid inhibition of FLT3 signaling in this assay. The potential impact of
stromal signaling via FLT (the cognate FLT3-ligand) and resulting protection of leukemic blast
cells from apoptosis (Sato, 2011; Yang, 2014) likely requires higher exposure levels, especially in
the bone marrow compartment. Considering available safety data from Studies 2689-CL-0005,
AML18, and AC220-007 (QuANTUM-R), the dosing regimen utilized in Study AC220-A-U302 thus
represents an appropriate trade-off between safety and efficacy relevant considerations.
A 2-week escalating dose schedule was implemented as a risk minimization measure to monitor
dose-dependent QT prolongation and was based on QT interval corrected with Fridericia’s
formula (QTcF) interval data from the Phase 2 Study 2689-CL-2004. The target dose of 60 mg
QD was supported by Study 2689-CL-0011 of quizartinib monotherapy as maintenance in
FLT3-ITD (+) AML, which showed that both 40 mg QD and 60 mg QD were well tolerated, with
similar incidence of Grade 3/4 adverse events (AEs) and with rapid and durable inhibition of
FLT3 at the 60 mg QD dose in the PIA.
Safety and efficacy data from Study AC220-A-U302 demonstrate a significant survival benefit
for the dosing regimen utilized with an adequate and manageable safety profile and a positive
benefit-risk assessment when considering the overall poor outcomes in the intended patient
population. A statistically significant effect of quizartinib exposure on OS was found in subjects
with newly diagnosed AML, where higher exposure was associated with longer OS, supporting
the overall clinical benefit of dose escalation to 60 mg. The estimated slope was relatively
uncertain (relative standard error [RSE] 37%) and led to a modest E-R relationship. Quizartinib
prolonged QTcF in a concentration-dependent manner. No E-R relationship was identified for
the probability of the first occurrence of the investigated AEs; however, a quizartinib treatment
effect was found for treatment-emergent AEs (TEAEs) leading to dose reduction/interruption,
TEAEs leading to study drug discontinuation, events in the Torsade de Pointes (TdP)/QT
prolongation standardized MedDRA query (SMQ), and myelosuppression.
The Applicant’s Position:
109
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Quizartinib should be administered in combination with standard chemotherapy at a dose of
40 mg QD for 2 weeks in each cycle of induction and consolidation, starting from Day 8 of
induction or Day 6 of consolidation chemotherapy. During the Continuation Phase, quizartinib
should be initiated at a starting dose of 30 mg (26.5 mg free base) QD and then increased after
2 weeks of treatment to the recommended dose of 60 mg (53 mg free base) QD if corrected
QTcF was ≤450 ms. Treatment with quizartinib can be continued for up to 36 cycles (28-day
cycles), as long as the patient derives clinical benefit.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s proposed dosing regimen of 35.4 mg (free base) once daily for
the induction and the consolidation phases. Of note, the 40 mg (salt form) once daily dose
described in the Applicant’s text above is equal to the 35.4 mg dose (free base).
FDA does not consider the Applicant’s proposed dosing regimen for the maintenance phase to
be sufficiently justified. See Section 6.2.2.3 for the PMR and Section 6.3.1 for FDA’s assessment
of the proposed dosing regimen for the continuation/maintenance phase. E-R analysis in
patients who received at least one maintenance dose suggests that higher quizartinib exposure
during maintenance does not result in improved survival. Given the quizartinib concentrationdependent QTcF prolongation and other safety events associated with quizartinib treatment,
the proposed 53 mg once daily maintenance dose of quizartinib may expose patients to
unnecessary toxicity without an improvement in survival compared to lower maintenance
doses.

6.2.2.2.

Therapeutic Individualization

Data:
Data are included in other sections of this document.
The Applicant’s Position:
Clinically significant DDI were seen with strong CYP3A inhibitors and moderate CYP3A inducer
efavirenz. Dose reduction is required in the case of strong CYP3A inhibitors, and moderate or
strong inducers of CYP3A should be avoided. Quizartinib and AC886 have minimal or no
potential for clinically relevant transporter-mediated DDI and minimal potential to impact the
PK of other drugs given concomitantly. Additionally, quizartinib dose should be adjusted based
on QTcF with electrocardiogram (ECG) monitoring.
Intrinsic factors including age, sex, and Black race, were identified in the population PK analysis
as having statistically significant effects on the PK of quizartinib and AC886. However, these
110

{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
effects were not considered clinically meaningful. Therefore, no dose adjustment is required in
any of these subpopulations. Mild or moderate renal or hepatic impairment have no clinically
meaningful impact on the PK of quizartinib or AC886, and dose adjustments are not warranted.
Since there is no data on subjects with severe renal or hepatic impairment, the use of
quizartinib in these populations is not recommended.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s positions that dose reduction is required in the presence of
strong CYP3A inhibitors and that concomitant use of strong or moderate CYP3A inducers should
be avoided. See Section 6.3.2.4 for specific recommendations for dosage adjustment for
concomitant use of strong CYP3A inhibitors.
FDA generally agrees that no dosage adjustments are necessary according to age, sex, body
weight, race (in Black or African American, or, White or Asian) patients, mild or moderate
hepatic impairment, mild or moderate renal impairment, or coadministration of weak or
moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors.
FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s proposed dose modification criteria in the
continuation phase. The proposed product label currently reads:

(b) (4)

If a QTcF >500 ms is observed during
induction or consolidation, the dose of quizartinib should not be escalated during the
continuation phase.
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the impact of FLT3-ITD VAF and NPM1
mutations on OS and response rate. Baseline demographics and disease features including
FLT3-ITD VAF and NPM1 mutation status are described in Table 24, while Figure 3 shows the
impact on overall survival (OS) (Section 8.1.2). The OS hazard ratios favoring quizartinib were
observed in all VAF subgroups (highest in the subgroup with >50% VAF), consistent with the
mechanism of action of quizartinib, and in the subgroup of patients with mutations in NPM1.
This is consistent with the mechanism of action of quizartinib and with the published literature
on the role of wildtype NPM1 as a negative prognostic factor. EFS and CR/CRc analyses
conducted by the Applicant showed that results seen in the mutant NPM1 and wildtype NPM1
subgroups are consistent with the overall study population (Table 48 and Table 49). The
Applicant’s exploratory subgroup analyses suggest a benefit on EFS and no suggestion of harm
for other endpoints which, taken together with the high unmet medical need in NPM1 wildtype
patients, supports approval in both subgroups. EFS hazard ratios of 0.760 in the wildtype NPM1

6.2.2.3.

Outstanding Issues

The Applicant’s Position:
None.
The FDA’s Assessment:
There is one outstanding question regarding whether the proposed dosing regimen for the
maintenance phase is appropriate:
The randomized, controlled trial, QuANTUM-First, used planned maintenance dosage of
26.5 mg quizartinib once daily (QD) for two weeks followed by dose escalation to 53 mg QD if
QTcF remained less than or equal to 450 ms. Maintenance treatment was to continue for up to
36 months. Subjects were randomized to quizartinib or placebo treatment before starting
induction treatment but were not re-randomized prior to starting maintenance treatment. As a
result, it is uncertain whether safety and efficacy outcomes, including overall survival, were
impacted by maintenance quizartinib dose because the effect(s) of quizartinib dose and
exposure during maintenance cannot be evaluated independently of the effects of quizartinib
dose and exposure during induction and consolidation.
Additionally, the quizartinib dose and exposure during maintenance were directly impacted by
QTcF interval due to the QTcF-dependent dose escalation. Factors associated with QTcF,
including age and other patient and disease characteristics, may also confound the analysis of
maintenance efficacy and safety according to quizartinib maintenance dose and exposure.
The E-R analyses did not identify any clear associations between OS or EFS and higher average
quizartinib exposure from first maintenance dose up to time of event/censoring (see detailed
analysis in Section 19.4.3.2). However, higher quizartinib exposure is associated with greater
QTcF prolongation and may put patients at risk of a worse safety profile. The recommended
53 mg QD quizartinib maintenance dosage is expected to result in over 2-fold higher quizartinib
exposure (AUC, Cmax, Ctrough) compared to the recommended 35.4 mg QD induction and
consolidation dosage (see detailed analysis in Section 19.4.1.2). Therefore, the recommended
quizartinib maintenance dosage may result in a worse safety profile without improvements to
overall survival compared to lower maintenance dosages.
Based on the available information, the risk/benefit profile of the inclusion of quizartinib
maintenance therapy, and the appropriate dose for maintenance therapy, are unclear.
Additional information is necessary to determine a clinically beneficial maintenance dosage that
112
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
does not expose subjects to unnecessary toxicity or safety events and is tolerable. The
additional data would address the known serious risks of QTcF interval prolongation,
neutropenia, and other signals of serious risk related to the use of quizartinib in the
maintenance setting.
FDA will issue the following PMR:
Conduct a randomized trial of quizartinib maintenance therapy that compares the
recommended dosage of 53 mg daily to a lower dosage (e.g., 26.5 mg daily) to further
characterize serious adverse reactions including but not limited to the rates of Grade ≥3
adverse reactions, Grade ≥3 neutropenia, QTcF interval prolongation, and dose reductions,
interruptions, and discontinuations due to adverse reactions and provide an analysis of
dose- and exposure-response relationships for safety. Incorporate systematically assessed
patient-reported outcome assessments to further characterize safety and tolerability, including
patient-reported symptoms, function, and overall side effect impact. Eligible patients will
include newly-diagnosed, FLT3-ITD positive AML patients in complete remission following
consolidation chemotherapy. The study should also provide an analysis of dose- and exposureresponse relationships for efficacy, including overall survival and relapse-free survival.

Strong CYP3A inhibitor ketoconazole increased the Cmax of singledose quizartinib by 17%, AUCinf by 94%, and the predicted steadystate quizartinib AUCtau and Cmax by 96% and 86%, respectively.
Moderate CYP3A inducer efavirenz decreased quizartinib AUCinf
and Cmax by 90% and 45%, respectively, and AC886 AUCinf and
Cmax by 96% and 68%, respectively. In the PPK analysis, weak
CYP3A inducers were not found to be a statistically significant
covariate for quizartinib and AC886 PK parameters.

Excretion

Excretion Pathways

The principal route of excretion for total radioactivity from an oral
dose was in the feces. The total radioactivity recovered in feces was
76.3% (4% unchanged quizartinib) and in urine was 1.64% through
the 336-h collection period. The fecal total recovery up to 748 h
was projected to be approximately 89%.

• Quizartinib and AC886 have minimal potential to affect the PK of
other drugs through CYP enzyme pathways
• Quizartinib is a weak P-gp inhibitor; quizartinib increased
dabigatran AUC and Cmax by approximately 12%
• Quizartinib and AC886 have minimal potential to affect other
transporters at therapeutic concentrations
• Quizartinib is predicted by a PBPK model to have minimal effect
on UGT1A1 substrates
• In vitro, quizartinib is a substrate for P-gp but not for BCRP,
OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OCT1, OAT2, MATE1, or MRP2

The Applicant’s Position:
The pharmacology and PK of quizartinib and its active metabolite AC886 are well characterized.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA generally agrees with the pharmacology and PK characteristics presented in Table 11 with
the exception of the dose proportionality range reported for patients with AML. Quizartinib
exposure (AUC and Cmax) increased proportionally in the dose range of 17.7 mg to 53 mg (free
base) in patients with AML, and not 17.1 to 53 mg which is mistakenly reported in Table 11.
E-R analysis of QTcF prolongation indicated that higher quizartinib concentrations are
associated with greater increases in QTcF interval. Clearance is expected to be lower during
maintenance treatment compared to induction or consolidation, and maintenance quizartinib
exposure is predicted to be higher than induction or consolidation quizartinib exposure
following the proposed dosage. In a typical subject in Study AC220-A-U302, the median QTcF
interval prolongation is expected to be +18.4 msec (90% CI 16.3 to 20.5 msec) at the maximum
concentration at steady state (Cmax,ss) following 26.5 mg QD during maintenance treatment and
+24.1 msec (90% CI 21.4 to 26.6 msec) at Cmax,ss following 53 mg QD during maintenance
treatment. See Section 19.4.3.4 for the detailed analysis of quizartinib concentration impact on
QTcF prolongation.

Clinical Pharmacology Questions
6.3.2.1. Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of
effectiveness?
The Applicant’s Position:
Yes. Ex vivo pharmacodynamic (PD) data (PIA) from Study 2689-CL-2004 show rapid and near
complete inhibition at 30 mg QD and 60 mg QD, supporting that adequate target coverage can
be expected under the proposed dosing regimen (Nepomuceno, 2014). Additionally, results
from the E-R analysis for efficacy provide additional supporting information (see Sections
6.2.2.1 and 19.4.3.1).
116
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
The FDA’s Assessment:
The primary evidence of effectiveness is the analysis of OS in Study AC220-A-U302 which is
described in Section 8.1. Supportive evidence of effectiveness from the clinical pharmacology
program is summarized below.
Using blood samples collected under protocol 2689-2004, an ex-vivo pharmacodynamic assay
was conducted evaluating the inhibition of phosphorylation of FLT3 using plasma inhibitory
assay (PIA) and STAT5. The assay was conducted on 47 samples collected day 8 and 15 samples
collected day 15 of quizartinib treatment. Administration of either 26.5 or 53 mg/day
quizartinib resulted in plasma levels that resulted in near complete FLT3-ITD inhibitory activity
as measured by the PIA assay. Expansion of the assay to evaluate inhibition of FLT3-WT and KIT
phosphorylation activity demonstrated reduced effects of quizartinib at both doses on these
kinases compared to FLT3-ITD, with notably large reductions in KIT inhibitory activity.
The E-R analysis of efficacy supports the conclusion that quizartinib induction and consolidation
is effective. After accounting for the impact of age on OS, the E-R analysis found that improved
OS may be associated with either higher quizartinib exposure during induction treatment or
treatment with quizartinib (versus placebo) (refer to Section 19.4.3.2 for detailed analysis).
The E-R analysis does not provide evidence of the effectiveness of quizartinib maintenance
treatment. The maintenance E-R analysis identified that improved OS was associated with
lower age and HSCT in subjects who received at least one dose of maintenance treatment.
However, no significant associations were identified between OS and induction exposure,
maintenance exposure, and/or treatment arm (i.e. quizartinib versus placebo). The E-R analyses
are described in detail in Section 19.4.3.
6.3.2.2. Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient
population for which the indication is being sought?
The Applicant’s Position:
Yes. Study AC220-A-U302 enrolled adult subjects from 18-75 years of age with FLT3-ITD (+)
newly diagnosed AML and demonstrated a clinically meaningful improvement in survival in the
overall population with a favorable benefit-risk assessment. Population PK analyses did not
indicate body weight, age, sex, race, or other factors to result in clinically meaningful changes in
exposure that would warrant does adjustments. See also Section 6.3.2.3.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The E-R analysis of efficacy supports the conclusion that the proposed 35.4 mg once daily dose
117
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
of quizartinib during induction and consolidation is acceptable from a clinical pharmacology
perspective.
For maintenance treatment, the E-R analysis does not provide evidence that the proposed
quizartinib maintenance dosage of 53 mg once daily is appropriate. The maintenance E-R
analysis indicates that improved OS was associated with lower age and HSCT in subjects who
received at least one dose of maintenance treatment. However, improved OS was not
associated with induction exposure, maintenance exposure, or quizartinib treatment (versus
placebo) in subjects who received at least one dose of maintenance treatment.
Safety analyses indicate that quizartinib is associated with safety events and that higher
quizartinib concentrations are associated with greater QTcF prolongation. Therefore, E-R
analyses suggests that the proposed maintenance dose of 53 mg once daily may expose
patients to unnecessary toxicity without improving survival compared to lower maintenance
doses. A PMR will address the need for additional data to determine an acceptable quizartinib
maintenance dosage (refer to Section 6.2.2.3). The maintenance E-R analysis is described in
detail in Section 19.4.3.2.

6.3.2.3.
Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for
subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors?
The Applicant’s Position:
No. Intrinsic factors, including age, sex, and race, may have an impact on quizartinib PK, but the
changes seen in the population PK analysis are not clinically meaningful and do not warrant any
dose adjustments. Mild or moderate renal or hepatic impairment have limited or no potential
to impact the elimination of quizartinib or AC886, and dose adjustments are not warranted.
Since there is no data on patients with severe renal or hepatic impairment, the use of
quizartinib in these populations is not recommended.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees that no clinically meaningful differences in exposure are expected according to body
weight, age, race (Black or African American, White, or Asian), mild or moderate hepatic
impairment, and mild or moderate renal impairment. Of note, there was limited representation
of racial subgroups other than White (65%), Asian (18%), or Black or African American (9%), and
it is unknown if the PK of quizartinib or AC886 differs in other racial subgroups.
Body weight and race were associated with statistically significant impacts on quizartinib
clearance. The median quizartinib AUCss, Cmax,ss, and Cmin,ss are 22 to 23% higher in a typical
118
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
patient weighing 65 kg compared to a typical patient weighing 85 kg, but this exposure
difference is not expected to have a significant impact on clinical outcomes. The PPK model also
predicted that Black and African American subjects would have 30 to 35% higher median AUCss
and Cmax,ss compared to subjects of other races, but E-R analysis did not find any E-R safety
associations where 30 to 35% higher exposure would result in higher rates of TEAEs. Therefore,
Black and African American patients with previously untreated AML are not expected to have
clinically significant differences in quizartinib exposure compared to patients of other races. See
Section 19.4.1.2 for detailed analysis of covariate effects on exposure.
Renal Impairment: The results of the ADME study (AC220-006) showed that <2% of the total
dose is excreted renally. Accordingly, renal impairment is expected to have a negligible impact
on quizartinib elimination. This relationship between creatinine clearance and circulating drug
concentrations was evaluated using population PK methods. Data from 932 subjects
(273 healthy subjects and 659 acute myeloid leukemia (AML) patients) were included in the PPK
model. Creatinine clearance (estimated by the Cockcroft-Gault equation) was assessed as a
covariate in the PPK analysis on quizartinib and AC886 clearance. Creatinine clearance in
subjects with mild and moderate renal impairment was not found to be a statistically significant
covariate on quizartinib and AC886 clearance (PPK Report AC220-PMx009). There were
insufficient data from subjects with severe renal impairment to draw meaningful conclusions
regarding the impact of severe renal impairment on quizartinib clearance (n = 4 [0.4%]). Based
on the PPK analyses, quizartinib dose adjustment is not required in patients with mild or
moderate renal impairment. Data were insufficient to draw conclusions regarding the impact of
severe renal impairment. Consequently, administration of quizartinib in subjects with severe
renal impairment is not recommended.
Hepatic Impairment: There have been two studies evaluating the impact of hepatic impairment
on quizartinib exposure.
Study AC220-016 was conducted in subjects with mild (n=8), moderate (n=8) hepatic
impairment based on Child-Pugh classification, and healthy subjects (n=14) who received a
single oral dose of 26.5 mg quizartinib. Noncompartmental analysis (NCA) of the PK data
showed that mild hepatic impairment has no significant effect on quizartinib and AC886
exposures (Table 12). The effect of moderate hepatic impairment had no significant effect on
quizartinib exposure, but resulted in a 35% decrease in AC886 exposure (Table 12).

In cancer patients, hepatic function is routinely classified using the NCI-ODWG criteria.
Evaluation of the liver scores in individual subjects showed that the ascites score was the main
contributor to the classification of subjects into either the mild or moderate liver impairment
categories for Child-Pugh. Based on the large difference in the number of subjects considered
healthy subjects or impaired based on the liver classification system, and the high risk of QTcF
prolongation, small changes in exposure may lead to potential risk of serious adverse events. As
a result, data using the NCI-ODWG classification criteria were considered as they are more
conservative.
The hepatic impairment subject population was re-evaluated using the NCI-ODWG criteria. This
resulted in 7 additional subjects being considered to have normal liver function, 8 subjects
meeting the criteria for mild hepatic impairment, and only a single subject meeting the criteria
for moderate hepatic impairment. Repeating the analysis using the NCI-ODWG classification
system showed that the effect of mild hepatic impairment has no clinically meaningful effect on
quizartinib and AC886 exposure. In addition, a second study was conducted, enrolling subjects
that met the NCI-ODWG criteria for moderate hepatic impairment.
Study AC220-A-U105 was conducted in in subjects with moderate (n=6) and healthy subjects
(n=6) based on NCI-ODWG criteria. The NCA showed that total quizartinib Cmax, AUClast, and
AUCinf were comparable for subjects with moderate hepatic impairment and control healthy
subjects; geometric mean ratios were 95.1%, 109%, and 109%, respectively.
The unbound fraction (fu) of quizartinib at 4 hours post dose was lower in subjects with
moderate hepatic impairment (mean of 0.00452, ranging from 0.00112 to 0.0108) compared
with control subjects (mean of 0.00900, ranging from 0.00318 to 0.0180). The mean unbound
Cmax and AUCinf of quizartinib were 55% and 49% lower, respectively, in subjects with

moderate hepatic impairment compared to control subjects.
These results should be interpreted cautiously as the mechanism for the decrease in free
(unbound) drug in patients with moderate hepatic impairment is not well explained at this
time. In AC220-A-U105, there were 17 PK samples per patient (~ 204 PK samples) available for
use in the total drug AUC calculation. Cmax and AUC for unbound drug were extrapolated from a
single free drug determination per patient (~12 samples) using the 4 hour post dose PK sample.
Quizartinib is over 99% protein bound, so the unbound fraction is quite small. Assay variability
and the small sample size may have influenced this finding. Table 13 summarizes quizartinib PK
in AC220-A-U105.
Active metabolite (AC886) Cmax and AUCinf were 25% and 30% lower, respectively, in subjects
with moderate hepatic impairment compared to control subjects. Mean t1/2 was longer for
subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (111.1 ± 48.7 hours) compared to control subjects
(81.4 ± 20.7 hours).
Moderate hepatic impairment does not significantly impact quizartinib exposure. Dose
adjustment for mild or moderate hepatic impairment is not required.

6.3.2.4. Are there clinically relevant food-drug or drug-drug interactions, and
what is the appropriate management strategy?
The Applicant’s Position:
Yes. Strong CYP3A inhibitors result in a clinically significant increase in exposure and warrant a
121

dose reduction to 50% of the regular dose when quizartinib is given concomitantly with strong
CYP3A inhibitors. Moderate or strong inducers of CYP3A have the potential to impact the
exposure to quizartinib and AC886 and should be avoided.
There were no clinically meaningful changes to quizartinib or AC886 plasma exposures when
coadministered with food or gastric acid-reducing agents (ARAs). Quizartinib can be
coadministered with gastric ARAs and without regard to food.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Food Effect:
The impact of a high-fat (50%), high calorie (approximately 150, 250, and 500 to 600 calories
from protein, carbohydrate, and fat, respectively, for a total of 1000 calories meal) on
quizartinib and AC886 PK was evaluated in 63 healthy subjects administered a single oral
26.5 mg dose of quizartinib in Study AC220-019. No significant change in AUC or Cmax was
observed in the fed compared to the fasted subjects. Median Tmax was 2 hours longer for
quizartinib and 31 hours longer for AC886 when quizartinib was given with a high-fat meal
compared to the fasted state (Table 14). These results support the labeling recommendation of
administering quizartinib with or without food.

FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position that dose reductions are warranted when
quizartinib is given concomitantly with a strong CYP3A inhibitor. It is noted that during the first
two weeks of maintenance a 33% (instead of 50%) dose reduction from 26.5 to 17.7 mg is
recommended. The following dosage modifications are recommended:
• If the dose being administered is 35.4 mg, then the dose is reduced to 17.7 mg in the
presence of a strong CYP3A inhibitor.
• If the dose being administered is 26.5 mg, then the dose is reduced to 17.7 mg in the
presence of a strong CYP3A inhibitor.
• If the dose being administered is 53 mg, then the dose is reduced to 26.5 mg in the
presence of a strong CYP3A inhibitor.
Based on results from Study AC220-015, no clinically significant differences in quizartinib
exposure are expected with concomitant use of moderate CYP3A inhibitors.
In Study AC220-015, the effect of fluconazole, a moderate CYP3A inhibitor, or ketoconazole, a
strong CYP3A inhibitor, on quizartinib and AC886 PK was evaluated using an open-label,
randomized, placebo-controlled, parallel design study. Three groups of 31 healthy subjects each
were evaluated for PK interactions:
Group 1 Ketoconazole 200 mg twice
Quizartinib 26.5 mg single dose
daily on Days 1 through 28
on Day 8
Group 2 Fluconazole 200 mg twice daily Quizartinib 26.5 mg single dose
on Days 1 through 28
on Day 8
Group 3 Placebo twice daily on Days 1
Quizartinib 26.5 mg single dose
through 28
on Day 8
Repeated coadministration of ketoconazole resulted in a 94% increase in quizartinib geometric
mean AUC0-inf and a 17% increase in quizartinib geometric mean Cmax. Active metabolite AC886
geometric mean AUC0-inf decreased by 15%, and its geometric mean Cmax decreased by 60%.
The time to reach maximal AC886 concentration was prolonged indicating that CYP3A4mediated conversion of quizartinib to AC886 was inhibited. These changes are considered
clinically relevant drug-drug interaction at steady-state for both exposure parameters.
Fluconazole coadministration had a lesser impact on quizartinib exposure. There was an 18%
increase in quizartinib AUC0-504h and an 11% increase in quizartinib Cmax. The AC886 AUC0-504h
increased by 12% and AC886 Cmax increased by 2%. The changes in exposure following
fluconazole administration were not considered to be clinically significant.

time curve up 504 hours post-dose; BID = twice daily; CI = confidence interval; Cmax = maximum plasma concentration.
Source: AC220-015 Final Study Report; Table 14, 16; Page 62, 65

Effect of Strong and Moderate CYP3A4 Inducers:

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Study AC220-A-U106 determined that quizartinib AUC up to infinity (AUC0-inf) decreased by 90%
and Cmax decreased by 45% following a single 53 mg dose of quizartinib with concomitant use of
efavirenz (a moderate CYP3A inducer). The AUC0-inf of AC886 decreased by 96% and the AC886
Cmax decreased by 68%. The PPK data is inadequate to assess the impact of weak, moderate, or
strong CYP3A inducer coadministration on quizartinib or AC886 PK. Due to the potential for
decreased effectiveness with lower quizartinib exposure, FDA agrees with the Applicant’s
position to avoid concomitant use of strong or moderate CYP3A inducers.
Effect of Gastric Acid Reducing Agents:
Study AC220-018 evaluated the effect of a proton pump inhibitor (PPI), lansoprazole, 60 mg on
days 1-5 on quizartinib and AC886 PK following a single oral dose of 26.5 mg quizartinib in
62 healthy subjects. Concomitant administration of lansoprazole had no meaningful effect on
quizartinib AUC, Cmax and Tmax compared to quizartinib alone (Table 17). These results suggest
that quizartinib can be taken concomitantly with PPIs without adjusting the quizartinib dose.

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
The Applicant’s Position:
As of 06 May 2022, the quizartinib clinical program consisted of 26 clinical studies conducted by
the Applicant, including 13 studies in either newly diagnosed or R/R AML, 1 study in solid
tumors, and 12 single-dose studies in healthy subjects and subjects with hepatic impairment.
Of the 13 studies in subjects with AML, 10 completed studies are included in the evaluation of
the safety and efficacy of quizartinib (3 in newly diagnosed AML, 1 in maintenance
monotherapy after HSCT, and 6 in R/R AML). A pediatric study, AC220-A-U202, in R/R FLT3-ITD
(+) AML (see Section 8.2.9) and a foreign study, AC220-A-A103, in Chinese subjects with newly
diagnosed AML are ongoing studies and were excluded from the NDA. Additionally,
Study DS3032-A-U105 was excluded as it was a combination study with milademetan and thus,
was not using the target quizartinib treatment regimen.
The studies summarized in Table 18 provide the basis for the evaluation of clinical
pharmacology, efficacy and safety of quizartinib in the indicated population with Study AC220A-U302 providing the pivotal data supporting safety and efficacy of quizartinib in the proposed
indication. Nine additional studies support the safety of quizartinib and are included in the All
AML Pool and an additional 14 studies provide additional information pertinent to this
application. The doses of quizartinib administered in these studies ranged from 12 mg to
450 mg QD, with treatment duration of up to 241 weeks.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA aggress with the Applicant’s description of their Clinical Studies Relevant to this
Application.
The FDA review was based on data from NDA 216993, relevant published literature, and
relevant information in the public domain.
Of note, Study AC220-A-U302 is the pivotal study that was the basis for establishing the safety
and efficacy of quizartinib in newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD mutated patients. The Applicant
submitted a complete dataset for this study, using a data cut date of August 13, 2021, at the
time of the initial NDA submission (August 24, 2022). This data set will form the basis for this
clinical review.
At the time of the NDA submission, the Applicant submitted complete datasets from 9
additional studies in patients with AML, 1 study in patients with solid tumors, and 12 completed
studies in healthy volunteers (Table 18). The additional studies in patients with AML provide
supporting evidence of efficacy, particularly Study AC220-007. Data from all of the additional
studies were used to supplement the analysis of safety.

8 Statistical and Clinical Evaluation
Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
The clinical development program of quizartinib in AML subjects consists of 13 clinical studies,
10 of which supported efficacy. Five Phase 1 studies and three Phase 2 studies examining the
efficacy of quizartinib at different dose levels in subjects with FLT3-ITD (+) have been
completed. Two large pivotal Phase 3 studies were conducted: Study AC220-007 in patients
with R/R FLT3-ITD (+) AML and Study AC220-A-U302 in patients with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD
(+) AML.
The efficacy of quizartinib in the treatment of patients with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML
is based on the data from a single pivotal Phase 3 study AC220-A-U302 (N = 539) in newly
diagnosed AML subjects, with supplemental data from the three Phase 1 studies, 2689-CL-0005
(N = 18; newly diagnosed AML), AC220-A-J102 (N = 7; newly diagnosed AML), and 2689-CL-0011
(N = 13; post-HSCT AML), for the safety and preliminary efficacy of quizartinib in this indication.
In addition to the studies mentioned above, the results from Studies AC220-007, AC220-002,
2689-CL-2004, CP0001, AC220-A-J101, and AC220-A-J201 provide additional supporting data
pertinent to the use of quizartinib in patients with AML and are described in the sections below.
Pivotal Study in Newly Diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML - Study AC220-A-U302
(QuANTUM-First)
The primary evaluation of efficacy of quizartinib in the treatment of patients with newly
diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML is based on the data from the single pivotal Phase 3 study,
AC220-A-U302 (N=539).
Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
136
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Study AC220-A-U302 was a Phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, global study
to compare the effect of quizartinib versus placebo (administered with standard induction and
consolidation chemotherapy, then administered as continuation therapy for up to 36 cycles) on
the primary endpoint of OS in subjects 18 to 75 years of age with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+)
AML. The study design from the Screening Visit through the Treatment Period is shown in
Figure 1. The Long-term Follow-up Phase was to begin upon completion of 36 cycles of study
drug (quizartinib/placebo) in the Continuation Phase or permanent discontinuation of study
drug in any phase.
Figure 1 – Study Design Schematic (Screening to Treatment Period) for Study AC220-A-U302

a

During Induction Cycle 2 investigators may choose to administer the “7+3” chemotherapy regimen, or the “5+2”
chemotherapy regimen, and quizartinib/placebo will therefore start on Day 8 or Day 6, respectively.

Following the disclosure within the RATIFY study of most relapses occurring within 24 months,
the duration of the Continuation Phase was increased to 36 cycles. Patients were appropriately
informed of available treatment options.
Trial Location: This study was conducted at 193 study sites across 26 countries in North
America, Europe, and Asia/Other Regions.
Treatment assignment: 539 subjects were enrolled and randomized to one of the two
treatment arms (quizartinib arm or placebo arm) in a 1:1 ratio through a stratified permuted
block randomization procedure by an Interactive Voice/Web Response System (IXRS).
Randomization was stratified by region (North America, Europe, Asia/Other Regions), age
(<60 years old, ≥60 years old), and WBC count at the time of diagnosis of AML (<40×109/L,
≥40×109/L).
137
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Blinding: This is a double-blind placebo-controlled study. Neither the subjects nor any of the
investigators, Applicant, or contract research organizations (CROs) were aware of the
quizartinib/placebo treatments received. During the course of the prior NDA review, 5 patients
were unblinded at the request of the FDA, all of whom received quizartinib.
Study treatment: During the first induction cycle, patients received a standard 7+3 induction
regimen with cytarabine 100 mg/m2/day (200 mg/m2/day allowed if institutional or local
standard) by continuous intravenous (IV) infusion from Days 1 to 8 and anthracycline
(daunorubicin 60 mg/m2/day or idarubicin 12 mg/m2/day) on Days 1, 2, and 3. Study drug
(quizartinib 40 mg or placebo) was to be administered QD starting on Day 8 (at the end of the
cytarabine infusion) for 14 consecutive days. Subjects could receive either a second cycle of
identical induction chemotherapy or a five-day infusion of cytarabine and 2 daily doses of
daunorubicin or idarubicin (5 + 2) according to institutional routine practice followed by 14 days
of quizartinib 40 mg (or placebo) QD.
Patients achieving CR or CRi at the end of induction could proceed to consolidation, consisting
of (1) treatment with HDAC (3.0 g/m2 IV infusion every 12 hours on Days 1, 3, and 5, reduced to
1.5 g/m2 for patients aged ≥60 years) followed by quizartinib (40 mg/day) or placebo for 14
days starting on Day 6 of each cycle (up to 4 cycles), or (2) allo-HSCT only (between the end of
induction to within 3 months of starting continuation), or (3) treatment with HDAC followed by
quizartinib (40 mg/day) or placebo followed by allo-HSCT.
Continuation monotherapy with quizartinib/placebo was to begin after induction and
consolidation therapy upon blood count recovery. In the Continuation Phase, the dose of
quizartinib/placebo started at 30 mg orally QD and increased to 60 mg/day on Cycle 1 Day 16 if
the average QTcF of the triplicate ECG was ≤450 ms. Study drug continued for up to 36 cycles
or until relapse, start of non-protocol-specified AML treatment, death, unacceptable toxicity, or
study closure, whichever occurred first.
For subjects concomitantly receiving a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, the dose of quizartinib was to
be reduced from 40 to 20 mg QD during the Induction and Consolidation Phases and from 30 to
20 mg QD or from 60 to 30 mg QD during the Continuation Phase.
Duration of treatment: Total duration of treatment with study drug was up to 42 cycles
(inclusive of the Induction, Consolidation, and Continuation Phases). All subjects who
completed 36 cycles of quizartinib/placebo in the Continuation Phase or permanently
discontinued study drug in any phase entered the Long-term Follow-up Phase.
Choice of the control arm: At the time of initiation of the global, multicenter study AC220-AU302 (Sep 2016), chemotherapy with or without allo-HSCT was the standard for the treatment
of AML. Hence, placebo along with standard combination chemotherapy was used as the
comparator arm in the study.

138
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
In 2017, the multikinase Type I FLT3 inhibitor midostaurin was approved in the US for the
treatment of newly diagnosed FLT3-TKD (+) or FLT3-ITD (+) AML based on the results of the
RATIFY study (Stone, 2017). Approval status and accessibility varied globally following the US
approval with limited availability of midostaurin in the European Union and Asia/Pacific regions,
from which the majority of subjects were enrolled. At the time of this approval,
screening/enrollment of Study AC220-A-U302 was well underway. As such, modifying the
comparator from placebo to midostaurin in this global study would have significantly delayed
critical results for an urgently needed treatment option for patients with newly diagnosed
FLT3-ITD (+) AML. In this context, it is important to note that nonclinical and prior clinical data
suggested that the second-generation, highly selective, Type II FLT3 inhibitor quizartinib may
result in improved clinical activity for patients with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML (Cortes,
2019; Aikawa, 2020) and Study AC220-A-U302 was conducted to clinically test this hypothesis in
patients up to 75 years of age.
The Applicant consulted the scientific steering committee, medical experts in AML, and
ethicists, who concluded that equipoise was maintained and the study should continue as a
placebo-controlled study. The recommendation was based on the assessment that newly
diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML remains a poor prognostic and aggressive disease with a high
unmet medical need, and that advancing a next-generation FLT3 inhibitor would provide
patients the opportunity to gain access to additional treatment options. Given the poor
prognosis of patients with FLT3-ITD (+) AML, the high risk of relapse in this population, and the
limited global availability of midostaurin in key regions, no changes in the control arm of the
Study AC220-A-U302 were implemented.
Dose modification/dose discontinuation: Guidelines for dose delay or dose modification and
treatment discontinuation in response to specific AEs were specified in the study protocol.
Study drug dose reduction or dose interruption were to be implemented for QTcF prolongation,
other nonhematologic toxicities, myelosuppression, and concomitant administration of a strong
CYP3A4 inhibitor. The minimum dose of study drug was not to be lower than 30 mg/day in any
phase of the study in general and not lower than 20 mg/day for subjects receiving strong
CYP3A4 inhibitors. If study drug was interrupted, missed doses were not to be made up.
Concomitant medications: Subjects were not allowed to receive concomitant chemotherapy,
immunotherapy, radiotherapy, transplant, or any ancillary therapy for AML that was not
specified in the protocol or that was considered to be investigational (ie, used for nonapproved
indication[s] and in the context of a research investigation) while on study drug.
Medications associated with QT/QTc prolongation were prohibited. Exceptions were made for
antibiotics, antivirals, and antifungals that were used as standard of care for the prevention or
treatment of infections or if the investigator believed that beginning therapy with a potentially
QTc-prolonging medication (such as antiemetic) was vital to an individual subject’s care while
on study. In that case, additional ECG monitoring was to be performed at the commencement

were strong or moderate CYP3A4 inducers.
Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors were to be avoided, if possible, but if necessary for subject care,
strong CYP3A4 inhibitors were allowed with a corresponding dose reduction of study drug.
There were no restrictions for moderate or weak CYP3A4 inhibitors.
Diagnostic Criteria: Subjects were enrolled based on FLT3-ITD (+) status as determined centrally
(b) (4)
using the
FLT3-ITD clinical trial assay (CTA) for the
determination of FLT3-ITD mutation status from bone marrow and/or blood samples. This CTA
is a molecular assay that employs polymerase chain reaction (PCR) and capillary electrophoresis
technology. The Applicant also collaborated with Invivoscribe Inc., to develop a PCR and
capillary electrophoresis-based molecular assay for detecting FLT3-ITD mutations as an in vitro
diagnostic assay for a companion diagnostic.
Inclusion/Exclusion criteria: The study enrolled subjects with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML.
Key inclusion criteria:
• Newly diagnosed, morphologically documented primary AML or AML secondary to
myelodysplastic syndrome or a myeloproliferative neoplasm, based on the World Health
Organization (WHO) 2008 classification (at screening)
• ≥18 years or the minimum legal adult age (whichever was greater) and ≤75 years (at
screening) and receiving standard induction chemotherapy regimen as per protocol
• Presence of FLT3-ITD activating mutation in bone marrow (≥3% FLT3-ITD/total FLT3)
• Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group (ECOG) PS 0 to 2 (at the time the subject signed
his/her first informed consent form)
• Adequate renal function (creatine clearance >50 mL/min), adequate hepatic function
(total serum bilirubin ≤1.5 × upper limit of normal [ULN]; serum alkaline phosphatase
[ALP], aspartate aminotransferase [AST] and alanine aminotransferase [ALT]
≤2.5 × ULN), and serum electrolytes within the institution’s normal limits at the time of
randomization (Day 7 of induction chemotherapy)
Key exclusion criteria:
• Diagnosis of acute promyelocytic leukemia (APL), French-American-British (FAB)
classification M3 or WHO classification of APL with translocation, t(15;17)(q22;q12), or
BCR-ABL-positive leukemia (ie, chronic myelogenous leukemia in blast crisis)
• Diagnosis of AML secondary to prior chemotherapy or radiotherapy for other neoplasms
• History of known central nervous system (CNS) leukemia, including cerebrospinal fluid
positive for AML blasts; lumbar puncture was recommended for subjects with
symptoms of CNS leukemia to rule out extramedullary CNS involvement
• QTcF >450 ms or diagnosis of or suspicion of long QT syndrome (LQTS; including family
history of LQTS); or uncontrolled or significant cardiovascular disease (eg, history of

Study assessments: During the Induction Phase, a bone marrow aspirate specimen and blood
samples for hematology (complete blood count [CBC]) were collected on Day 21 (or up to
Day 28) of Cycle 1 and Cycle 2. The investigator assessed the subject’s response based on the
local laboratory results using the modified International Working Group response criteria for
AML (Cheson, 2003).
Starting on Day 21, blood samples for hematology (CBC) were also collected at least weekly to
assess count recovery. If an accurate assessment of response could not be made based on the
Day 21 bone marrow aspirate, then the bone marrow aspirate was to be repeated upon count
recovery or Day 56 (±3 days), whichever occurred first.
CR was defined as an absolute neutrophil count (ANC) of ≥1000 cells/mm3, a platelet count of
≥100,000 cells/mm3, and bone marrow with <5% blasts. CRi was defined as meeting all the
criteria for CR with incomplete hematological recovery with residual neutropenia (ANC
<1000 cells/mm3) and/or platelet count <100,000 cells/mm3. There should be no blasts with
Auer rods or the presence of extramedullary disease (eg, CNS or soft tissue involvement).
During the Continuation Phase, a bone marrow aspirate specimen for relapse assessment and
blood samples for hematology (CBC) were collected, and physical examination (including an
evaluation for signs and symptoms of relapse) was performed at the visits specified in the study
protocol. If relapse was suspected based on abnormal peripheral smears or development of
cytopenias at any time during the Continuation Phase, a bone marrow aspirate specimen was
to be collected for analysis of response/remission.
During the study treatment, subjects were monitored regularly in all phases for toxicities,
including triplicate ECG for central reading, laboratory tests, electrolytes, and hematology
monitoring. Dose modifications were required if the subject experienced QTcF prolongation
based on study-supplied ECG machine read, other nonhematologic toxicities, or
myelosuppression or had received concomitant strong CYP3A4 inhibitors during treatment.
The Long-term Follow-up Phase began upon completion of 36 cycles of study drug in the
Continuation Phase or upon permanent discontinuation of study drug in any phase. During this
phase, subjects were regularly assessed for relapse and survival status.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the trial design of the phase 3 Study AC220-AU302. However, we note that there are typos in the study schema and text above describing
the duration of continuous cytarabine infusion. The duration of continuous cytarabine was 7
days not 8 as noted in the text and in the study diagram above. Treatment with quizartinib or
placebo would begin on day 8 after the completion of the cytarabine infusion.

FDA notes that the Applicant’s definition of CR response is different from that specified in FDA’s

opposed to ≥ the respective cutoffs).
Study Endpoints
The Applicant’s Description:
The primary endpoint for Study AC220-A-U302 was OS defined as the time from randomization
until death from any cause. Subjects alive or lost to follow-up at the time of analysis were
censored at the date when they were last known to be alive. OS is the most reliable cancer
endpoint since it is precise and easy to measure, documented by the date of death, and bias is
not a factor in endpoint measurement (FDA, 2020).
Secondary endpoints included EFS, CR rate, percentage of subjects achieving CR with FLT3-ITD
MRD negativity, composite complete remission (CRc) rate, and rate of subjects achieving CRc
with FLT3-ITD MRD negativity.
• EFS was defined as the time from randomization until the date of the earliest of any of
the following: refractory disease or treatment failure as determined at the end of the
Induction Phase (ITF), relapse, or death from any cause. In the clinical study protocol,
ITF was defined as failure to achieve CR or CRi by the end of the Induction Phase. The
assessment of response was allowed up to Day 56 from the start of the last cycle of
induction. The Applicant incorporated the Agency’s feedback to define ITF as failure to
achieve CR within 42 days of the start of the last cycle of induction chemotherapy for
the primary analysis of EFS and the original protocol definition of EFS was maintained
as a sensitivity analysis.
• CR and CRc rates were the percentages of subjects achieving CR or CRc after induction,
respectively.
• Percentages of subjects achieving CR with FLT3-ITD MRD negativity or CRc with
FLT3-ITD MRD negativity following induction therapy. MRD is the presence of a small
number of leukemic cells in the bone marrow or blood of subjects with AML below the
level of detection using conventional morphologic assessment. The FLT3-ITD MRD
assessment by next-generation sequencing was used to detect and quantify residual
FLT3-ITD mutations present in a fraction of remaining leukemic cells in bone marrow or
blood and assessments were made using a cutoff of 1 × 10-4 (1 in 10,000 cells).
Exploratory endpoints considered important to characterize overall efficacy were relapse-free
survival (RFS), duration of CR, and cumulative incidence of relapse (CIR).

142
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
•

RFS for subjects who achieved CR or CRi in the Induction Phase was defined as the time
from randomization until documented relapse or death from any cause, whichever
came first.

•

Duration of CR was defined as the time from the first documented CR until the date of
documented relapse or death from any cause, whichever came first.

•

CIR (post hoc analysis ) was defined as the cumulative incidence of relapse from
randomization in all subjects who achieved a CR during the Induction Phase.

The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the study endpoints.
Statistical Analysis Plan (SAP) and Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
The primary analysis for OS was conducted after 287 OS events were observed with a minimum
of 24 months of follow-up since the time of the last subject randomized. Sensitivity and
supplementary analyses to examine the robustness of the OS results were conducted as
appropriate. The comparison of the distribution of OS between treatments arms was made
using a stratified log-rank test performed at a 2-sided α = 0.05 level with the three stratification
factors used for randomization. The median OS was calculated based on Kaplan-Meier
estimates and the corresponding 95% confidence interval (CI) was calculated using the method
provided by Brookmeyer and Crowley (Brookmeyer, 1982). The hazard ratio (HR) with 95% CI
for treatment group was estimated using stratified Cox proportional hazards (PH) models, with
the 3 stratification factors used for randomization per IXRS.
EFS was analyzed using the stratified log-rank test and stratified Cox PH model. Subjects who
had no EFS events were censored at the last response assessment date. The primary analysis
for EFS was based on the response assessment by Independent Review Committee (IRC) with
ITF defined as not achieving response of CR, using a 42-day window from the start of the last
cycle in induction for CR evaluation. The ITF definition in the original protocol considering CR
and CRi by the end of induction (ie, within a 56-day window) was used in the sensitivity and
supplementary analysis of EFS. Further details on these analyses are provided in the SAP.
Analysis sets: The Intent-to-treat (ITT) Analysis Set included all subjects who were randomized.
The Safety Analysis Set included all subjects who received at least one dose of
quizartinib/placebo.
Method for handling missing data: In general, missing or dropout data were not imputed for
the purpose of data analysis, unless otherwise specified. In the case where AE and
prior/concomitant medication start dates and/or stop dates were partial or missing, refer to
Section 8.2.3 of the SAP for detailed imputation rules (Appendix 16.1.9). The censoring rules
143
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
for OS and EFS are detailed in the SAP.
Statistical methods for multiplicity: To control for the family-wise type I error rate for primary
and secondary efficacy endpoints, a serial hierarchically ordered gatekeeping strategy was
employed. The primary assessment of OS in the ITT Analysis Set was evaluated first, and if
significant at a 2-sided alpha of 0.05, a statistical evaluation of EFS in the ITT Analysis Set was to
be performed. After EFS evaluation, the order of other secondary endpoints to be tested was
CR rate, rate of subjects achieving CR with FLT3-ITD MRD negativity, CRc rate, and rate of
subjects achieving CRc with FLT3-ITD MRD negativity. Testing was to be stopped once one test
in the sequence failed to be statistically significant. No interim analysis was planned or
performed.
Planned subgroup analyses: Predefined subgroup analyses for OS based on demographics (age,
sex, race, and geographical region) and baseline disease characteristics (baseline ECOG PS, WBC
count at the time of diagnosis, choice of anthracycline used during the Induction Phase, AML
cytogenetic risk score, FLT3-ITD VAF at randomization, and NPM1 mutation status) were
conducted.
The subgroup analyses of OS were conducted in a similar manner as the primary analyses of OS.
However, the analyses were performed without stratification and no p-values were reported.
Forest plots presenting the HRs and 2-sided 95% CIs by subgroup categories were provided on a
single plot.

Version 2.0 incorporated cumulative changes made to the clinical study protocol up to and
including version 7.0 (see Table 20) and was finalized before the database lock occurred.

Financial disclosure information was provided for clinical investigators involved in the covered
clinical Study AC220-A-U302. The summary of financial disclosure information is provided in
Appendix 19.2. There were 0 clinical investigators with disclosable financial interests.
The Applicant’s Position:
No concerns were raised regarding the overall integrity of the study data.
The FDA’s Assessment:
We confirm the Applicant’s position.
Subject Disposition
Data:
Overall, 3468 subjects were screened for participation in the study, of whom 2929 subjects
were screen failures. Of the screen failures, 2556 subjects were determined to be FLT3-ITD (‒)
per central testing, some of which did not satisfy other eligibility criteria, while 2358 (80.5%)
were listed on the CRF as screen failures due to a negative results for FLT3-ITD status. In total,
539 subjects comprising the ITT Analysis Set were subsequently randomized into the study (268
subjects in quizartinib arm and 271 subjects in placebo arm). Six subjects (3 in each arm) did not
receive any study treatment, hence, the safety population comprised of 533 subjects (265
subjects in quizartinib arm and 268 subjects in placebo arm), all of which entered the Induction
Phase.

The Applicant’s Position:
Similar percentages of subjects in the quizartinib and placebo arm received 2 cycles of
induction, discontinued study drug during Induction and Consolidation Phase. However, more
subjects entered the Continuation Phase in the quizartinib arm compared to placebo. Also,
similar percentages of subjects received protocol-specified HSCT.
While similar percentages of subjects discontinued study drug over all phases, there are notable
differences in rates between the quizartinib and placebo arm due to refractory disease or
relapse, with fewer such events on the quizartinib arm, while discontinuation of study drug due
to AEs was more frequent in the quizartinib arm. Overall, there were a higher percentage of
subjects alive and continuing in long-term follow-up and fewer study discontinuations, mainly
due to fewer subjects dying, on the quizartinib arm compared to the placebo arm. These
differences between arms are reflective of a treatment effect observed for quizartinib.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s summary of subject disposition as of the data cutoff date of
August 13, 2021. We note that the more patients discontinued study treatment due to AE on
the quizartinib arm (21.6% quizartinib versus 8.6% placebo). However, discontinuation of study
drug due to refractory disease or disease relapse was higher on the placebo arm (32%
quizartinib versus 50% placebo). Since discontinuation of study drug due to disease
148
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
relapsed/refractory disease portends a poor outcome, it is not surprising that more quizartinib
patients remained on study at the time of data lock (45% quizartinib versus 38% placebo) and
that more patients had discontinued study due to death on the placebo arm (50% quizartinib
versus 58% placebo). Lastly, consistent with remaining eligible for later stages of treatment,
more quizartinib patients started the continuation phase than placebo treated patients (44%
quizartinib versus 34% placebo).

The Applicant’s Position:
Protocol deviations occurred at similar rates in both study arms and are not likely to affect the
study results or the conclusions of the primary analysis.
The FDA’s Assessment:
We agree with the Applicant’s assessment. The rate of protocol violations was similar on both
arms. Since the trial was randomized and double blinded and the rate of violations was similar
on both arms, it is unlikely that protocol violations introduced bias into the study results.
Therefore, all patients, including those with important protocol deviations, were included in our
analysis of efficacy endpoints.

The Applicant’s Position:
Demographic and baseline characteristics for subjects in the ITT Analysis Set were consistent
with a FLT3-ITD (+) newly diagnosed AML population. Subjects were evenly distributed among
treatment arms by age, sex, race, and ECOG PS.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s tabulations and assessment of demographic and baseline
characteristics for the ITT population. It is notable that eligibility criteria for Study AC220-AU302 included patients up to age 75. Consequently, 40% of the trial population was ≥60 years
of age, with a full 25% ≥65 years old. This is an older trial population than was enrolled on the
Ratify Study, which enrolled a younger population with an age cutoff of 60. Also of interest, due
to the approval of midostaurin by FDA in April 2017, most patients for study AC220-A-U302
were enrolled from Europe and Asia in countries where midostaurin was not available at the
time of patient enrollment.

The Applicant’s Position:
Relevant baseline characteristics, including AML history and relevant genetic, morphologic, and
cytogenetic characteristics were well balanced between arms. The study population had
predominantly high-risk features based on cytogenetic risk factors as well as tumor burden,
both of which are associated with a worse prognosis. The most frequent FAB type was acute
myelomonocytic leukemia, and the majority of subjects in both the quizartinib and placebo
arms had an intermediate or unfavorable risk status according to cytogenetic patterns.
The FDA’s Assessment:
We agree with the Applicant’s assessment of the ITT population. The baseline demographics
were similar between treatment arms in terms of disease characteristics including cytogenetic
risk group, FLT3-ITD VAF level, and presenting WBC count. FDA has confirmed the tabulations.
Treatment Compliance, Concomitant Medications, and Rescue Medication Use
Data:
The median relative dose intensity (ie, the percentage of intended dose administered) was
100% (range: 20%, 207%) in the quizartinib arm and 100% (range: 40%, 200%) in the placebo
arm.
All subjects were administered concomitant medications during the study and the use of
concomitant medications was generally comparable between subjects in the quizartinib arm
and the placebo arm. Major protocol deviations due to the use of prohibited medications were
reported in none of the subjects in the quizartinib arm and in 1 (0.4%) subject in the placebo
arm.
No rescue medication was used in Study AC220-A-U302.
The Applicant’s Position:
Treatment compliance was high and comparable between the quizartinib arm and placebo arm,
indicating that quizartinib treatment had comparable tolerability to placebo and concomitant
medication use was balanced between the treatment arms.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. Table 25 presents the drug exposures by
treatment phases. It shows that a second course of induction was administered to 20%
(109/539) of the patients, 65% (348/539) initiated at least one cycle of consolidation, and 39%

The Applicant’s Position:
Study AC220-A-U302 demonstrated a statistically significant and clinically meaningful
improvement in OS for quizartinib in combination with chemotherapy then as continuation
therapy compared with chemotherapy and placebo followed by placebo as continuation
therapy in a population of subjects with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML. While
Study AC220-A-U302 was not powered to evaluate differences between the prespecified
subgroups, the improvement in OS was observed across most of these subgroups in favor of
quizartinib.
The improvement in OS is of particular relevance because Study AC220-A-U302 was conducted
in a population of subjects with predominantly high-risk features based on cytogenetic risk
factors as well as tumor burden, both of which are associated with a worse prognosis and
shorter median OS (Levis, 2004) and for whom new and effective therapies represent a
particularly high unmet need. The study included younger (≤60 years) and older (>60 years;
upper limit was 75 years) subjects notably those who are eligible for intensive therapy, for
whom current treatment options remain unsatisfactory.
The FDA’s Assessment:
158
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
FDA conducted independent analyses on the primary endpoint OS and found the primary
analysis results were consistent with those reported by the Applicant (Table 26 and Figure 2).
However, due to plateauing of the survival curves just above and below the median, the
median estimates may exaggerate the survival benefit. Furthermore, there was a detrimental
effect of OS in the first few months. During Induction, there was a higher number of deaths due
to adverse events for quizartinib versus placebo (7.2% vs. 4.5%). The data cut-off was
performed when all patients had either discontinued or had at least 24 months of follow-up.
Median follow-up time was 39.2 months in both arms. Most of the censoring occurred after 24
months post-randomization.
FDA sensitivity analyses of OS are presented in Table 28, including unstratified analysis,
stratification factors per eCRF (rather than randomization system as in primary analysis), Per
Protocol Set, and censoring OS at the start of conditioning for HSCT. The results of FDA
sensitivity analyses of OS from the unstratified analysis and the censoring OS at the start of
conditioning for HSCT were comparable to that reported by the Applicant (Table 27). These
sensitivity analyses are supportive of the primary analysis result.
