Subgroup analyses were conducted to understand if any patient subgroup was driving the early
detriment in mortality. Appendix 19.5.1 presents subgroup results for OS by 3 months, 6
months, or any time. While there is some heterogeneity amongst the subgroups in terms of
treatment effect, no single subgroup appears to be driving the early detriment in OS.
Review of the subgroup analysis presented in the Figure 3 Forest Plot above resulted in
additional questions about the nature of quizartinib’s effect. The following clinical comments
are exploratory hypotheses motivated by these results.
Clinical reviewer comments: In terms of age, we see that while all age groups appeared to
benefit from quizartinib treatment, the youngest age group appeared to derive the greatest
benefit. It is notable that this age group, patients <60, is analogous to the patient population
159
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
enrolled on the midostaurin pivotal study. However, it is important to remember that age is an
independent risk factor for survival in AML, which was again demonstrated with median survival
for patients ≥ 65 on both arms being inferior to the younger patients.
Another two subgroups that are interesting to consider were based on presenting WBC count
and FLT3-ITD VAF level. Again, while the point estimate for all level of these subgroups showed
benefit with quizartinib treatment, patients presenting with WBC ≥ 40x109/L or higher FLT3-ITD
VAF level appeared to show greater benefit from quizartinib treatment. This might not be
surprising since active FLT3-ITD is thought to drive a particularly proliferative form of AML.
Therefore, the higher presenting WBC could indicate greater reliance on the oncogenic signaling
from the mutant FLT3-ITD and greater sensitivity to loss of that signal. Along the same line, a
higher presenting FLT3-ITD VAF would also argue for greater reliance on FLT3 signaling in the
AML clones existing at presentation and sensitivity to loss of that signaling.
Results of the NPM1 subgroup are interesting. NPM1 mutations frequently cooccur with FLT3ITD mutations and was thought to improve the risk category of the FLT3-ITD AML, at least for
FLT3-ITD that has a low VAF. The results above suggest that quizartinib benefits patients that
have both FLT3-ITD and NPM1 mutant AML while being neutral for those with FLT3-ITD and WT
NPM1. It is not clear why this might be the case. Two things to point out are that the most
recent ELN AML guidelines now consider FLT3-ITD with either NPM1 Wt or NPM1 mutant to be
intermediate risk disease (Döhner, 2022). The second is that on the control arm, patients with
NPM1 mutant disease had a lower median OS than patient with NPM1 Wt disease. Therefore,
the result observed on Study AC220-A-U302 regarding NPM1 status likely reflects a combination
of 1) recent recognition that in the presence of any level of FLT3-ITD, NPM1 status no longer
predicts significant prognostic difference and 2) the relative underperformance of NPM1
mutant/overperformance of NMP1 Wt patients on the placebo arm of the trial. Lastly, it is
difficult to make firm conclusions based on this post-hoc subgroup analysis. As noted in Section
6.2.2.2, the results for EFS in the NPM1 Wt subgroup were supportive of positive benefit of
quizartinib (HR 0.760).
The last group that suggested a surprising result was response by cytogenetic risk group. While
intermediate, unfavorable, and even unknown cytogenetic risk groups appeared to benefit from
quizartinib treatment, patients with favorable risk disease appeared to benefit on the placebo
arm. Patients in this risk group have either t(15;17), t(8;21), inv(16), or t(16;16). Since patients
with t(15;17) were excluded from Study AC220-A-U302, patients in this group were represented
by t(8;21), inv(16), or t(16;16) also known as core binding factor AML. This group is considered
favorable risk due to its sensitivity to standard treatment and overall better prognosis than
other forms of AML. It is unclear why patients that have these cytogenetic rearrangements and
FLT3-ITD would benefit more from chemotherapy with placebo than from chemotherapy with
160
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
quizartinib. Given the small number of events, this result might be driven by a few chance events
and not generalizable to a larger population of such patients.
In addition, FDA conducted the analyses of continuation (i.e., maintenance) treatment effect.
Of the 539 randomized patients, 208 patients entered the Continuation Phase (116 [44%] in the
quizartinib arm and 92 [34%] in the placebo arm). We define patients who entered the
Continuation Phase as the Continuation Analysis Set. In the Continuation Analysis Set, 66/116
(57%) on the quizartinib arm and 49/92 (53%) patients on the placebo arm were treated for >12
cycles of continuation, and 18/116 (16%) and 16/92 (17%) patients, respectively, completed the
planned 36 cycles of continuation therapy.
CDTL Comments: Of note, the 208/539 (39%) randomized patients who entered the
continuation/maintenance phase on QuANTUM-First was greater than the number who
entered maintenance on the RATIFY trial of midostaurin (29%).
As shown in Table 29, patients on the quizartinib arm who entered the Continuation Phase
were numerically younger and more likely to have WBC ≥ 40x109/L at baseline.

Time to initiation of continuation therapy was longer on the quizartinib arm than the control

patients in the quizartinib arm (70 [60%]) started continuation following HSCT (with or without

Below Sections A and B summarized the results of the FDA analyses of the continuation
therapy.
Section A: Unadjusted and adjusted analyses of efficacy in the continuation phase
Unadjusted Analyses
This section presents crude analyses of efficacy in the continuation phase. These analyses
condition on subgroups based on post-randomization events such as entering continuation and
receiving HSCT. For simplicity, outcomes are defined from randomization. In general, such
162
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
analyses are biased, since randomization is broken, and baseline covariates are no longer
guaranteed to be balanced. Furthermore, such analyses do not account for differences in time
from randomization to continuation or HSCT. Despite potential biases, these analyses are
presented to provide a simple overview of efficacy for such subgroups and to provide context
for subsequent analyses. Section B below presents analyses which adjust for covariate
imbalance and differences in time to continuation or HSCT.
Figure 4 and Figure 5 present Kaplan-Meier curves of OS and RFS in the 208 patients from the
Continuation Analysis Set. As of the data cutoff date (13 August 2021), a total of 51 patients
died (25 and 26 in the quizartinib and placebo arm, respectively); median OS was not reached
for either arm, and HR = 0.68 (95% CI: 0.4; 1.18) from unstratified Cox model in favor of
quizartinib (see Figure 4). The treatment effect appears to plateau around 12 months after
randomization.
Similar results are observed in the RFS analysis among 166 patients from the Continuation
Analysis Set who achieved CR at the end of Induction. 22 (23%) patients relapsed out of 94
quizartinib patients and 8 (9%) died; 24 (33%) patients relapsed out of 72 placebo patients and
5 (7%) died. Median RFS was only reached in the quizartinib arm at 48.6 months but not
reached in the placebo arm, with a HR = 0.74 (95% CI: 0.44; 1.23) from unstratified Cox model
in favor of quizartinib. The treatment effect appears to plateau around 12 months after
randomization (Figure 5), similar to OS.

In summary, in the exploratory subgroup analysis of the 89/208 (43%) of patients who received
maintenance therapy with VANFLYTA or placebo following consolidation chemotherapy without
HSCT, the OS HR was 0.40 (95% CI: 0.19, 0.84). Of 119/208 (57%) of patients who received
maintenance therapy with VANFLYTA or placebo following HSCT, the OS (from randomization)
HR was 1.62 (95% CI: 0.62, 4.22).
Adjusted Analyses
FDA conducted adjusted analyses to potentially account for variability in the timing of postrandomization events by setting the landmark time to the time of HSCT or the time of
continuation therapy. For patients who entered continuation, the hazard ratio for OS (from
start of continuation) was 0.69 (95% CI: 0.4, 1.20). This is similar to the hazard ratio obtained
for OS defined from time of randomization.
Figure 7 presents a comparison of patients who entered continuation by HSCT status (with vs.
without) from the start of continuation therapy. Figure 7 suggests that the survival benefit was
mainly observed in patients who entered the continuation phase without HSCT.

analyses (propensity score adjustment) as well as additional FDA analyses (landmark analyses
and time-varying Cox model analyses).
Section B: Impact of quizartinib on MRD status in the continuation phase
FDA sought to determine whether there was evidence of increased activity for quizartinib in the
setting of MRD positivity prior to continuation. The protocol specified that MRD was to be
captured at the following times: Screening; End of Induction; Consolidation Last Cycle; PostHSCT; Continuation Cycle 1, Day 1; Continuation Cycle 4, Day 1; and End of Treatment (i.e.,
completion of 36 cycles of continuation or permanent discontinuation).
Out of 368 CR + CRi responders, 321, 239 and 162 had at least one MRD assessment in
Induction, Consolidation, and Continuation, respectively. Out of 208 patients who entered
Continuation, 137 patients (74/116 [64%] quizartinib and 63/92 [69%] placebo) had MRD data
available at the initiation of this phase of treatment.
Based on the MRD cutoff of 0.01%, 13 patients (7 and 6 in the quizartinib and placebo arms,
respectively) were MRD positive and 124 patients (67 and 57 in the quizartinib and placebo
arms, respectively) were MRD negative at the initiation of Continuation. We define patients in

Continuation as the MRD+ (or MRD-) Continuation Analysis Set.

inference has limited value. Descriptive analyses are presented in Table 33, where the
conversion rate from MRD+ to MRD- during the Continuation Phase is summarized.

Among 124 patients in the MRD- Continuation Analysis Set, 70 (56%) and 54 (44%) started
continuation with and without HSCT respectively. The OS analyses in this subgroup are
generally similar to those in Continuation Analysis Set (see Appendix 19.5.3). Therefore, the
findings by HSCT status do not appear to vary by MRD status.
The ASH presentation by MJ Levis et al [Blood 140, no. Supplement 1 (2022): 546-548]
suggested that the effect of quizartinib may vary for patients who receive HSCT depending on
MRD status prior to HSCT. The analyses presented are of OS from time of HSCT by MRD status
(MRD+ vs. MRD-). The Kaplan-Meier curves are reproduced below in Figure 9, with the addition
of the categories of “Not in CR at HSCT” and “Unknown CR MRD at HSCT”.

Reviewer’s Comment: There is no discernible difference in the treatment effect across the
various subgroups in Figure 9 and Figure 10. While Figure 9 suggests a possible difference in the
treatment effect of quizartinib on OS for patients who receive HSCT by MRD status, this is not
clearly reflected in the subset of patients that received maintenance therapy. The small sample
size and post-hoc selection of such subgroups makes inference challenging. However, to the
extent that MRD status might be useful in identifying which patients might benefit from
maintenance quizartinib therapy, the available data are insufficient to make any such
recommendation. As noted below, the MRD data are further limited by the fact that a sizeable
percentage of the MRD data was either missing or >90 days prior to HSCT.
FDA evaluated overall survival results based on MRD status prior to HSCT for patients who
received maintenance (i.e., continuation) therapy. In this analysis, an MRD cutoff of 0.01% is
utilized. Table 34 presents the MRD status for such patients. For these analyses, FDA considered
patients with missing MRD status to be “unknown”; Levis 2022 considers such patients to be
MRD+. Other minor differences between the MRD definitions are noted inTable 34. Of note, the

MRD status in patients who receive both HSCT and maintenance therapy, however, we
reiterate that the interpretation should be taken with caution because of the small sample size
and post-hoc selection of such subgroups.

A post hoc analysis was performed to analyze the CIR from randomization in all subjects who
achieved a CR in the Induction Phase, treating death prior to relapse as a competing risk. Rates
of CIR were estimated using a nonparametric method and were summarized descriptively at
the following timepoints: 12, 24 and 36 months (Figure 18).

The Applicant’s Position:
The primary analysis for the secondary endpoint of EFS was based on a definition of ITF as not
achieving a CR within 42 days from the start of the last induction cycle per the FDA AML
Guidance (FDA, 2020) and was not statistically significant. Because the primary analysis of EFS
was not statistically significant, formal hierarchical testing was not continued and results of
other secondary efficacy endpoints are presented descriptively. Of note, in the original
protocol, EFS was based on ITF defined as a failure to achieve a CR or a CRi at the end of the
Induction Phase, using a window of up to 56 days from the start of the last induction cycle. At
the time of the update to the EFS definition, all subjects randomized in the study, including
those who were ongoing, had completed the Induction Phase.
A prespecified sensitivity analysis for EFS was performed using a definition of ITF as failure to
achieve CR by the end of induction (ie, within the protocol-specified 56 day window). This
analysis provides additional supportive evidence for the efficacy of quizartinib (see Table 37).

183
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Numerically higher CRc rates were evident in the quizartinib arm compared with the placebo
arm and were primarily driven by higher rates of CRi in the quizartinib arm than in the placebo
arm. Similar rates of CRc with FLT3-ITD MRD negativity at the end of induction were observed
between the treatment arms at a 1 × 10-4 cutoff. However, when MRD was assessed as
detectable versus not detectable (at a cutoff of 0), differences between quizartinib and placebo
were observed, suggesting the use of quizartinib in combination with chemotherapy leads to
deeper responses (Table 41).
While the rates of CR and CR with FLT3-ITD MRD negativity at the end of induction were similar
between treatment arms (see Table 41), both the median duration of CR and the median RFS
were substantially longer in subjects who did achieve a CR during induction on the quizartinib
arm when compared to the placebo arm, with a difference of 26.2 months and 25.7 months,
respectively. In a post hoc analysis, the CIR in subjects achieving a CR in the Induction Phase
also shows a higher percentage of subjects in the placebo arm experiencing relapse at
24 months and 36 months. Taken together, these data indicate that quizartinib delays relapse
in subjects who achieve a CR during the Induction Phase, which further supports the clinical
benefit of quizartinib in the claimed indication.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA analyses for EFS, the key secondary endpoint:
The primary analysis results of EFS are presented in Table 42 and Figure 19: Kaplan-Meier plot
of EFS on QuANTUM-First (ITT Analysis Set). Most EFS events are treatment failure, defined as
failure to achieve CR within 42 days of start of the last cycle of induction. There is a numerically
higher treatment failure rate in the quizartinib arm (51%) than the placebo arm (48%), causing
a bigger drop of the Kaplan-Meier curve in the quizartinib arm at Day 1, and therefore, a
crossing hazards pattern due to higher relapse rate in the placebo arm after Day 1. The
estimated HR is 0.92 (95% CI: 0.75, 1.11) in favor of quizartinib but the corresponding two-sided
p value of 0.2371 does not cross the efficacy boundary of 0.05, which is consistent with that
reported by the Applicant (Table 37).

FDA conducted sensitivity analyses of EFS as well. Below Table 43 shows the results of the EFS
sensitivity analyses, including Sensitivity analysis 1 where the 42-day window for achievement
of CR is removed and Sensitivity analysis 2 where treatment failure is defined as failure to
achieve CR or CRi by the end of induction without a 42-day window. In Sensitivity analysis 2, HR
estimations are lower because of greater treatment failure rate in the placebo versus
quizartinib arm. The corresponding Kaplan-Meier curves (not shown but similar to Figure 14
and Figure 15) do not feature the same crossing pattern as in the primary analysis (Figure 19).
The differences in EFS analyses are driven by longer times to response observed in the
quizartinib arm. The median time to CR was 28 days (range: 15, 105) and 23 days (range: 14, 91)
in the quizartinib and placebo arm, respectively. Among CRc responders, the median time to
CRc was 26 days (range: 12, 105) and 22 days (range: 14, 91), respectively.

Dose/Dose Response
Data:
The data leading to establishing the dose and dose regimen used in Study AC220-A-U302 are
summarized in Section 6.2.2.
The Applicant’s Position:
As with other receptor tyrosine kinase inhibitors, maintaining adequate target coverage is
important in achieving clinical efficacy. The dosing regimen was established by considering
available clinical efficacy and safety data from prior clinical studies, including ex vivo PD data.
The efficacy, safety, and tolerability data from Study AC220-A-U302 confirm that the dosing
regimen utilized represents an appropriate balance between safety and efficacy considerations.
The FDA’s Assessment:
See FDA’s assessment in Section 6.2.2 above.

The Applicant’s Position:
As discussed under the Section Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant endpoints, in

longer duration compared to placebo as measured by the median duration of CR. The
Kaplan-Meier plot of the duration of CR shows sustained separation of the curves for up to 3
years and no notable differences in the demographics and baseline disease characteristics of
the responders were observed between the 2 treatment arms.

See FDA’s Assessment under the section “Efficacy Results – Secondary and other relevant
endpoints” above.
Persistence of Effect
Data:
Overall, more subjects in the quizartinib arm than the placebo arm entered the Continuation
Phase (Table 21: Subject Disposition in Study AC220-A-U302) and approximately 30% of
subjects in the quizartinib arm were treated for more than 1 year (Table 70). Addition of
quizartinib to standard chemotherapy resulted in a 16.8-month prolongation of median OS and
the survival benefit was maintained over time as demonstrated by higher OS rates for
quizartinib compared to placebo at 12, 24, 36, and 48 months (Table 26). Additionally, there
was sustained separation in RFS curves (see Figure 16). The duration of CR was approximately
3 times longer in subjects treated with quizartinib compared to placebo with sustained
separation of the CR curves out to 3 years (see Figure 17), and lower CIR rates were seen in
subjects who achieved CR treated with quizartinib compared with those in the placebo arm (see
Figure 18).
The Applicant’s Position:
Quizartinib in combination with standard chemotherapy delays relapse and thereby results in
improved OS.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA was able to obtain similar results for the endpoints OS, DOR, and RFS to that presented
above under Data section provided by the Applicant. Note that the analyses of DOR and RFS
were exploratory without pre-specified statistical test(s).

In addition to the clinical endpoints, Study AC220-A-U302 was also designed to collect patientreported outcomes (PRO) as exploratory endpoints. Assessments were conducted using two
PRO scales: European Organization for Research and Treatment of Cancer QLQ-C30 (EORTC
QLQ-C30) and EuroQol 5 Dimension 5 Level (EQ-5D-5L). EORTC QLQ-C30 is a self-administered,
cancer-specific 30-item questionnaire and incorporates scales for global health status/quality of
life (QoL), functional status, symptoms, and perceived financial impact of the disease. EQ-5D-5L
is a generic and preference-weighted measure for capturing QoL on the day of the assessment.
Among the 539 subjects who were included in the ITT Analysis Set, 509 (94.4%) were included
in the PRO Analysis Set (N = 254 for quizartinib and N = 255 for placebo).

Descriptive analyses were conducted to describe the absolute scores and changes from PRO
baseline (Induction Cycle 1 Day 8) for each score on the EORTC QLQ-C30 and EQ-5D-5L scales.
A mixed-effects model for repeated measures (MMRM) analysis was performed to summarize
the longitudinal changes in each QoL score and systematic differences in changes between the
quizartinib and placebo arms. The MMRM analysis included change from PRO baseline as the
response variable and QoL score at PRO baseline, time/visit, and a treatment-by-time
interaction as covariates. A 10-point change in score was established as minimal clinically
important difference (MCID) for EORTC QLQ-C30 scales, which is defined as “the smallest
difference in score in the domain of interest which patients perceive as beneficial and which
would mandate, in the absence of troublesome side effects and excessive cost, a change in the
patient’s disease management” (Deschler, 2010; Dimopoulos, 2013; Roussel, 2020; ). A change
of 0.06 was used as the MCID for EQ-5D-5L index scores, and a 7-point MCID was used for visual
analog scale (VAS) (McClure, 2017).

The Applicant’s Position:
Study AC220-A-U302 is the first study to explore the impact of quizartinib on QoL for adult
patients with FLT3-ITD (+) AML. PRO data were collected for exploratory purposes only and the
study was not designed to formally compare the treatment impact of quizartinib on PRO
measures to that of placebo when combined with standard chemotherapy. A clinically
meaningful improvement in QoL over time was observed in both treatment arms, albeit this
observation may be confounded by potential bias introduced by attrition of subjects in later
timepoints. When comparing QoL measures between treatment arms, the differences between
them did not exceed the MCID of 10 points, suggesting no detrimental impact on QoL resulting
from the addition of quizartinib to standard-of-care chemotherapy for newly diagnosed
FLT3-ITD (+) AML.
195
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicants assessment that the PRO data above shows an improvement (>
10-point change) for both arms from the baseline measurement. However, we note that this is
not necessarily surprising considering that the baseline was assessed in patients recently
diagnosed with acute leukemia and after just having completed 7 days of intensive
chemotherapy. We also note that later timepoints of analysis occur only in patients that live
and remained on study to report their improvement from this QOL nadir at the time of the
baseline measurement.
We also note that during the intensive periods of treatment (induction and consolidation) the
QOL measures are almost identical between treatment arms. However, during the continuation
phase, there appeared to be some separation across the selected PRO endpoints which
numerically favors the placebo arm over the control arm. Of note, the interpretation should be
taken with caution.
Clinical reviewer comments: This result is perhaps not surprising since the beneficial effect of
quizartinib within this study design was to prevent relapse of disease. That is, the postulated
benefit of quizartinib is to persist on study and be alive at the cost of added toxicity. This leads
us to question the utility of QOL investigation in the setting of remission when the testing is
against a placebo. Since one cannot feel better due to the benefit of the drug in this design,
what in effect is being assessed is the toxicity burden of the continuation therapy against
placebo treatment. If the study was assessing the toxicity burden of two comparative
treatments in a continuation setting, QOL measure in addition to standard analysis of toxicity
could add to our understanding of overall tolerability.
Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial
Data:
Analysis of HSCT in Study AC220-A-U302
Per protocol in Study AC220-A-U302, subjects were permitted to undergo allo-HSCT after CR or
CRi was achieved. Allo-HSCT was to be performed after the Induction Phase, any time during
the Consolidation Phase, or, if certain criteria were met, within the first 3 months of the
Continuation Phase.

The Applicant’s Position:
The rates of HSCT were high in both treatment arms, which reflects the clinical practice for this
subpopulation of high-risk patients with FLT3-ITD (+) AML. Rates of HSCT overall and during the
first CR (CR1) were numerically higher with quizartinib than with placebo. Approximately 30%
of subjects in the quizartinib arm and 27% in the placebo arm were transplanted in CR1, which
is considered standard of care for FLT3-ITD (+) AML in many countries.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The timing of HSCT with respect to disease status was similar on both arms, with most
transplantations occurring as part of first line therapy (either during first CR or in patients who
did not achieve a CR). Despite the high frequency of HSCT on the study, the OS benefit in favor

a

The Applicant’s Position:
Although the study was not designed to assess the impact of NPM1 mutation status on
treatment outcomes, the results seen in the mutant NPM1 (NPM1mut) and WT NPM1 (NPM1wt)
subgroups are consistent with the EFS and CR/CRc analyses in the overall study population.
Consistent with the well-established role of NPM1wt as an adverse prognostic factor (Pollyea,
2021;;Döhner, 2017), better outcomes were observed in NPM1mut subjects compared to
NPM1wt subjects, including the longer median EFS based on the primary and 2 sensitivity EFS
analyses, as well as the higher rates of CR and CRc in NPM1mut subjects versus NPM1wt subjects.
No differences were seen when comparing the rates of CR or CRc for quizartinib and placebo in
both the NPM1mut and NPM1wt subgroups, which is consistent with the results seen in the
overall population. Similarly, when comparing the treatment effect of quizartinib versus
placebo based on EFS in both the NPM1mut and NPM1wt subgroups the results are consistent
with the trends in the overall population. When considering the protocol-defined EFS based on
ITF as not achieving CRc by the end of the Induction Phase, HRs of 0.760 in the NPM1wt
subgroup and 0.696 in the NPM1mut subgroup were observed, suggesting there may be a
199
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
benefit of quizartinib in both NPM1 subgroups. It is important to recognize the high unmet
medical need in the high-risk FLT3-ITD (+) NPM1wt population where treatment outcomes
associated with available therapies remain unsatisfactory.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment except a few numbers as follows:
In Table 48, Sensitivity analysis: ITF definition: Not achieving CRc by the end of the Induction
Phase, under NPM1 wild-type columns, 1) the lower boundary of 95% CI of median EFS for
quizartinib arm was 2.83, not 0.03; 2) the median EFS (95% CI) for placebo arm were 2.10 (1.64,
3.25), not 0.03 (0.03, 2.86) based on FDA analyses.
The effect of NPM1 mutation status on EFS and CR endpoints showed similar trends to those in
the OS analysis above. Specifically, that NPM1 mutated patients treated with quizartinib had
the longest median EFS, at least with the censoring rules used for the EFS sensitivity analysis.
This same group, NPM1 mutated quizartinib-treated, had the longest median OS estimate
above.
Clinical reviewer comment: Perhaps the most striking finding is derived from a look at the
control arm. Despite a large difference in CR rate for NPM1 mutant versus NPM1 Wt (69.3%
mutant vs 36.7% Wt), median OS was inferior in the placebo treated NPM1 mutated patient
than in NPM1 Wt (Median OS 15.1 months NPM1 mutated vs 17.3 months NPM1 Wt). As
mentioned above, this result agrees with the recent 2022 ELN risk groups that place all NPM1
mutant patients with FLT3-ITD in the intermediate risk group. This essentially means that within
the context of FLT3-ITD, NPM1 status does not improve long term outcomes. Lastly, it also
points to at least one genetic subtype of AML where there is a strong disconnect between initial
response rate and OS. A similar trend between response and OS has been reported in the
relapsed setting for NPM1 mutant AML.
Supportive Studies in Newly Diagnosed AML
The Applicant’s Position:
The three Phase 1 studies provide supplemental data for the safety and preliminary efficacy of
quizartinib in this indication: 2 studies in combination with chemotherapy in newly diagnosed
AML (2689-CL-0005 [N=18] and AC220-A-J102 [N=7]) and 1 study as maintenance monotherapy
in patients with AML after HSCT (2689-CL-0011 [N=13]). The efficacy results of these studies are
summarized below.
Study 2689-CL-0005 was a Phase 1, open-label, multiple-dose, dose-escalation study in subjects
with newly diagnosed AML (FLT3-ITD [+] or FLT3-ITD [-]) receiving standard induction and
200
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
consolidation chemotherapy. Induction (1 or 2 cycles) consisted of daunorubicin
(60 mg/m2/day) on Days 1, 2, and 3; cytarabine (200 mg/m2/day) on Days 1 to 7; and quizartinib
(40 or 60 mg/day) on Days 4 to 10 or 4 to 17. A total of 19 subjects were enrolled (18 treated
with quizartinib). Subjects were mostly white (78.9%), female (57.9%), with a median age of
43.0 years (between 22 and 60 years). Overall, 16 subjects (84%) achieved some degree of
response, and 9 subjects (47%) achieved CR (2689-CL-0005 clinical study report [CSR]).
Study AC220-A-J102 was a multicenter, open-label, Phase 1b clinical study of quizartinib
administered in combination with chemotherapy in Japanese subjects with newly diagnosed
AML. Subjects were enrolled in the study irrespective of FLT3 mutation status (FLT3-ITD [+] or
FLT3-ITD [-]). Quizartinib was administered at a dose of 20 mg/day at Level 1 and 40 mg/day at
Level 2. Dose escalation for a single subject was not allowed. The primary objectives of this
study were to evaluate the safety and PK of quizartinib when used with standard induction and
consolidation therapy in Japanese subjects with newly diagnosed AML, and to determine the
recommended dose of quizartinib for Japanese subjects in subsequent phases of development.
This study demonstrated that doses of 20 or 40 mg QD quizartinib in combination with standard
chemotherapy are generally well tolerated. A total of 7 subjects were enrolled. Subjects were
mostly female (57.1%), with a median age of 62 years (between 34 and 68 years). Overall, the
response rate was 85.7% (6 subjects), and 5 subjects (71.4%) achieved CRi (AC220-A-J102 CSR).
Study 2689-CL-0011 was a Phase 1 study that examined maintenance monotherapy with
quizartinib in subjects (aged ≥18 years) with AML in remission after receipt of an allo-HSCT.
Subject dose was assigned manually by using a modified rolling 6 design. Two dose levels were
tested, given daily in continuous 28-day cycles: dose level 1 at 40 mg and dose level 2 at 60 mg.
Dose limiting toxicities (DLTs) were assessed for the first 2 cycles, and subjects were allowed to
continue up to a maximum of 24 cycles. Thirteen subjects were enrolled, including 1 subject
who had previously received quizartinib as a part of Study 2689-CL-0005. All subjects had
received a human leukocyte antigen-matched allo-HSCT (4 related stem cell donors and 9 not
related stem cell donors), with a median of 56 days (range: 41 to 70 days) before study
enrollment. All subjects were FLT3-ITD (+) by local testing at the time of diagnosis. The majority
of subjects were male (53.8%), and all were white (100%). The median age was 43.0 years
(range: 23 to 61 years). Of the 13 total subjects, 10 (77%) received quizartinib for more than a
year and 6 subjects (46%) completed 24 cycles. No maximum tolerated dose (MTD) was
identified, but 60-mg QD dose was selected as the highest dose for continuous daily
administration based on randomized comparison of daily 30 and 60 mg doses in R/R AML. OS
ranged from approximately 13 to 142 weeks, with 9 subjects (69%) surviving ≥50 weeks and
4 subjects (31%) surviving >2 years (104 weeks) (2689-CL-0011 CSR).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. These small pilot studies showed that quizartinib
could be tolerated when combined with induction chemotherapy and as remission

maintenance monotherapy for extended periods of time. Due to the small size, heterogenous

Supportive Studies in Relapsed/Refractory AML
8.1.4.1. Pivotal Study in Relapsed/Refractory FLT3-ITD (+) AML – Study AC220007 (QuANTUM-R)
Trial Design
The Applicant’s Description:
Study AC220-007 was a global, randomized, open-label, active-controlled, Phase 3 study that
evaluated the efficacy and safety of quizartinib monotherapy compared with salvage
chemotherapy in subjects with FLT3-ITD (+) AML who were in first relapse within 6 months of
first remission or who were refractory after prior therapy, with or without prior HSCT. The
study design from the Screening Visit through the Treatment Period is shown in Figure 25.
Figure 25 – Study Design Schematic (Screening to Treatment Period) for Study AC220-007

Notes: Subjects could continue quizartinib or LoDAC until HSCT, determination by the investigator of no clinical
benefit, or unacceptable toxicity.
Subjects who received MEC or FLAG-IDA were to receive 1 cycle of therapy and could receive a second cycle of the same
therapy at the investigator’s discretion.
FLT3-ITD mutation status was assessed using a companion diagnostic within 14 days of randomization.

Trial location: This study was conducted at 114 sites across 18 countries in North America,
Europe, Australia, and Asia.
202
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Treatment assignment: 367 patients were enrolled and randomized in a 2:1 ratio using a
permuted block size of 6 to one of the 2 treatment arms: quizartinib arm (245 patients) or
salvage chemotherapy arm (122 patients). Randomization was stratified by prior therapy and
response and preselected salvage chemotherapy, without regard to investigative site. The
chemotherapy agent and regimen were preselected by investigators prior to randomization.
Blinding: This was an open-label study.
Study treatment: Patients were randomized to receive either quizartinib monotherapy (20- or
30-mg tablets administered orally QD) or salvage chemotherapy (administered subcutaneously
[low-dose cytarabine (LoDAC)] or IV [mitoxantrone, etoposide, and intermediate-dose
cytarabine (MEC) and fludarabine, cytarabine, and granulocyte-colony stimulating factor
(G-CSF) with idarubicin (FLAG-IDA)]).
For patients randomized to quizartinib, the starting dose was 30 mg/day, unless the patient was
receiving concurrent therapy with a strong CYP3A inhibitor, in which case the starting dose was
20 mg/day. For those not taking a strong CYP3A inhibitor, the dose of quizartinib was increased
from 30 to 60 mg/day starting on Day 16 (±1 day) if the subject’s average QTcF, based on
triplicate reading, was ≤450 ms on and before Day 15 (±1 day). For subjects taking a strong
CYP3A inhibitor, the dose of quizartinib was increased from 20 to 30 mg/day if they met the
above QTcF requirements.
The investigator preselected the specific salvage chemotherapy regimen before randomization.
All salvage chemotherapy was administered during 28-day cycles. The start of cycle 2 (MEC,
FLAG-IDA, or LoDAC) and subsequent cycles (LoDAC) could be delayed for up to 14 days to allow
for recovery from toxicity. Dose reductions were allowed for toxicity and were documented in
the source record. Patients receiving salvage chemotherapy, including those who underwent
HSCT, were followed on study and no crossover to investigational study drug was planned or
allowed.
Duration of treatment: Total duration of treatment with study drug was until HSCT, lack of
clinical benefit, or unacceptable toxicity.
Choice of control arm: At the time Study AC220-007 was implemented, guideline-recommended
chemotherapy regimens for R/R AML include intensive (‘aggressive’) cytotoxic regimens for
appropriate patients (‘fit patients’) or low-dose (‘less aggressive’) regimens for unfit patients.
Intensive combinations include cladribine, cytarabine, and G-CSF with or without mitoxantrone
or idarubicin; fludarabine, cytarabine, idarubicin, and G-CSF (FLAG) with or without idarubicin
(IDA); MEC; and clofarabine, cytarabine, and G-CSF. Therefore, salvage chemotherapy, which
may have included high-intensity chemotherapy (MEC or FLAG-IDA) or low-intensity
chemotherapy (LoDAC), was chosen as the active comparator for Study AC220-007.

Dose modification/dose discontinuation: Guidelines for dose modification and treatment

patients randomized to quizartinib in Study AC220-007 who underwent HSCT, quizartinib was
to be discontinued 7 days before initiation of a conditioning regimen. Following HSCT,
treatment with quizartinib was allowed by the protocol, provided that patients met
protocol-specified criteria that were intended to ensure recovery from the effects of HSCT and
were able to tolerate continued quizartinib administration.
Concomitant medications: The prohibited concomitant medications and those that were to be
avoided were the same as Study AC220-A-U302 (see Section 8.1.1).

(b) (4)
using the

Study assessments: For subjects receiving quizartinib, bone marrow assessments were
performed at screening and on Day 1 of Cycle 2 and Cycle 3. Bone marrow assessments were
also performed on Day 1 of each subsequent cycle unless the subject had already achieved CR,
complete remission with incomplete platelet recovery (CRp), or CRi. In that event, bone marrow
testing was repeated after every 3 subsequent cycles, unless there was evidence of relapse.
Bone marrow assessments were also performed at the Early Termination/End-of-treatment
Visit, and as clinically indicated. Following HSCT, bone marrow assessments were required on
Day 1 of post-HSCT Cycle 1, on Day 1 of Cycle 4 and every 3 cycles subsequently, at the Early
Termination/End-of-treatment Visit, and as clinically indicated.
For subjects receiving salvage chemotherapy, bone marrow assessments were required at
screening. For high-intensity chemotherapy (MEC or FLAG-IDA), bone marrow assessments
were also required on Day 29 (±14 days) of each cycle of therapy, and at the early
termination/End-of-treatment Visit, and as clinically indicated. For LoDAC, bone marrow
assessments were also required on Day 1 of Cycle 2 and Day 1 of Cycle 3. If a subject continued
to receive LoDAC past Cycle 2, bone marrow assessments were repeated on Day 1 of each
subsequent cycle unless the subject had already achieved a CR, CRp, or CRi. In that event, bone
marrow testing was repeated after every 3 subsequent cycles, unless there was evidence of
relapse. Also for LoDAC, bone marrow samples were required at the Early Termination/End-oftreatment Visit, and as clinically indicated.
Response to treatment was defined per Sponsor-modified Cheson criteria (Cheson, 2003). CR
was defined as <5% bone marrow blasts, no blasts with Auer rods, no persistence of

transfusion independence (defined as 4 weeks without RBC transfusions and 1 week without

platelet recovery (<100 × 109/L). CRi was defined as meeting all criteria for CR except with
incomplete hematological recovery with residual neutropenia <1 × 109/L and/or platelet count
<100 × 109/L. RBC and platelet transfusion independence was not required for CRi.
During study treatment (quizartinib or salvage chemotherapy), subjects were monitored
regularly for toxicities with ECGs, physical examinations, and laboratory tests (hematology,
chemistry and urinalysis) at the visits specified in the study protocol.
Quizartinib dose modifications were required if the subject experienced QTcF prolongation,
other nonhematologic toxicities, or myelosuppression, or were receiving concomitant strong
CYP3A inhibitors.

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
The Applicant’s Description:
The primary efficacy endpoint was OS, defined as the time between the date of randomization
and the date of death from any cause. The secondary efficacy endpoint was EFS, defined as the
time from randomization until documented refractory disease, relapse after CRc, or death from
any cause, whichever occurred first.
Exploratory efficacy endpoints included CRc rate, CR rate, duration of CRc, duration of CR, and
transplant rate.
•
CRc rate, which is the percentage of subjects achieving a best response of CR, CRp,
or CRi
•
CR rate, which is the percentage of subjects achieving a best response of CR
•
Duration of CRc and CR, which is the time from the first documented response
until the date of documented relapse
•
Transplant rate (bridge to transplant), which is the percentage of subjects
undergoing allo-HSCT directly following protocol-specified treatment with no
intervening AML therapy
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of the study endpoints and notes that the protocol
did not specifically define induction failure in the definition of EFS.
Statistical Analysis Plan and Amendments
The Applicant’s Description:
All efficacy analyses were performed according to treatment assigned at randomization. All
safety analyses were based on actual treatment received. The ITT Analysis Set included all
subjects who were randomized. The Safety Analysis Set included all subjects who received at
least 1 dose of the study drug. The Per-protocol Set (PPS) included all subjects in the ITT
Analysis Set who had no major protocol deviations that would affect assessment of efficacy
endpoints. Subjects who were randomized but did not receive any dose of study drug and those
who had negative FLT3-ITD test results based on central testing were excluded from the PPS.
The ITT Analysis Set was used for the analysis of all efficacy endpoints. Supporting analyses
were also conducted with the PPS. There were 2 planned cutoff dates for efficacy analyses: 1
interim analysis, when approximately 50% (140 of 280) of the expected number of events
(deaths) had occurred, and a final analysis at 280 OS events. EFS was to be analyzed only if the
OS result was positive. The actual interim analysis was performed when 153 (55%) OS events
were reported. The interim stopping boundary for OS was not crossed at that time, and the
data monitoring committee recommended for the study to continue without modification.
Calculation of sample size was based on comparison of OS, in the 2 treatment groups
(quizartinib and salvage chemotherapy) at a 2-sided significance level of 0.05, assuming that
207
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
median survival is 3.9 months in the salvage chemotherapy arm with an increase to 6 months,
in the quizartinib arm (HR 0.65). As of the defined data cutoff of 22 Feb 2018, there were
276 deaths to support the primary ITT analysis.
Analysis of OS and EFS were conducted for the prespecified subgroups including: age; sex; race;
geographic region; stratification factors from randomization (prior therapy and response and
preselected salvage chemotherapy); FLT3-ITD AR at randomization; AML history; prior
allo-HSCT; AML cytogenetic risk score; and baseline blast count.
Safety and tolerability were assessed by incidence, severity, and changes from baseline of all
relevant parameters including TEAEs, clinical laboratory values, vital signs, and ECG parameters.
Unless otherwise specified, all safety analyses were performed on the Safety Analysis Set and
grouped by actual treatment received. AEs to be included were restricted to TEAEs. Death,
other serious AEs (SAEs), and AEs associated with dose reduction, dose interruption, and
premature discontinuation of study drug were summarized by treatment arm. AEs of special
interest (AESIs) in this study were TdP/QT prolongation, hepatic disorders, cardiac failure,
cardiac arrhythmias; and sequelae of cytopenias, such as infections and bleeding.
The FDA’s Assessment:
We agree with the Applicant’s assessment of the SAP. The primary efficacy analyses were to be
performed on the intent-to-treat (ITT) population, consisting of all patients randomized. For OS,
patients were not censored at the time of subsequent non-protocol AML therapy, including
HSCT.
The study planned to accrue 363 patients and assumed a 10% drop out rate. With 280 deaths
and assuming a median OS of 3.9 months in the control arm and 6 months in the quizartinib
arm, the study had 90% power and a 2-sided significance level alpha of 0.05 to detect a hazard
ratio of 0.65 in OS. The primary analysis was to take place when 280 events occurred.
The primary efficacy endpoint OS was to be analyzed by the stratified log-rank test. The
stratification factors were to be those used for randomization. A non-stratified log-rank test
was to be used as supportive evidence of efficacy.
The key secondary endpoint EFS was to be analyzed by the stratified log-rank test with
covariates to control for response to first-line AML therapy and pre-selected salvage
chemotherapy in the ITT. To maintain the overall type 1 error rate at the 0.05 significance level,
hypothesis testing in the secondary analysis was to be performed only if the null hypothesis in
the primary analysis was rejected at the overall two-sided 0.05 significance level.
Sensitivity analyses for OS were to include an analysis where patients were censored at the
date of HSCT, and an analysis of OS on the PPS, of all patients randomized and without major
protocol deviations.
208
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Sensitivity analyses for EFS were to include an analysis on the PPS.
One interim analysis was planned at 140 events with an O’Brian-Fleming boundary for superior
efficacy and a conditional power (CP) of 10% for futility. The Lan-DeMets spending function was
to be employed to control the overall Type I error of 1-sided 0.025 for the primary efficacy
analysis of OS such that the nominal Type I error was to be 0.001525 at interim analysis and
0.0245 at the final analysis. For the secondary endpoint EFS, the analysis plan employed a
hierarchical (“gatekeeping”) testing procedure to ensure a study-wise1-sided Type 1 error rate
of 0.025, such that EFS would be analyzed using the same boundaries and nominal Type 1 error
for the primary analysis at the interim or final analysis.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The first patient was randomized to the study on 5/7/2014, after the first Amendment of the
study was dated 12/24/2013. The data cut-off for efficacy for this NDA was 12/21/2020.
FDA notes that Amendment dated 6/3/2015 clarified timing for quizartinib, changed the dose
escalation schedule and requirement of ECG monitoring post dose escalation, changed
response assessment timing to allow for count recovery, and revised inclusion criteria to
include patients in first relapse with DOR <6 months, including 6 months post-HSCT, and added
exclusion criteria for bradycardia due to QTc prolongation. Revisions to the eligibility criteria in
Amendment 2 might have contributed to some inconsistencies in prior therapies/relapse data.
Amendment 5 dated 9/2/2016 increased the planned number of subjects to 363 to reach
planned number of events (280) in a “reasonable time,” removed references to adaptive design
that would allow for increase of sample size at the time of the interim analysis, added efficacy
objectives/variables (CR, CRc, duration of CR and CRc), revised dose modifications for patients
with QTcF prolongation if already at a reduced dose, added language to help minimize the
number of subjects without survival information, revised censoring analysis for HSCT for the
sensitivity analysis of OS, and added subgroup analyses for primary and secondary endpoints,
and safety analyses. The revision in Amendment 5 with regard to follow-up raises the possibility
that the potential for a high rate of early censoring was foreseen already then.

Sources: AC220-007 CSR Table 14.1.1.1, Table 14.1.1.2, Table 14.1.4.1, Table 14.2.1.6a, Table 14.2.4.1b, Table 14.
2.4.1c, Listing 16.2.1.2, Listing 16.2.1.3, and Listing 16.2.6.1.1

Most subjects randomized to the salvage chemotherapy arm were preselected by the
investigator to receive intensive chemotherapy (FLAG-IDA regimen or MEC).
Of the subjects randomized, 241 and 94 subjects received treatment with quizartinib or salvage
chemotherapy, respectively. As a result there was an imbalance in the randomized not treated
subjects with a larger number occurring in the salvage chemotherapy arm compared with the
quizartinib arm (1.6% vs 23%, respectively).
A total of 93 subjects had an HSCT immediately following conclusion of study drug without
intervening AML therapy. A higher proportion of subjects in the quizartinib arm received HSCTs
(79 [32.2%]) than in the salvage chemotherapy arm (14 [11.5%]). At the time of the data cutoff,
21 subjects continued on quizartinib therapy: 6 subjects (2.4%) continued initial quizartinib
without intervening HSCT and 15 (6.1%) continued on quizartinib after HSCT. All subjects in the
salvage chemotherapy arm discontinued study drug and 16 (13.1%) remained in the study at
the time of data cutoff.
Differences between the treatment arms in the reasons for study drug discontinuation were
consistent with the treatment regimens and the longer duration of study drug for subjects on
the quizartinib arm compared to the salvage chemotherapy arm. Progressive disease was the
most common reason for study drug discontinuation in the salvage chemotherapy arm (40.2%)
and was more frequent than in the quizartinib arm (19.2%). The most common reason for study
drug discontinuation in the quizartinib arm (initial quizartinib treatment) was HSCT (32.2%).
Of the 79 subjects randomized to quizartinib who received HSCT immediately following
conclusion of study drug without intervening AML therapy, 49 restarted quizartinib after HSCT.
Of these 49 subjects, 34 subsequently discontinued quizartinib. The primary reasons for
discontinuing quizartinib post-HSCT were relapse (17 subjects) or AEs (10 subjects).
The FDA’s Assessment:
For the purposes of tabulation of early discontinuations in Table 51, the term “lack of efficacy”
included events including relapse, progression, lack of efficacy, or an AE with a PT related to the
underlying disease or relapse.

Notably, there was an imbalance in the number of patients randomized but not treated,
consisting of only 4 patients (2%) on the quizartinib arm compared to 28 patients (23%) on the
salvage arm. Further, while all 4 patients on the quizartinib continued to be followed on study,
and reason for discontinuation was death, of the 28 patients in the salvage arm, 17 withdrew
from the study, including 15 in whom this happened within a week of randomization, 1 in which
this happened within 10 days, and one in which this happened on day 36. With the exception of
2 patients for whom a death date was available, the remaining 15 patients were censored on
the day of study discontinuation with the reason “no survival follow-up within 3 months.”
Reviewer comment: The imbalance noted above, both in terms of the patients randomized and
not treated and to the early censoring for the primary endpoint that occurred almost exclusively
on the control arm, introduces potential bias that raises concerns regarding the credibility of the
study results. It is possible that the OS for the 15 patients censored early would improve the
results for the salvage arm to an extent that the difference between the arms, which is quite
small (6.5 weeks), would be rendered statistically insignificant. FDA also noted an imbalance in
early censoring between treatment arms with 9 patients on the control arm censored prior to

The FDA’s Assessment:
Demographic information for the patients on Study AC220-007 are summarized in Table 52. The
median age was slightly younger in the quizartinib arm, but the range was wider, including a
patient who was 81 years old. The majority of the patients were females on the quizartinib arm
and males on the control arm. Approximately one-third of the patients on each arm were
treated in the US. The slightly higher percentage of patients on the salvage arm with an ECOG
PS of 2 may bias the study results in favor of the quizartinib arm.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The higher rate of unknown cytogenetics for the salvage arm raises concerns regarding an
imbalance in expected outcomes between the arms. The classification system used for
cytogenetic risk relatively old and does not utilize the more modern ELN risk stratification that
incorporates more abnormalities, such that the balance in cytogenetic risk may not be accurate.
Since the randomization was stratified by pre-selected chemotherapy (high vs low, investigator
decision), the rates of each category were balanced between the arms.

As expected, the median age in the low intensity stratum was higher than in those stratified to
high intensity therapy, as was the rate of patients over 65 in that strata (50% of patients in each
arm among those pre-selected for low intensity therapy compared to approximately 20% for
those pre-selected for high intensity therapy). The striking imbalance between patients with an
217
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
ECOG PS of 2 between the arms in the low intensity stratify highlights the difference in the ITT
population, but this is the stratum more affected by the imbalance, and may bias the study
results in favor of the quizartinib treated arm. The fact that 23% of patients in the low intensity
stratum had prior HSCT while none in the control arm in this stratum is also concerning for
imbalance, but this can be in both directions: if it is due to the fact that the patients disease was
responsive to therapy and thus allowed for achievement of CR that allowed for transplant,
while patients in the salvage arm had more resistant disease, this would bias the results in favor
of quizartinib; in contrast, the morbidities due to prior transplant (that might be the reason for
pre-selection to this stratum by the investigators) may increase the risk of toxicities in the
quizartinib arm and bias the results in favor of the control arm. Since these cannot be
determined, uncertainty remains between this imbalance, and must be taken into account
upon interpretation of the study results. The imbalance in cytogenetic risk in the low risk
stratum (specifically unfavorable risk) also biases the results in favor of the quizartinib arm;
however, since the cytogenetic risk stratification system used for definitions of these categories
is fairly outdated, the impact on the study results is uncertain.
In an attempt to analyze the impact of the highly biased rate of untreated patients on the
salvage arm (23%) as compared to the quizartinib arm (2%), FDA performed an analysis of
demographic characteristics of the untreated patients for comparison to the ITT population,
summarized in Table 55 below:

The Applicant’s Position:
In Study AC220-007, treatment with quizartinib monotherapy resulted in a statistically
significant and clinically meaningful improvement in OS compared with salvage chemotherapy
(1-sided P = 0.0177 by stratified log-rank test) in a population of subjects with R/R FLT3-ITD (+)
AML. The improvement in OS was supported by 3 preplanned and several post hoc sensitivity
analyses, all of which showed treatment effects that were consistent with that of the overall ITT
analysis. Despite questions raised by the FDA over the robustness of the OS data, the results
nevertheless are supportive of clinical benefit of quizartinib monotherapy in FLT3-ITD (+) AML
patients. The improvement in OS was also observed across most prespecified subgroups in
favor of quizartinib, which is supportive of efficacy in the overall population enrolled in
Study AC220-007.
The FDA’s Assessment:
As mentioned above (disposition) the high rate of early censoring, highly imbalanced between
the arms, and the relatively small difference between the median OS in each arm, raised
concerns regarding the credibility of the study results for the primary endpoint. If the OS of the
ITT population on the salvage arm was even slightly better than the results with this censoring,
the HR and p-value might not hold up. In addition, the 6.5-week survival advantage may not be
clinically meaningful given safety concerns. Thus, the benefits were previously determined by
223
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
FDA to not outweigh the risks as monotherapy in patients with R/R FLT3-ITD mutated AML.
However, the OS results for quizartinib in comparison to salvage chemotherapy provide some
support for the clinical activity of quizartinib in patients with FLT3-ITD mutated AML.
See also the FDA Briefing Document, 2019 for comprehensive analyses conducted by the review
team at the time of the initial NDA review for quizartinib.
Data Quality and Integrity
Data:
There were 28 subjects whose last survival assessment was more than 3 months prior to the
cutoff date for the study-wide, final analysis data collection. These included the subjects who
were censored at less than 8 weeks (n = 18), plus an additional 10 subjects. At the FDA’s
request, the Applicant conducted a targeted and limited public record sweep and found data on
13 of these subjects. An updated ITT analysis including these subjects was consistent with the
original ITT analysis. The median OS was 26.9 (95% CI 23.1-31.0) weeks for the quizartinib arm
compared with 20.4 (95% CI 17.0-25.3) weeks for the salvage chemotherapy arm (HR 0.77
[95% CI 0.60, 0.99]; P = 0.0175).
The Applicant’s Position:
The updated ITT analysis was consistent with the original ITT analysis for OS for AC220-007 and
supportive of use of quizartinib in combination with standard chemotherapy in the treatment
of patients with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Based on the updated data received, there were still 6 patients, all on the control arm, for
whom censoring occurred within 16 days of randomization without any further information. An
additional patient, on the quizartinib arm, was censored at exactly 6 weeks, 3 other patients on
the control arm were censored between 6 and 8 weeks, and 1 patient on the control arm was
censored at exactly 8 weeks. When looking at all censored patients for whom “no survival
follow-up” is the reason for censoring (beyond 8 weeks), 4 patients remain, 2 on each arm.
While the updated data was somewhat helpful in decreasing the amount of uncertainty with
regard to the outcome for the primary endpoint, the imbalance in early censoring (9 patients
on the control arm and 1 patient on the quizartinib arm censored before 8 weeks, with the all
censoring within the first 16 days being on the control arm) left residual uncertainty with regard
to the credibility of the study results.

The Applicant’s Position:
The secondary endpoint of EFS showed quizartinib was not statistically significantly different
from salvage chemotherapy but did demonstrate a trend in favor of the quizartinib arm. A
number of exploratory endpoints including CR rate, CRc rate, duration of CRc, and transplant
rate numerically favored the quizartinib arm.
The FDA’s Assessment:
One of the exploratory endpoints of the trial was CR rate, and duration of CR as well as
composite complete remission (CRc) rate, as defined above. FDA considers durable CR a clinical
benefit outcome in patients with relapsed/refractory AML (Jen, 2018); durable CRh (defined as
227
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
<5% bone marrow blasts by morphological examination with an ANC of >0.5 Gi/L and a platelet
count of >50Gi/L, that do not meet CR requirements, in the absence of peripheral blasts or
extramedullary disease) may be considered a clinical benefit endpoint in a population of
patients being treated without curative intent, using agents that are relatively non-toxic and
not myelosuppressive; even if achieved, it may require supporting data for transfusion
independence in order to be considered a direct clinical benefit. However, CRi is not considered
an endpoint equivalent to CR for regulatory decision making.
In the FDA analysis of remission rates on Study 007, FDA adjudicated each CR or CRh based on
the datasets including bone marrow blast percentage and accompanying ANC and platelet
counts. Since per the protocol and SAP, the CR requirements above had to be met in the
absence of a RBC transfusion in the prior 4 weeks and platelet transfusion in the prior 1 week,
FDA performed the per protocol analysis, but also performed a sensitivity analysis where CRs
and CRhs were adjudicated soley on the basis of the bone marrow aspirate, CBC and
extramedullary disease results described above. The protocol did not specify the timeframe
within which the CBC results for determination of CR or CRh had to be collected in relation to
the timing of the bone marrow assessment; for FDA adjudication of these outcomes, a 7-day
window was used; a sensitivity analysis allowing for a window of 14 days was formed by FDA as
well. The results of the applicant’s analysis, as well as that of the two FDA analyses, are
summarized in below; the specific patients for whom the FDA adjudicated CR or CRh rates
differed from those assessed by the applicant in the sensitivity analyses are listed in the text
below the table.
Table 61: CR and CRh rates, Study AC220-007

To look for overall efficacy signals, FDA performed a final sensitivity analysis in which all
patients with <5% blasts in their BM were considered CR if they met the peripheral count
recovery criteria at any point (even beyond 14 days) after the BM assessment, and CRh if they
met those criteria at any point. Using these criteria, there was no change in the results for the
quizartinib arm (14 CRs and 13 CRhs), but for the control arm, there were an additional 2 CRs
for a total of 6 CRs (5%) and 2 CRhs (2%), for a CR+CRh rate of 7% (8/122). The 4 additional

The results of the CR and CRh analyses above raised concerns regarding the benefit of
quizartinib in the population proposed in the initial NDA review. Since CR, and CR+ CRh in
certain clinical settings, are endpoints representative of clinical benefit that have been used as
the basis of approval of agents for the treatment of AML, a finding of high CR and/or CR+CRh
rates in the quizartinib arm on Study AC220-007 would have supported the OS findings above
and served as further evidence of efficacy in this population; conversely, the low CR and CRh
rates described above raised further doubt regarding the results of the primary endpoint, as
they were not internally consistent or robust. However, considering the results of QuANTUMFirst described above, the rate of CR+CRh responses with quizartinib is reflective of measurable
monotherapy activity of quizartinib in patients with FLT3-ITD mutated AML.
When CR/CRh rates have been used as the basis for marketing approval in the treatment of
AML (e.g. ivosidenib, enasidenib, gilteritinib), supportive evidence for transfusion
independence (TI) is necessary to strengthen the evidence of clinical benefit that CR/CRh
represents (i.e., the recovery of counts to the point where transfusions are not necessary). On
Study AC220-007, FDA performed an analysis of patients on the quizartinib arm who were
transfusion dependent (TD) at study entry and achieved a 56-day window of transfusion
independence at some point post-baseline. The denominator included 99 patients on the
quizartinib arm who had documented transfusions through study day -1. In this analysis, 26 of
these patients (26%; 95% CI: 19, 36) achieved TI at some point on treatment. In the context of
the original NDA review, in the absence of evidence of substantial effectiveness of the drug on
the underlying disease (i.e., CR and/or CR/CRh), TI alone could not be used as the sole evidence
of benefit for quizartinib in patients with R/R FLT3-ITD mutated AML. However, we consider the
data on TI from AC220-007 to be supportive of some monotherapy activity of quizartinib.

The FDA’s Assessment:
On Study AC220-007, patients on the quizartinib arm were treated with different starting doses
depending on whether they were on concomitant CYP3A4 inhibitor (20 mg) or not (30 mg), and
dose escalation on day 16 was dependent on this factor (to 30 mg or 60 mg) as well as the QTc
value on day 15, such that a dose- and exposure- response analysis would be appropriate.
However, given the issues with regard to reliability of the study results as pertaining to the
primary endpoint, interpretability of these analyses are subject to the same limitations as those
described above with regard to interpretation of the overall study results, and are not further
described here.
Due to the small numbers of CR/CRhs on Study AC220-007, response cannot be used as a
clinical benefit endpoint that would allow for these analyses by dose either.

Supportive Studies in Relapsed/Refractory AML – Study AC220-002
Study AC220-002 was an open-label, Phase 2 study of the efficacy and safety of quizartinib
monotherapy in subjects with R/R AML with and without FLT3-ITD activating mutations. The
study was divided into 2 stages. The exploratory stage included the first 60 FLT-ITD (+) subjects
to complete the first 28-day treatment cycle, and the confirmatory stage included all remaining
subjects.
In each stage, subjects were divided into 2 cohorts (approximately 120 FLT3-ITD (+) and
30 FLT3-ITD (-) subjects each) as follows:
•
Cohort 1: Subjects ≥60 years of age who relapsed after 1 first-line chemotherapy
regimen (with or without consolidation) in <12 months after CR1 or who were
primary refractory to first-line chemotherapy
•
Cohort 2: Subjects ≥18 years of age (including subjects ≥60 years of age) who were
relapsed or were refractory after 1 second-line (salvage) regimen or who were
relapsed or were refractory after HSCT
Quizartinib was administered QD as an oral solution continuously in 28-day cycles with no
breaks between cycles. Initially, the dose of quizartinib was 200 mg/day. The dose was
subsequently reduced to 135 mg/day (for males) or 90 mg/day (for females), because of
occurrences of QTcF prolongation with the 200 mg/day dose. Subjects received up to 3 cycles
of induction with quizartinib to achieve best response, and responders continued receiving
quizartinib as consolidation and maintenance therapy.
231
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Data:
Data from the exploratory and confirmatory stages were combined for analysis (AC220-002
CSR).
A total of 333 subjects were enrolled: 157 in Cohort 1 and 176 in Cohort 2. Subjects were
predominantly white (87.4%) and almost equally distributed between males (51.1%) and
females (48.9%), with a median age of 63.0 years (range: 19 to 86 years). The majority (248) of
subjects were FLT3-ITD (+), 84 subjects were FLT3-ITD (-), and 1 subject had unknown or
indeterminate FLT3-ITD status.
A total of 125 FLT3-ITD (+) subjects (50.4%) achieved a CRc, including 63 subjects (56.3%) in
Cohort 1 and 62 subjects (45.6%) in Cohort 2. In FLT3-ITD (+) subjects, the median duration of
CRc was 12.1 weeks for Cohort 1 and 10.6 weeks for Cohort 2. Twenty-eight FLT3-ITD (-)
subjects (33.3%) achieved a CRc, including 16 subjects (36.4%) in Cohort 1 and 12 subjects
(30.0%) in Cohort 2. Subjects responded early in their treatment with quizartinib, with FLT3-ITD
(+) subjects achieving a CRc in approximately 4 weeks. For FLT3-ITD (-) subjects, the time to CRc
was nearly double for Cohort 1 compared with Cohort 2 (8.1 versus 4.1 weeks, respectively).
The ability to control disease and induce remissions enabled a substantial number of subjects to
undergo allo-HSCT, particularly for the younger age group in Cohort 2. Of the 112 FLT3-ITD (+)
subjects in Cohort 1, 11 subjects had HSCT and 101 subjects had no HSCT after quizartinib. Of
the 136 FLT3-ITD (+) subjects in Cohort 2, 47 subjects were able to go on to HSCT and
89 subjects did not go onto HSCT after quizartinib.
In Study AC220-002, a threshold VAF >10% was used to determine FLT3-ITD positivity. Since
the CRc rates were similar in subjects with FLT3-ITD VAF ≤10% (CRc range of 36% to 58%) and
those with VAF >10% (CRc range of 38% to 72%), while subjects with no detectable ITD
mutation had lower response rates, a lower cutoff of ≤3% was chosen as the threshold for later
studies (AC220-007 and AC220-A-U302).
Median OS for FLT3-ITD (+) subjects was approximately 25 weeks for both cohorts. For the
FLT3-ITD (-) subjects, the median OS was 19.1 weeks in Cohort 1 and 25.1 weeks in Cohort 2.
The median OS was 34.1 weeks (95% CI: 26.0, 49.0) for FLT3-ITD (+) subjects who underwent
HSCT and 18.4 weeks (95% CI: 15.0, 22.7) for subjects who did not undergo HSCT in Cohort 2.
Supplemental Data from Other Phase 1 and Phase 2 Clinical Studies in
Relapsed/Refractory AML
Study CP0001 was a Phase 1, first-in-human, open-label, 3+3 dose-escalation study of
quizartinib in subjects with R/R AML or previously untreated AML who were unsuitable for
induction chemotherapy because of age, comorbidities, or other factors. Quizartinib oral
solution was initially administered at a starting dose of 12 mg QD for 14 days followed by a

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
14-day rest period (1 cycle). Subsequently, the protocol was amended to specify a continuous
dosing (CD) schedule starting at 200 mg/day for 28 days (1 cycle). Nine dose escalations (from
12 to 450 mg) were achieved by using the 14-day regimen with 3 to 8 subjects per dose level.
Two CD regimens were tested with 17 subjects on 200 mg/day and 8 subjects on 300 mg/day. A
total of 76 subjects received treatment in the study. Subjects were mostly white (89.5%) and
male (60.5%) with a median age of 59.5 years (range: 23 years to 86 years). Seventeen subjects
(22.3%) were FLT3-ITD (+), 37 (48.7%) were FLT3-ITD (-) based on a 1% ITD to WT ratio, and
22 (28.9%) had unknown or indeterminate FLT3-ITD status. The overall response rate in the ITT
Analysis Set, FLT3-ITD (+), FLT3-ITD (-), unknown or indeterminate FLT3-ITD status was 30.3%,
52.9%, 13.5% and 40.9%, respectively (CP0001 CSR).
Study AC220-A-J101 was a multicenter, open-label, Phase 1 study that assessed the safety and
PKs of quizartinib as multiple-dose monotherapy in Japanese subjects with R/R AML. Subjects
were enrolled in the study irrespective of FLT3 mutation status (FLT3-ITD [+] or FLT3-WT).
Seventeen subjects were registered, of which 16 subjects were treated. The median age of
enrolled subjects was 68 years (range: 33 to 91 years), with an almost equal proportion of
males and females. Of the 17 enrolled subjects, 16 were included in the Safety Analysis Set, the
Efficacy Analysis Set, and the PK/PD Analysis Sets. The subjects received a dose of 20 mg/day
quizartinib for the first cohort, with doses selected from among 20, 30, and 60 mg for the
second and subsequent cohorts. The efficacy evaluation showed that the overall response rate
was 56.3% (9 of 16 subjects), including 5 subjects (55.6%) in the 20-mg cohort, 2 subjects
(66.7%) in the 30-mg cohort, and 2 subjects (50.0%) in the 60-mg cohort. Although no CR was
reported in any subject, CRc was found in 6 subjects (37.5%) in total, 2 subjects (22.2%) in the
20-mg cohort, 2 subjects (66.7%) in the 30-mg cohort, and 2 subjects (50.0%) in the 60-mg
cohort. Partial remission was observed in 3 subjects (18.8%) overall, all of whom in the 20-mg
cohort (3 subjects [33.3%]). CRi was observed in 5 subjects (31.3%) overall, including 2 subjects
(22.2%) in the 20-mg cohort, 1 subject (33.3%) in the 30 mg cohort, and 2 subjects (50.0%) in
the 60-mg cohort. CRc was reported in 5 of 7 FLT3-ITD (+) subjects (71.4%) overall, including
1 subject (33.3%) in the 20-mg cohort, 2 subjects (100.0%) in the 30-mg cohort, and 2 subjects
(100.0%) in the 60-mg cohort (AC220-A-J101 CSR).
Study AC220-A-J201 was a multicenter, open-label, single-arm, Phase 2 clinical study to assess
the efficacy and safety of quizartinib monotherapy in Japanese subjects with FLT3-ITD (+) AML
who have relapsed or were found to be refractory within 6 months after initial remission
induction therapy (without consideration of whether they had received HSCT or not).
The initial dose of quizartinib was 30 mg/day. For subjects receiving strong CYP3A inhibitors,
the initial dose was 20 mg/day. Subsequently, whether or not to increase the initial dose of
30 to 60 mg/day or 20 to 30 mg/day was determined on Cycle 1 Day 16 and Cycle 2 Day 1 or
thereafter in accordance with criteria for dose increase as per protocol. For subjects who
achieved CRc after the start of treatment of quizartinib, subsequently relapsed, and met the
criteria for dose increase, the dose of quizartinib was increased in the same manner.

The dose of the study drug was reduced in accordance with each of the criteria for dose
reduction as per protocol. A total of 37 subjects were enrolled by the CSR data cutoff date
(26 Feb 2018) and were included in the analysis sets. All 37 subjects were included in the Safety
Analysis Set, and 32 subjects were included in the Efficacy Analysis Set. Subjects were mostly
female (59.5%), with a median age of 65 years (range: 31 to 81 years). Overall, the CRc rate was
53.8% (14 subjects; 90% CI: 36.2, 70.8). Although no CR was reported in any subject, CRi was
achieved as a best response in 13 subjects (48.1%). CRp was achieved in 1 subject (3.7%). The
response rate was 77.8% (21 subjects; 95% CI: 57.7%, 91.4%) (AC220-A-J201 CSR).
Study 2689-CL-2004 was an open-label, randomized, multiple-dose, Phase 2 study in subjects
with FLT3-ITD (+) AML that have relapsed or were refractory after 1 salvage regimen or HSCT.
Subjects were randomly assigned to 28-day cycles of either 30 or 60 mg/day quizartinib.
Seventy-six subjects were randomized, of which 74 subjects received treatment with
quizartinib. Subjects were mostly white (77.6%) males (57.9%) and had a median age of
54.5 years (range: 19 to 77 years). The majority of subjects (90.8%) were FLT3-ITD (+). In total,
36 of the 76 randomized subjects (47.4%) achieved a best response of CRc until end of
treatment. Eighteen subjects in each dose group (47.4%) achieved CRc. Median OS was
20.9 weeks and 27.3 weeks with 1 and 5 long-term (≥52 weeks [ie, ≥365 days]) survivors for the
30 and 60-mg treatment arms, respectively (2689-CL-2004 CSR).

and the possibility that QuANTUM-R represents an under-estimation of the OS seen with this
treatment is less likely.
