As mentioned above with regard to Study AC220-002, the low rate of CRs in this study is
consistent with the findings in QuANTUM-R, and supports the concerns raised above regarding
the lack of robustness and internal consistency of the efficacy findings on QuANTUM-R. It is
clear, however, across the monotherapy studies that quizartinib does have an anti-leukemic
effect on the blast cells - hence, the CRc responses. While the mostly CRi component responses,
in and of themselves, do not constitute clinical benefit, they serve as evidence of quizartinib’s
monotherapy antileukemic activity.
For Study 2689-CL-2004, there was no active control or placebo, and cross-trial comparisons
between QuANTUM-R and study 2689-CL-2004 are necessarily exploratory in nature with
regard to time to event endpoints such as OS. However, as noted above for trial AC220-002, OS
for both cohorts were similar to the outcomes for the quizartinib arm on QuANTUM-R, such
that the marginal 6.5 week OS advantage on that trial (if credible) appears consistent with
other trials using quizartinib, and the possibility that QuANTUM-R represents an underestimation of the OS seen with this treatment is less likely.

The Applicant’s Position:
The primary efficacy data supporting this application are the statistically significant and
clinically meaningful results from the pivotal study, AC220-A-U302 (QuANTUM-First), which
demonstrate that quizartinib added to chemotherapy improves OS of subjects with newly
diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML and supports the conclusion that quizartinib compared with
placebo in combination with standard induction and consolidation chemotherapy and then as
continuation monotherapy for up to 36 cycles is an effective treatment in this patient
population.
The results from Study AC220-A-U302 are supported by early Phase 1 studies in newly
diagnosed AML, where quizartinib was added to frontline standard chemotherapy,
demonstrating promising clinical activity in a limited number of subjects with FLT3-ITD (+) AML.
Evidence of quizartinib monotherapy activity is provided by the randomized, active-controlled,
pivotal Phase 3 study, AC220-007, in which a 24% reduction in the risk of death during the
observation period compared to salvage chemotherapy in subjects with R/R FLT3-ITD (+) AML,
with additional Phase 2 studies providing further supportive evidence of consistent clinical
activity of quizartinib in FLT3-ITD (+) AML. Median OS estimates were consistent across the
studies, with a median OS of approximately 27 weeks in subjects treated with quizartinib
60 mg. The results were also consistent across studies with respect to rates of CRc and CR and
the duration of CRc.
Quizartinib consistently demonstrated a clinical benefit across the 10 clinical studies in AML,
including 2 randomized controlled studies, both in combination with standard chemotherapy
regimens and as a single agent, and support the conclusion that quizartinib provides a clinically
meaningful improvement for patients with FLT3-ITD (+) AML, a fatal disease with poor
prognosis and continued high unmet medical need.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Methods
Issues with clinical development program
Prior to Study AC220-A-U302 (QuANTUM-First), the Applicant investigated the safety and
effectiveness of quizartinib in R/R AML in Study AC220-007 (QuANTUM-R). This study was
discussed at ODAC due to trial conduct and safety issues. A major limitation of this trial was
that it was open-label. There were notable imbalances between the arms, calling into question
the validity of the OS results. Furthermore, QT prolongation was identified as a significant
safety risk.
Issues with the pivotal (QuANTUM-First) trial design
242
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
The FDA defines maintenance therapy for AML as an extended but time-limited course of
treatment given after achievement of CR with the objective of reducing the risk of relapse
beyond the period of treatment (Guidance for Industry: Acute Myeloid Leukemia, 2022).
Presently, there are no drugs approved as maintenance therapies for AML. While Onureg was
approved for continued treatment of patients with AML who achieve CR or CRi, this is not
considered a maintenance indication (Jen 2022). Levis et al (2019) reviewed post-HSCT trials of
FLT3 inhibitors, noting that no trial has established the necessity of maintenance therapy in this
setting. QuANTUM-First did not re-randomize patients to assess the effect of maintenance
therapy. Consequently, the evidence for quizartinib maintenance therapy is limited and based
purely on post-hoc, non-randomized comparisons.
Patient Population and Primary Endpoints
Two randomized, controlled trials were submitted in support of this NDA, QuANTUM-First and
QuANTUM-R. QuANTUM-First was a randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind trial in
newly diagnosed patients and is the only randomized trial in the population for which an
indication is currently sought by the Applicant. As such, QuANTUM-First serves as an adequate
and well-controlled clinical investigation into the effects of adding quizartinib to standard
induction and consolidation chemotherapy followed by maintenance monotherapy. FDA
analysis of the primary endpoint of OS for this study revealed a HR of 0.78 (95% CI 0.62, 0.98;
p=0.0324) as discussed above. The median OS in the quizartinib arm was 31.9 months (95% CI
21.0, NE) and 15.1 months (95% CI 13.2, 26.2) in the control arm, for a 16.8-month difference in
median OS between arms.
As discussed above, QuANTUM-R was randomized open label trial for R/R FLT3-ITD AML. FDA
analysis of the primary endpoint of QuANTUM-R revealed an OS HR of 0.77 (95% CI 0.59, 0.99;
p=0.019. The median OS in the quizartinib arm was 26.9 weeks (95% CI 23.1, 31.0) and 20.4
weeks (95% CI 17.0, 25.2) in the control arm, for a 6-week difference in median OS between the
arms. The credibility of the OS results for QuANTUM-R were plagued by uncertainties that have
been described above. Given these concerns and the questionable clinical utility of a 6-week
extension in median OS versus salvage chemotherapy, it was not thought that QuANTUM-R
represented substantial evidence of effectiveness for the treatment of patients with R/R FLT3mutated AML.
Secondary and Other Endpoints
FDA analysis of the per protocol EFS on QuANTUM-First was not statically significant with a HR
of 0.92 (95% CI 0.75, 1.11; p=0.2371). However, sensitivity analysis showed that the EFS analysis
was highly dependent on the time window allowed for count recovery before considering a
response as induction treatment failure. Review of CR rates between arms was remarkably
243
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
similar with CR rates of 55% on both treatment arms. However, the duration of CR was 38.6 and
12.4 months in the quizartinib versus placebo arms, respectively.
FDA’s analysis of the key secondary endpoint of QuANTUM-R revealed a 2.3-week difference in
median EFS between the quizartinib arm and control arm for a HR of 0.9 (95% CI 0.71, 1.16).
This was not statistically significant, with a p-value of 0.1138. CR rates on QuANTUM-R were 4%
on the quizartinib arm and 1% on the control arm, and CR/CRh rates were 11% on the
quizartinib arm and 3% on the control arm. These CR rates are similar to those seen in the nonrandomized earlier phase studies AC220-002 and 2689-CL-2004, with CR rates of 2 and 3%
respectively.
Subpopulations
As discussed above, analysis of subpopulations for QuANTUM-First generally showed benefit
with quizartinib treatment in most groups investigated. Only the small subgroup with favorable
risk cytogenetics suggested better outcomes on the placebo arm. Post-hoc analyses of the
effect of quizartinib in the maintenance phase suggest that patients who receive HSCT may not
derive any further benefit from quizartinib maintenance therapy.
Any analysis of subgroups in QuANTUM-R is restricted by the limitations described for the
primary analysis of the ITT population above. As described in detail, the OS results for
QuANTUM-R appear to be driven primarily by the substratum of patients preselected for LDAC.
There was a striking imbalance between the rates of subsequent HSCT in this particular stratum
(23% in the quizartinib arm and none on the LDAC arm).
Dose and Dose-Response
Since the OS results on study QuANTUM-First and QuANTUM-R cannot be used as response
assessment (CR rates were equivalent on treatment arms of QuANTUM-First and CR rates in the
R/R trials were low across the development program), an assessment of dose-response is not
possible at this time.
Onset, Duration, and Durability of Efficacy Effects
The durability of CR on QuANTUM-First is mentioned above. Since the number of patients on
quizartinib arm who achieved a CR on the Study QuANTUM-R was so small (4%), an analysis of
time to response and/or durability response is not informative. The same is true for CR rates
across the remaining trials in the clinical development program.
Other Relevant Benefits
244

{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
The applicant points to CRc, defined as CR+ CRi+ CRp as evidence of clinical benefit across
multiple trials in Table 65 and Table 66 above. It is noted that CRi and CRp are not considered
evidence of clinical benefit in the treatment of AML (Jen 2018). The lack of equivalency of CR as
compared to CRi and CRp in the context of cytotoxic therapies was reported in a cohort of over
6000 patients with AML treated across multiple countries and centers (Walter 2010), and
absent any additional data using targeted therapies specifically the equivalency of these
endpoints remains unknown. However, the statistically significant OS result from QuANTUM-R,
the demonstration of monotherapy activity in terms of modest CR and CRh rates across R/R
AML trials (Study AC220-007, AC220-002, and 2689-CL-2004), and the demonstration of
moderate CRc rates across monotherapy trials (Table 66) are sufficient to provide confirmatory
evidence that quizartinib has activity in FLT3-ITD AML.
Integrated Assessment of Effectiveness
Data:
Study AC220-A-U302 provides the primary data to support efficacy claims under this NDA; see
Section 8.1.2 for details. Additional studies of quizartinib in combination with chemotherapy
and as monotherapy in newly diagnosed or R/R AML provide additional supportive data (see
Sections 8.1.4.2, 8.1.5, and 8.1.6).
The Applicant’s Position:
Study AC220-A-U302 demonstrates that selective and potent inhibition of FLT3-ITD mutant
signaling results in a significant improvement in survival when combined with standard
induction and consolidation chemotherapy, and then as continuation monotherapy for up to
36 cycles in adult FLT3-ITD (+) newly diagnosed AML subjects aged between 18 and 75 years
old. Considering the high-risk population enrolled in Study AC220-A-U302, quizartinib is
addressing a significant unmet medical need for patients with FLT3-ITD (+) AML. This is
particularly the case for older subjects (≥60 years), notably those who are eligible for intensive
therapy, for whom current treatment options remain unsatisfactory, with a lack of randomized
data to inform on appropriate treatment approaches.
Study AC220-A-U302 is the first and only global, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled
Phase 3 study that has demonstrated a survival benefit specifically in subjects with FLT3-ITD (+)
AML, a population associated with an inferior survival with chemotherapy alone. The study
compared the effect of quizartinib versus placebo (administered with standard induction and
consolidation chemotherapy, then administered as continuation monotherapy for up to
36 cycles) on the primary endpoint of OS in adult subjects with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+)
AML. At the time of initiation of this study, chemotherapy with or without allo-HSCT was the
standard for the treatment of AML; hence, placebo along with standard combination
chemotherapy was used as the comparator. The study population is representative of the
target population of patients with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML, and demographics and
245
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
disease characteristics of subjects in Study AC220-A-U302 were well balanced between the
2 treatment arms.
The study met its primary endpoint by demonstrating a statistical significance in OS of
quizartinib compared with placebo with an HR (95% CI) of 0.776 (0.615, 0.979), corresponding
to an estimated reduction in the risk of death of 22.4% in favor of the quizartinib arm. The
median OS (95% CI) was 31.9 months (21.0, not estimable [NE]) in the quizartinib arm versus
15.1 months (13.2, 26.2) in the placebo arm, resulting in prolongation of median OS by
16.8 months compared with standard chemotherapy (Table 26). The survival rates at 1 year
(67.4% versus 57.7%) and 2 years (54.7% versus 44.7%) also favored the quizartinib arm. The
results of the preplanned supplemental and sensitivity analyses of OS were consistent with
those of the primary OS analysis, showing a benefit of quizartinib, as demonstrated by
consistent HRs (95% CI) of 0.774 (0.614, 0.975) for the unstratified OS analysis and 0.752 (0.562,
1.008) for the OS analysis censored at the start date of the conditioning regimen for allo-HSCT
(Table 27). An improvement in OS was observed across most prespecified subgroups in favor of
quizartinib. These results are considered clinically meaningful improvements, given the poor
prognosis and the high unmet medical need. Furthermore, the E-R analysis indicated that the
proposed dosing regimen in Study AC220-A-U302 is appropriate.
With regard to the secondary endpoint, EFS, although the primary analysis of EFS based on the
definition of ITF as failure to achieve CR within 42 days of the start of the last cycle of induction
chemotherapy (FDA, 2020) was not statistically significant, the sensitivity analysis based on CR
in the Induction Phase using the protocol-defined 56-day window showed a clear benefit in
favor of quizartinib as demonstrated by an HR (95%CI) of 0.818 (0.669, 0.999)(Table 37). Given
that the protocol was designed to assess ITF up to 56 days from the start of the last induction
cycle, the sensitivity analysis is consistent with the OS result and supports the overall
conclusion on the efficacy of quizartinib.
Because the primary analysis for EFS was not significant, further statistical testing of other
secondary endpoints was stopped. Analyses of secondary and exploratory endpoints showed
that while the rates of CR were similar between treatment arms, a prespecified exploratory
analysis of RFS in subjects who achieved a CR at the end of Induction Phase showed that
subjects in the quizartinib arm had 3 times longer median RFS (39.3 months) than those in the
placebo arm (13.6 months), resulting in a 38.7% relative risk reduction of relapse or death
(Table 65). This benefit in RFS was maintained over time, and RFS rates are higher in the
quizartinib arm compared with the placebo arm (73.4% versus 52.4% at 12 months and 57.8%
versus 40.9% at 24 months, respectively). Furthermore, in a post hoc analysis in subjects who
achieved a CR, quizartinib delayed relapse compared to placebo with lower CIR at 24 months
(31.2% vs. 43.3%, respectively) and at 36 months (33.8% vs. 45.1%, respectively) from
randomization (Figure 18). Additional exploratory analyses showed that the median (95% CI)
duration of CR was approximately 3 times longer in subjects in the quizartinib arm (38.6 [21.9,
NE] months) versus the placebo arm (12.4 [8.8, 22.7] months). The Kaplan-Meier plot of the
duration of CR shows sustained separation of the curves for up to 3 years. There were no
246
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

notable differences in the demographics and baseline disease characteristics of subjects who
achieved a CR between the 2 treatment arms (eCTD M2.7.3 Tables 14.1.2.1.2 and 14.1.3.1.1.3).
Taken together, these data demonstrated that quizartinib combined with chemotherapy
followed by continuation monotherapy improves the OS of newly diagnosed subjects with FLT3ITD (+) AML. The benefit in OS is further supported by clinically meaningful improvement in
RFS, the longer duration of remission, and the lower CIR in CR subjects treated with quizartinib.
The FDA’s Assessment:
We agree with the Applicant’s assessment. QuANTUM-First demonstrated a statistically
significant and clinically meaningful improvement in the primary endpoint of overall survival.
This endpoint reflects both the long-term safety and efficacy of quizartinib treatment in newly
diagnosed FLT3-ITD AML. Moreover, the KM plot of OS in Figure 2 above shows the treatment
arms plateauing with a persistent difference, suggesting that the benefit to OS demonstrated
during the Study has a continuing effect on long-term survival. While the per protocol EFS
analysis was not statistically significant, sensitivity analyses conducted with an induction
assessment window in line with study conduct did show benefit. Analysis of other secondary
and exploratory endpoints showed a consistent effect whereby the addition of quizartinib
treatment to standard therapy extended the duration of CR responses and improved RFS.
The results of FDA analyses of data from QuANTUM-First, including unadjusted and adjusted
analyses of OS and RFS, suggest inconclusive evidence regarding use of quizartinib as
maintenance therapy after HSCT. Note that this conclusion is based on exploratory post-hoc
analyses; a more formal analysis is not possible since QuANTUM-First was inadequately
designed to isolate the effect of maintenance therapy. Thus, the evidence was inadequate to
support positive benefit-risk for patients post-HSCT. According to Levis et al, no FLT3 TKI has
definitively demonstrated improved efficacy for continued treatment following HSCT (Levis,
2019). Based on the lack of a demonstrated improvement in OS in patients post-HSCT, and the
potential for harm (HR > 1), we recommend a Limitation of Use in the prescribing information
stating that quizartinib is not indicated as maintenance monotherapy following allogeneic
HSCT. Although evaluations of maintenance post-consolidation chemotherapy are also limited
by the exploratory post-hoc nature of the analyses, the results are compelling enough clinically
(HR 0.4 for non-HSCT patients) that we recommend inclusion of maintenance monotherapy
following consolidation chemotherapy in the indication statement.

The Applicant’s Position:
The characterization of the safety profile of quizartinib in adult subjects with FLT3-ITD (+) newly
diagnosed AML is primarily based on data from Study AC220-A-U302. Since this is a placebocontrolled study, the results allow for an unbiased comparison of the safety profile of
quizartinib in combination with standard chemotherapy with that of chemotherapy alone.
The safety database is further augmented by inclusion of an All AML Pool that provides an
additional 816 subjects with AML that were treated with quizartinib, of whom 404 subjects also
received quizartinib at doses in the range of 30 to 60 mg QD. Studies in healthy volunteers and
subjects with hepatic impairment provide supporting information for the assessment of safety
profile of quizartinib. As such, the safety database allows for a comprehensive assessment of
the safety profile of quizartinib at the recommended dosing regimen and at higher quizartinib
doses, including detection and characterization of common AEs, to adequately inform on
appropriate toxicity management, and the evaluation of the overall benefit-risk in the proposed
indication.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s approach to safety data. As stated above, the characterization
of common AEs for the proposed indication is based on Study AC220-A-U302 (QuANTUM-First).
This study is a double-blind, placebo-controlled study that allows for assessment of AEs that
develop with quizartinib use in the context of intensive induction and consolidation
chemotherapy. The Applicant also notes that their development program and safety database
include a total of 1081 patients with AML that were treated quizartinib at various dose levels,

alone and in combination with chemotherapy, to further inform the incidence of severe but less
frequently seen AEs.
The efficacy evaluation for QuANTUM-First in the USPI will be presented at the time of the
protocol specified final analysis (August 13, 2021); therefore, the safety analysis for purposes of
the USPI will also be presented from that timepoint. The Applicant submitted an updated safety
dataset using a data cutoff date of (April 13, 2022). The updated dataset did not contribute
significant differences in adverse events, AEs resulting in death, dose reductions or
interruptions, or dose discontinuations due to AEs.
Safety Review Approach
Data:
AEs were coded using the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA), Version 24.0
and were assigned grades (severity) based on the NCI Common Terminology Criteria for
Adverse Events (CTCAE) v4.03. The lower-level terms from each study’s database were mapped
in the integrated database to the MedDRA hierarchy. A TEAE was defined as a new AE or an AE
with worsening grade occurring on or after Day 1 of study drug treatment until 30 days after
the last dose of study drug. Related AEs occurring more than 30 days after the last dose of
study drug were also considered TEAEs. AEs collected before the start of study drug treatment
were not considered TEAEs.
Different categories of TEAE (eg, all grade and Grade 3 /4 TEAE, related TEAE, serious TEAE,
TEAE associated with fatal outcome, quizartinib discontinuation and interruption) were
evaluated for the pivotal study AC220-A-U302 and the All AML Pool, with focus on the 30 to
60 mg quizartinib dose group.
For the comprehensive evaluation of the safety profile of quizartinib, predefined MedDRA
searches for specific categories of events of interest were conducted. The searches, defined in
Table 69, were selected based on the quizartinib safety experience in previous studies, TEAEs/
treatment-emergent serious AEs (TESAEs) expected to occur with treatment with the
chemotherapeutic agents used during the Induction and Consolidation Phases of Study AC220A-U302 and certain other studies, and the reported AEs associated with treatment using other
FLT3 inhibitors.
In addition to TEAEs, safety laboratory, vital signs and ECG data were evaluated. Subgroup
analyses of TEAE and ECG data according to age, sex, race, ethnic origin, geographic region,
duration of exposure, and concomitant use of strong CYP3A inhibitors and QT-prolonging
medications were done, to evaluate the safety of quizartinib in specific subject subpopulations.
Mild or moderate hepatic impairment and renal impairment were not associated with
significant effect on quizartinib exposure (see Table 11); therefore analysis of safety data in
subgroups of subjects with hepatic and renal impairment was not performed.

The safety review approach is considered comprehensive and appropriate to identify and

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s safety review approach. We again note that TEAEs from
Study AC220-A-U302 will form the basis for assessment of common AEs for the proposed
indication. Study AC220-A-U302 was a randomized placebo-controlled trial and contained 87%
(265/303) of all frontline AML patients from the total safety pool. The pooled analyses are used
for signal seeking for other important AEs.

The Applicant’s Position:
The size and composition of the safety database allows for a comprehensive assessment of
safety in AML subjects in general and specifically in the intended population of newly diagnosed
AML subjects that are FLT3-ITD (+). In Study AC220-A-U302 the total subject exposure was
longer in the quizartinib compared to the placebo arm. In addition, it is noteworthy that a
sizeable proportion of subjects in the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302 and the 30 to 60
mg dose group of the All AML Pool (30% and 17%, respectively) remained on treatment for >1
year, which allowed for appropriate assessment of the long-term safety of quizartinib in this
subject population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s position that exposure in the newly diagnosed
AML subjects allowed for a comprehensive assessment of safety. The Applicant notes and we
concur that the median exposure was longer in the quizartinib arm of the trial. This is also
reflected by a higher percentage of quizartinib patients being exposed for over 365 days and in
quizartinib patients have higher mean days of treatment. This longer exposure is reflective of
quizartinib patients remaining in remission longer than the placebo-treated patients and

therefore being eligible for later phases of on-study treatment. The Applicant notes that 79

assessment of the long-term safety of quizartinib. However, whether this allows for complete
assessment of long-term safety is a matter of analysis of the safety signals derived from these
subjects. Additionally, as was noted in the clinical pharmacology section, the appropriate dose
for maintenance phase of treatment was not systemically investigated.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
The Applicant’s Position:
Relevant demographic characteristics are well balanced between the quizartinib and placebo
arms in Study AC220-A-U302, providing assurance with respect to the safety conclusions and
the interpretation of comparisons made between treatment arms. The pivotal study and the
pooled AML dataset include a substantial proportion of subjects older than 65 years, which
allowed for the assessment of the safety profile of quizartinib in older age groups. The
population enrolled is sufficiently diverse and representative of a broader population of adult
patients with FLT3-ITD (+) newly diagnosed AML.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that Study AC220-A-U302 was well balanced between
the randomized arms. The population enrolled on Study AC220-A-U302 generally reflects
patients with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD mutated AML that are fit for treatment with intensive
chemotherapy. It is notable that the median age of subjects enrolled was 56 years for both
arms and that 40% of enrolled subjects were age ≥ 60 years. The racial composition of the study
was 60% white, 29% Asian, 10% other (including not collected per local regulations), and 1%
Black. While this racial composition is generally consistent with the geographic regions in which
the trial was conducted, it is unclear what the final breakdown might have been if country
specific privacy laws had allowed for the racial reporting of all enrolled subjects. It is notable
that the reported enrollment of Black/African American subjects was (1%) on study AC220-AU302, (3%) in the total AML quizartinib safety population, and (31%) of subjects enrolled to the
healthy volunteer study. The total AML quizartinib safety population demographics are similar
to those of Study AC220-A-U302. Some notable features include slightly older population with
median age of 58 years old and 47% of patients age ≥ 60 years, higher percentage of white
subjects (72%) and a lower percentage of Asian subjects (16.3%), and wider distribution of
FLT3-ITD VAF levels (based on inclusion of patients with FLT3-WT).
Adequacy of the safety database:
Data:
See preceding sections.
The Applicant’s Position:
The size and composition of the safety database allows for a comprehensive assessment of the
safety of quizartinib in the intended population of newly diagnosed AML patients that are FLT3ITD (+) and in specific patient subgroups.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The number of patients and the duration of
follow up included in the safety database is adequate to provide a reasonable estimate of

Adequacy of Applicant’s Clinical Safety Assessments
Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
Data:
The Applicant’s quality control and quality assurance activities, data management, and
statistical analysis of the study data were implemented per the Applicant’s applicable GCP
standard operating procedures. Monitors designated by the Applicant to ensure compliance of
the investigators and other site staff members involved in this study were granted direct access
to the source documents maintained at individual study sites and verified that CRF data were
accurately reported and consistent with the source data. As applicable, CROs and central
laboratories implemented data quality control activities based on their relevant in house
standard operating procedures.
The Applicant’s Position:
No material concerns were identified with respect to the quality and integrity of the submitted
datasets or individual case narratives. Data sets and case narratives were sufficiently complete
to allow for a thorough review of safety. Furthermore, no data integrity concerns were
reported in the course of site monitoring by the Applicant or its designees and data captured in
CRF and AE databases were consistent.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The quality of the safety data submitted was adequate to allow substantial primary review.
Categorization of Adverse Events
Data:
The safety profile observed with quizartinib was evaluated on the basis of the following:
•
•
•

Frequency, type, severity, and causal relationship of TEAEs to study treatment
Frequency of deaths, treatment-emergent SAEs, and other clinically significant TEAEs
(including AEs associated with study drug discontinuation, dose reduction, and/or study
drug interruption, and AESIs and other select AEs)
AESI for the study including
- QTcF prolongation Grade ≥3, either serious or non-serious and whether or not
causally related
- Combined elevations of aminotransferases (AT) and bilirubin

Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
•
•
•

TEAEs identified based on predefined MedDRA searches for specific events of interest
(Table 69)
Frequency and type of TEAEs in subgroups according to key intrinsic and extrinsic factors
(eg, age, sex, race, ethnic origin, duration of exposure, geographic region, and
concomitant use of strong CYP3A inhibitors)
Changes in laboratory variables, vital signs and ECGs, with particular attention to
abnormalities of CTCAE Grade 3 or 4.

See also Section 8.2.1.
The Applicant’s Position:
The categorization of AEs was considered appropriate for evaluating the safety data in patients
with AML in general and specifically in the intended population with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD
(+) AML.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agreed with mapping of reported terms to preferred terms per MedDRA 24.0. Terms that
referred directly to relapse, persistence of disease, or progression of AML were excluded from
FDA’s analyses. TEAEs were assessed by frequency (event per patient) and maximum grade
reported.
FDA reviewed the Applicant’s grouped terms as above but also developed a list of group terms
based on PTs that were reported in the AML ISS pool (see Appendix 19.5 for a table of FDA
grouped terms used throughout FDA safety analyses unless otherwise noted). FDA assessed
laboratory abnormalities as both reported AEs and by shifts from baseline labs. Since all
patients in Study AC220-A-U302 presented with acute leukemia, most patients had some level
of abnormal baseline labs induced by the disease process. Therefore, analysis anchored from
this abnormal baseline would not identify labs that normalized with control of the acute
leukemia but later became abnormal again. Therefore, analysis of both lab shifts and lab
abnormalities reported as TEAEs were evaluated to identify abnormal signals.
Routine Clinical Tests
Data:
Clinical tests for safety assessment included clinical laboratory evaluations (hematology,
biochemistry, coagulation, and urinalysis), vital signs and ECGs. The schedule of assessments in
the protocol specified the time points at which laboratory evaluations were conducted. Per
protocol, abnormal laboratory, vital signs, or ECG values considered clinically significant by the
investigator were to be reported as TEAEs.

ECG in the study were obtained in triplicate and average of triplicate readings were used for the
central ECG analysis. The investigator could use ECG obtained from the study-supplied ECG
machine to guide initial decisions regarding dose reduction, interruption, or escalation until the
average analysis from the central ECG laboratory became available. Once the analysis from the
central ECG laboratory had been received, it should be used to guide dosing decisions.
Monitoring for hypokalemia at least 3 times weekly was performed during the Induction and
Consolidation Phases of the study.
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical monitoring of subject safety (methods and time points) described in the protocol is
considered to be reasonable and adequate for the population, disease, and targeted indication
being investigated.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. See the discussion concerning laboratory AEs
versus laboratory value shift analysis in the FDA comment above.

a

The Applicant’s Position:
AML disease progression was the most common cause of death in the quizartinib studies.
Overall, 9.8% and 21.8% of subjects, respectively, died due to disease progression within 30
days and more than 30 days of last dose of quizartinib. AEs were the second most common
cause of death within 30 days after last dose of quizartinib, and infections and infestations were
the most frequent category of fatal AEs.
In the pivotal study, AC220-A-U302, on-treatment deaths were all deaths that occurred within
30 days of the last dose of study medication. In accordance with reporting requirements for
deaths in the study, the primary cause of on-treatment death was recorded as an AE. In Study
AC220-A-U302, AML disease progression was not required to be reported as an AE per
protocol; therefore, the incidence of TEAE of AML progression and/or relapse is much lower
than in the earlier studies in the development program. In the AC220-A-U302 study, the most
frequently reported types of TEAEs with an outcome of death were infections (septic shock and
sepsis). These fatal infections predominantly occurred during the Induction and Consolidation
Phases when subjects were also receiving intensive chemotherapy, with low incidence of fatal
infections in the Continuation Phase.
270
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
In Study AC220-A-U302, a numerically higher incidence of deaths within 30 days of the start of
study drug was observed in the quizartinib arm compared with the placebo arm. The majority
of these early deaths in the quizartinib arm were also due to infections (sepsis and septic
shock).
The incidence of cardiac events, including cardiac arrhythmias, associated with fatal outcome
was low. Two subjects from the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302 experienced cardiac
arrest that were associated with recorded ventricular fibrillation on ECG (reported as a separate
TEAE for one of the subjects) and occurred in the context of Grade 3 or 4 hypokalemia. One of
the subjects was successfully resuscitated by cardioversion, however the second subject died as
a result of the event. An additional subject in the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302 died
in his sleep 10 days after the last dose of quizartinib with no cause of death identified and no
significant QTcF prolongation during the study.
In addition to the cases from Study AC220-A-U302, a comprehensive review of all potential
cardiac arrhythmia events from the entire quizartinib development program revealed 1
additional case of cardiac arrest, which occurred at higher quizartinib doses in subjects with
some evidence of QTcF prolongation prior to the event. The event of fatal cardiac arrest
occurred in a subject in the setting of sepsis 4 days after the starting quizartinib dose of 90 mg
was increased to 135 mg and the subject was on a concomitant strong CYP3A inhibitor. The
subject’s QTcF interval had increased from baseline of 408 ms to a maximum of 496 ms 4 days
before the cardiac arrest but was reportedly 471 ms the day before death.
Other TEAE with fatal outcome were infrequent at 30 to 60 mg dose of quizartinib, with very
low incidence of major hemorrhagic events, including intracranial hemorrhage.
Early mortality is a known complication in newly diagnosed AML subjects and can be attributed
to several confounding factors, such as age, frailty, organ dysfunction, underlying disease
(cytogenetic abnormality), comorbidities, and complications from chemotherapy. Bleeding and
infections are the most common complications of standard chemotherapy and can result in
increased risk of treatment-related early mortality during or after induction therapy. In Study
AC220-A-U302, the OS Kaplan-Meier (KM) curves show an early crossing attributed to early
deaths. The observed incidence rate of early deaths in the study is small and in line with
reported early mortality rates in patients with newly diagnosed AML receiving standard
chemotherapy (Sasaki, 2022). OS captures all-cause mortality during the study and the
imbalance in deaths seen early in the study does not persist over the course of quizartinib
treatment.
The OS KM curves show a clear and sustained separation in favor of the quizartinib arm, with an
early crossing attributed to early deaths. Early mortality is a known complication in newly
diagnosed AML subjects and can be attributed to several confounding factors, such as age,
frailty, organ dysfunction, underlying disease (cytogenetic abnormality), comorbidities, and
complications from chemotherapy. Bleeding and infections are the most common
complications of standard chemotherapy and can result in increased risk of treatment-related
271
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
early mortality during or after induction therapy. Overall survival captures all-cause mortality
during the study and the quizartinib arm has demonstrated a substantial improvement in OS
over the placebo arm. The early imbalance in deaths does not persist over the course of
quizartinib treatment and the overall conclusion on efficacy is not impacted.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA performed an assessment of deaths, based on review of individual patient narratives, in
patients enrolled on Study AC220-A-U302 that occurred within 30 days of the last dose of study
drug, either placebo or quizartinib. In comparison to the Applicant, the FDA used simplified
categories for root cause of death including TEAE treatment related, AML progressive disease,
and TEAE related to transplant. We would have also considered intercurrent illness/event as a
root cause of death. However, we did not identify any deaths on Study AC220-A-U302 that we
would have categorized as such. Table 77 below shows the summary of FDA adjudicated deaths
between arms.

In making root cause determination, FDA did not assign the AE to either the chemotherapy, if
recently given, or study drug (placebo or quizartinib). Instead, the determination was made that
the AE was related to recent protocol therapy (not to progressive/ relapsed disease, transplant
related TEAE, or intercurrent illness) and that comparison between treatment arms would be
used to assess for potential contribution from quizartinib treatment. This is in contrast to the
Applicant’s approach to adjudication where their physician reviewers attempted to assign the
AE to either study drug and/or chemotherapy. When comparing root cause assignments, FDA
considered the Applicant’s root cause in agreement with FDA’s use of TEAE treatment-related if
they attributed the AE to either chemotherapy or study drug. FDA did not agree with the
Applicants root cause of death in the 27 cases listed in Table 78 below. We note that in 25 of
these cases, the Applicant attributed the root cause to an AE secondary to AML. For these
cases, FDA considered these events to be related to the treatment, since most were
complications from cytopenia such as infections and bleeding events and these cytopenias
were greatly exacerbated by recent treatments with chemotherapy and study drugs without
mention of refractory or relapsed disease. We would also note that the Applicant was
inconsistent with the approach to such cases, with cases of sepsis-related deaths occurring
during induction alternatively being attributed to an adverse event from chemotherapy or as

There were also two cases that the Applicant was unable to judge the cause of death.
Subject
, was a 51-year-old male assigned to the quizartinib treatment. He presented
back to hospital at the end of consolidation cycle 1 with chest pain, SOB, and cyanosis that was
diagnosed as a PE and treated as such. However, the Applicant’s physician reviewers noted that
a definitive diagnosis of PE was not made, and the subject died several days later after their
status worsened. Due to the proximity to study treatment, FDA considered this death
treatment related. Although, FDA agrees that the proximal cause of death was not clear from
narrative, PE could have been consistent with the described presentation.
(b) (6)

Subject
, was a 69-year-old male assigned to the quizartinib treatment. The subject
died in his sleep at end of consolidation cycle 1, 10 days after last dose of quizartinib and 24
days after last dose of HiDAC. This subject had not had issues with QTc prolongation while on
study. Due to the proximity to chemotherapy and quizartinib treatment, FDA considered this
treatment related.
(b) (6)

FDA noticed an unexpected signal in the early deaths on the quizartinib arm while reviewing
patient narratives. We noted that there was an overrepresentation of Russian sites in the early
induction deaths in general and on the quizartinib arm in particular. While the overall induction
on-treatment death rate was 6.1% for all patients on both arms of Study AC220-A-U302,
Russian sites had an on-treatment induction death rate of 32% (6/19) and randomized
induction death rate of 37% (7/19; one patient died after randomization but prior to receiving
treatment with quizartinib). Most of these deaths were on the quizartinib arm (5/6 and 6/7,
respectively). When FDA reviewed the death narratives, 5/6 induction on-treatment deaths
described rapidly progressive septic shock unresponsive to initial treatment. We also noted that
the choice of empiric antibiotics did not reflect the standard of care generally employed in the
treatment of neutropenic patients. It is unclear if a more traditional choice of antibiotics might
have changed the outcomes for these cases. The remaining case was hemorrhagic stroke that
occurred in a patient after a second cycle of induction chemotherapy.

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
In Study AC220-A-U302, more subjects in the quizartinib arm experienced TESAEs, compared to
the placebo arm (143 subjects [54.0%] and 123 subjects [45.9%], respectively). Quizartinib is
associated with development of myelosuppression in treated subjects, which is a risk factor for
the development of infections. In the AC220-A-U302 study, infections (pneumonia, septic
shock, and sepsis) and blood disorders (febrile neutropenia) were the most commonly reported
TESAE in the study.
Overall, the incidence of serious infections and cytopenias was generally well balanced
between the 2 treatment arms, consistent with the use of induction and consolidation
chemotherapy in the study. The majority of these events occurred predominantly in the
Induction and Consolidation Phases of the study; however serious cytopenias and infections
(pneumonia) also occurred in the Continuation Phase, reflecting the known myelosuppressive
effect of quizartinib. Serious infections are a known risk for subjects with AML receiving
intensive cytarabine and anthracycline chemotherapy (Baluch, 2019; Afessa, 2006).
Generally, the types of TESAEs reported in the 30 to 60 mg group of the All AML Pool were
consistent with those reported in Study AC220-A-U302.
SAEs reported from the pivotal study are consistent with those observed in the quizartinib AML
development program, which included subjects with R/R disease, receiving quizartinib
monotherapy.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA performed an analysis of all-grade treatment-emergent SAEs utilizing FDA grouped terms
occurring in ≥2% of subjects on Study AC220-A-U302, which is presented in Table 81 below. The
results agreed with the Applicant’s analysis overall but differed in the rates of grouped events.
It is notable that despite higher rate of overall TESAEs in the quizartinib arm (54% quizartinib
versus 46% placebo) that there was not a strong signal in any one TESAE to account for this
difference. FDA utilized criteria of occurrence in at least 5% of subjects and an event rate 2%
greater on the quizartinib arm to determine TESAEs that were likely increased by quizartinib
exposure beyond the high background rate of intensive chemotherapy treatment. With these
criteria, only the TESAE of Febrile neutropenia was more common in quizartinib arm (11%
quizartinib versus 8% placebo) than the placebo arm and included in labeling. FDA considered a
lower event threshold rate of at least 2% of quizartinib-treated subjects and an event rate 2%
greater on the quizartinib arm to determine TESAEs but even these criteria would not have
yielded additional quizartinib associated TESAEs.

The Applicant’s Position:
In Study AC220-A-U302 overall, infections (septic shock, pneumonia and sepsis) and
cytopenias (thrombocytopenia, neutropenia, and febrile neutropenia) were the most
frequent events associated with study drug discontinuation. The percentage of subjects with
TEAEs associated with study drug discontinuation was higher with quizartinib than with
placebo; this difference was observed in all phases with the highest incidence and difference
282
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
between quizartinib and placebo seen in the Continuation Phase. The higher incidence of
TEAE associated with discontinuation in the Continuation Phase is likely due to the longer
duration of this phase, with accrual of events. Infections associated with quizartinib
discontinuation tended to occur in the early phases of the study, whereas discontinuation
due to TEAEs of cytopenias was more frequent in the Continuation Phase. In the
Continuation Phase, cytopenias (thrombocytopenia, neutropenia, and anemia) and
gastrointestinal disorders (nausea, diarrhea, and vomiting) were the most frequent events
associated with study drug discontinuation and were reported by subjects only in the
quizartinib arm (AC220-A-U302 CSR Table 14.3.2.2.1).
Management of QT interval prolongation with quizartinib includes quizartinib dose modification
and interruption. However, only 2 subjects (0.8%) in the quizartinib arm discontinued due to
TEAEs of ECG QT prolonged.
The incidence and types of TEAEs associated with discontinuation in the 30 to 60 mg group of
the All AML Pool were consistent with those reported in Study AC220-A-U302.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of study drug discontinuation events. FDA assessed
TEAEs leading to study drug discontinuation in Study AC220-A-U304 with FDA group terms as
shown in Table 85 below. Our analysis agreed with the Applicant’s in that sepsis was the most
common TEAE causing treatment discontinuation on Study AC220-A-U302. Sepsis and
pneumonia were the most common TEAES in the ISS AML Quizartinib pool causing study drug
discontinuation. Per the tabulations below, sepsis as a cause of discontinuation was included in
quizartinib labeling.

The Applicant’s Position:
In Study AC220-A-U302, dose interruption and dose reduction due to TEAEs occurred more
frequently in quizartinib-treated subjects. Overall, infections (pneumonia, septic shock, and
sepsis) and cytopenias (neutropenia, neutrophil count decreased, thrombocytopenia, and
platelet count decreased) were the most frequent events associated with study drug dose
reduction and dose interruption. Infections associated with quizartinib dose reduction and
interruption tended to occur in the early phases of the study, whereas discontinuation, dose
reduction, or dose interruption due to TEAEs of cytopenias was more frequent in the
Continuation Phase.
Management of QT interval prolongation with quizartinib includes quizartinib dose modification
and interruption. Therefore, TEAEs of ECG QT interval prolonged associated with quizartinib
interruption or dose reduction were reported commonly in the study (7 subjects [2.6%] and
10 subjects [3.8%], respectively).
The incidence and types of TEAEs associated with dose interruption in the 30 to 60 mg group of
the All AML Pool were consistent with those reported in Study AC220-A-U302. Integration of
data for the All AML Pool was not performed for dose reduction, as these data were not
captured consistently across all clinical studies included in the All AML Pool.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA conducted an analysis of TEAEs leading to dose interruptions using FDA group terms from
Study AC220-A-U302 as shown in Table 87 below. This shows the results for TEAEs with a
frequency of 2% or greater. Per FDA group terms, the most frequent TEAE leading to dose
interruption was neutropenia in 11% of quizartinib treated patients. For labeling, we assess
quizartinib related interruptions of clinical significance as those occurring in ≥2% of subjects
and ≥2% greater on the quizartinib arm than the placebo. Of those listed in the table only
neutropenia, myelosuppression, and thrombocytopenia were included in labeling.

Significant Adverse Events
286
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Data:
The proportions of subjects with Grade 3/4 events were similar between the treatment arms.
The most frequently reported Grade 3/4 TEAEs in the quizartinib arm included cytopenias
(febrile neutropenia, neutropenia, neutrophil count decreased, and thrombocytopenia),
hypokalemia, and infections (pneumonia), of which the events of neutropenia and neutrophil
count decreased occurred at a ≥5 percentage points higher incidence in the quizartinib arm
than in the placebo arm (eCTD 2.7.4 Table 2.1.2.3).
Generally, the pattern and incidence of Grade 3/4 TEAEs in the 30 to 60 mg group of the All
AML Pool were consistent with those in the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302. Blood
cytopenias were the most frequently reported Grade 3/4 events in the All AML Pool, with no
observed dose response trend. Infections (pneumonia and sepsis) were the second most
frequently reported types of severe TEAE; no dose-dependent trend was noted for these TEAEs
(eCTD 2.7.4 Table 2.1.2.3).
The Applicant’s Position:
Blood cytopenias (neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, and anemia) and infections were the most
common severe (Grade 3/4) TEAEs. Severe cytopenias are expected to occur in subjects
receiving induction and consolidation chemotherapy, and management of these is part of
routine clinical care for subjects with newly diagnosed AML. Overall, the majority of TEAEs of
blood cytopenias were managed with study drug interruptions or dose reductions and few
subjects discontinued quizartinib due to these events. AEs reported are described in other
sections of this document.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicants assessment that blood cytopenias and infections were the most
common severe TEAEs. FDA conducted analysis of grade 1-4 and grade 3-4 TEAEs with FDA
group terms in Table 94 below. However, we note that although severe cytopenias are
expected from intensive chemotherapy, the rates of cytopenias reported as severe TEAEs were
higher on the quizartinib arm of this blinded study. This indicates that quizartinib worsened
cytopenias beyond those induced by intensive chemotherapy alone.

TEAEs identified as adverse drug reactions (ADRs) for the Continuation Phase of Study AC220A-U302 are described below.
The most common adverse reactions in the quizartinib arm during continuation monotherapy
were neutrophil count decreased (88%), platelet count decreased (72%), hemoglobin
decreased (55%), ALT increased (49%), upper respiratory tract infections (28%), nausea (23%)
and diarrhea (21%). No fatal adverse reactions occurred during continuation. The most
common Grade 3 or 4 adverse reactions reported in subjects with quizartinib were neutrophil
count decreased (72%), platelet count decreased (24%), and hemoglobin decreased (18%).
Serious adverse reactions in the quizartinib arm during Continuation monotherapy were
herpes infections (3.4%), neutropenia (2.6%), upper respiratory tract infections (1.7%), and
vomiting (1.7%).
The most common adverse reactions associated with dose interruption of quizartinib during
Continuation monotherapy were neutropenia (24%), thrombocytopenia (10%), and ECG QT
prolonged (4.3%). The most common adverse reactions associated with dose reduction of
quizartinib were neutropenia (21%), thrombocytopenia (10%), and ECG QT prolonged (5%).
The most common adverse reactions associated with permanent discontinuation of
quizartinib during Continuation monotherapy were thrombocytopenia (2.6%), nausea,
neutropenia and decreased appetite (1.7% each).

The Applicant’s Position:
The most common ADRs were ALT increased, cytopenias, and gastrointestinal disorders.
Adverse reactions with a fatal outcome were fungal infections and cardiac arrest. Serious
adverse reactions in ≥2% of subjects were neutropenia, fungal infections and herpes infections.
Severe (Grade 3/ 4) ADRs were cytopenias (neutrophil count decreased, platelet count
decreased, and hemoglobin decreased). Overall, ADRs for quizartinib were manageable by
dose modification and routine clinical practice.
The profile of quizartinib ADR for the proposed indications are consistent with the overall safety
profile of quizartinib determined in the development program for R/R AML.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA tabulations of TEAEs agreed with the Applicant’s in terms of rate of any TEAE, grade 3 or 4
TEAEs, grade 5 TEAEs, and TEAEs leading to either dose discontinuations, dose reductions, or
dose interruptions. Table 94 shows FDA tabulation of the most common grade 1-4 and grade 34 TEAEs that occurred in at least 10% of subjects on Study AC220-A-U302.

Due to the high rate of TEAEs induced by the intensive chemotherapy backbone, FDA identified
those TEAEs that occurred in at least 10% of subjects and ≥2% of patients on the quizartinib
arm than placebo as quizartinib-related adverse events. These are marked in Table 94 above
with (*) following the preferred term and were included in labeling. As the Applicant’s analysis
similarly showed, the most common TEAEs were GI toxicity, infectious complications, and
cytopenias. It is notable that although quizartinib increased the occurrence rate of some of the
above TEAEs, many of the TEAEs either occurred at the same as or more frequently on the
placebo arm. Moreover, other than QTc prolonged and neutropenia, quizartinib did not induce
a significant shift to higher toxicity (higher rates of grade 3 or 4) for the common TEAEs listed
above.
FDA looked at the rate of TEAEs that occurred in the monotherapy maintenance phase of
protocol treatment to understand both the potential risk of treatment during this longest phase
of treatment and the toxicity incurred from quizartinib in patients without the high background
rate of AEs caused by active disease or intensive chemotherapy. Table 95 shows that the most
common TEAE in quizartinib-treated patients during continuation therapy was neutropenia
(48% grade 1-4 and 43% with grade 3-4). Other common TEAEs that were at least 2% more
common with quizartinib during this phase included GI toxicity (diarrhea, nausea, abdominal
pain, vomiting, decreased appetite), thrombocytopenia, upper respiratory infections,
herpesvirus infections (shingles and HSV), mucositis, hypertransaminasemia, QTc prolonged,
musculoskeletal pain, cough, and headache. It is interesting to note that despite the high rate
of neutropenia, subjects were not at high risk of neutropenic fever or sepsis. Instead, there was
an increase in milder infections such as reactivation of latent herpes viruses and a higher rate of
reported upper respiratory infections.

During the maintenance phase, analysis of TEAE by history of transplant showed that rate of
individual TEAEs in quizartinib arm were an average of 8% higher if the subject had received a
transplant compared to those that did not. For example, the rate of neutropenia during the
maintenance phase was 51% for quizartinib-treated patients that underwent transplant but
only 43% for those that had not been transplanted (Table 96). All of the listed TEAEs followed
this trend except for QT prolongation, whose TEAE rate was not increased by history of
transplant (10% quizartinib-HSCT vs 13% quizartinib-nonHSCT). The majority of reported TEAEs
during this phase occurred during the first year of maintenance treatment.

The hematology parameters assessed were platelets, hemoglobin, WBCs, neutrophils, and
lymphocytes. The blood chemistry parameters assessed were sodium, magnesium, potassium,
calcium, serum creatinine, ALT, AST, total bilirubin (TBL), and ALP. Hepatic function
abnormalities are presented in Table 107 and the rest of the laboratory data are summarized
below.
In Study AC220-A-U302, as expected in a population of subjects with AML, many hematology
parameter values were outside the normal range at baseline and during the study. For most
subjects, Grade 3 or 4 values were observed for neutrophils, WBC, hemoglobin, platelets, and
lymphocytes with treatment.
Approximately 15% of subjects in each treatment arm had severe hypokalemia during the study
(shifts from normal potassium levels or Grade 1 hypokalemia at baseline to Grade 3 or 4
hypokalemia).
Shifts in renal function parameters (glomerular filtration rate [GFR] and creatinine clearance)
were observed infrequently among subjects in both treatment arms. Approximately 4% of
subjects in both treatment arms had shifts to GFR <30 mL/min/1.73 m2, and 2% of subjects had
shifts to creatinine clearance of <30 mL/min.
Similar decreases in hematology parameters, non-hepatic chemistry evaluations, and renal
laboratory parameters to those observed in Study AC220-A-U302 were observed in the 30 to
60 mg group of the All AML Pool.
The Applicant’s Position:
Quizartinib has a myelosuppressive effect and shifts in hematological parameters (anemia,
neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, lymphopenia) were observed in the pivotal study.
301
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
No clinically meaningful changes over time were found in clinical chemistry parameters in
either treatment arm. Overall, shifts in these chemistry parameters were observed
infrequently after treatment. Although hypokalemia is a risk factor for QT prolongation and
associated arrhythmia events with quizartinib, no difference in severe hypokalemia (Grade 3 /4)
was observed with quizartinib treatment relative to placebo treatment in Study AC220-A-U302
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of laboratory shifts. FDA analysis was generally in
agreement with the Applicant’s results. However, it is important to note that the unique
situation of acute leukemia and induction of remission means that most patients present with
baseline abnormalities in many labs and then develop further worsening with induction
chemotherapy. High baseline labs obscure on-treatment worsening that might occur after
resolution with remission attainment and hides prolonged abnormalities. Moreover,
induction/consolidation chemotherapy essentially make all patients severely cytopenic.
Therefore, FDA used a combination of laboratory shift analysis and laboratory values reported
as TEAEs to capture laboratory adverse events induced by treatment. To ascertain which labs
were worsening with quizartinib treatment above the background of intensive chemotherapy,
we again used a threshold of ≥2% worse on the quizartinib arm compared to placebo as
indicated with (*) in Table 100 below. One notable lab that did show a marked difference with
quizartinib treatment was elevations of creatine kinase (25% with quizartinib versus 7% with
placebo). It is interesting that creatinine kinase showed this elevation with quizartinibtreatment while musculoskeletal pain was less common on the quizartinib arm overall (27% vs
32%) and only slightly more common on the quizartinib arm during maintenance (20%
quizartinib versus 18% placebo) . While analysis of delayed count recovery reflected some of
the myelosuppression induced by quizartinib treatment, perhaps the best assessment of this
effect is shown in Table 95 reflecting use of continuous quizartinib treatment given to patients
in remission.

Electrocardiograms
Data:
Quizartinib prolongs QT interval in a dose-dependent and exposure-dependent manner.
Regular ECG monitoring was required per study protocol in the AC220-A-U302 study. The
schedule of assessments in the protocol specified the time points at which laboratory
evaluations were conducted.
ECG data were obtained in triplicate and were analyzed in a central laboratory, with the central
ECG reading utilized for the safety analyses. ECG matched with quizartinib PK measurements
were used for the concentration-QTc E-R analysis.
More frequent assessments were performed during the Induction and Consolidation Phases of
the study, with the frequency of testing reduced in the Continuation Phase. More frequent ECG
recordings were required during quizartinib initiation and dose escalation.
The Applicant’s Position:
The results of the ECG data analysis are discussed under the respective topic of QT prolongation
and associated clinical events in Section 8.2.5.1.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. QT prolongation is further discussed below.
QT
Data:
Quizartinib prolongs QT interval in a dose-dependent and exposure-dependent manner.
The Applicant’s Position:
The results of the QT interval data analysis are discussed under the respective topic of QT
prolongation and associated clinical events in Section 8.2.5.1.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that quizartinib prolongs the QT interval in a dosedependent and exposure-dependent manner.

A concentration-QTcF (C-QTcF) E-R analysis was also conducted to assess the subject
characteristics that may have impacted QTc prolongation.
In Study AC220-A-U302, a direct-response maximum effect (Emax) model best described the CQTcF relationship between quizartinib concentrations and QTcF. The median model-predicted
ΔQTcF at Cmax,ss in subjects with newly diagnosed AML in Study AC220-A-U302 during the
Continuation Phase at 30 mg and 60 mg, assuming no dose interruption or discontinuation,
were 18.4 ms (90% CI: 16.3, 20.5) and 24.1 ms (90% CI: 21.4, 26.6), respectively.
The covariates that were tested in the C-QTcF model included hypokalemia, serum calcium and
magnesium concentrations, age, body weight, sex, race, and use of QT-prolonging drugs, betablocker drugs, and anthracycline. Age and hypokalemia had an effect on the baseline QTcF.
The covariate analysis on the drug effect model identified age, concurrent daunorubicin use,
hypokalemia, and calcium levels as statistically significant covariates on Emax. However,
interindividual variability on Emax was increased after including the covariate effects, and these
effects were not seen in the graphical analysis. In addition, age, daunorubicin use, and
hypokalemia effects were estimated with high uncertainty with RSE >34%. As a result, these
covariate effects were not retained in the model. The remaining covariates had no effect on
the baseline QTcF.

Concomitant administration of QT-prolonging medications had no impact on the observed QTcF

administration. Most common drugs in this category were antifungals (fluconazole),
antiemetics (ondansetron) and antibiotics (ciprofloxacin, levofloxacin; Table 106).
