The Applicant’s Position:
Quizartinib is associated with QTc prolongation in a dose-dependent manner. QTc prolongation
may increase the risk of ventricular arrhythmias or TdP. While the incidence of TEAEs of ECG
QT prolonged in the study was higher in the quizartinib arm than in the placebo arm, the
majority of events were asymptomatic and Grade 1 or 2, and the incidence of Grade 3 QTcF
prolongation was low, demonstrating that QTcF prolongation with quizartinib is manageable
with dose modification or interruption and correction of additional risk factors (eg, monitoring
for and correction of electrolyte abnormalities, quizartinib dose modification for QTcF
prolongation, and for concomitant strong CYP3A inhibitor use). Ventricular arrythmia events
were infrequent in the study: 2 subjects (0.8%) in the quizartinib arm experienced cardiac arrest
with recorded ventricular fibrillation (both during the Induction Phase), 1 with fatal outcome, in
the presence of severe hypokalemia, and 1 subject died in his sleep with no cause of death
identified and who had no significant QTc prolongation on ECG while on study drug. In the
overall AML development program, the incidence of ventricular arrythmia events was low at
the recommended quizartinib doses.
310
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
QT prolongation is a notable risk to be mitigated. Quizartinib is a CYP3A substrate, and
concomitant administration of strong CYP3A inhibitors increases quizartinib exposure.
Therefore, quizartinib dose should be reduced when used concomitantly with strong CYP3A
inhibitors and the study protocol allowed for the use of concomitant strong CYP3A inhibitors
with appropriate quizartinib dose reduction. In addition, concomitant administration of QTprolonging medications, which were allowed in the study as warranted in the care of AML
subjects, may potentially lead to increased incidence of QT prolongation with quizartinib.
However, in Study AC220-A-U302 a large proportion of subjects received QT-prolonging
medications concomitantly, the QTcF analysis while receiving QT-prolonging medications was
not notably different from when these same subjects were not on concomitant QT-prolonging
medications.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA notes that preclinical characterization of quizartinib and its major metabolite AC886
suggested that it could affect the cardiac action potential. Specifically, patch clamp experiments
showed that quizartinib and AC886 inhibited hERG current (IKr) and IKs currents. These
experiments showed greater partial inhibition of IKs with 67% or 27% inhibition at 3 µM for
Quizartinib or AC886, respectively. This is compared to only 16% or 12% for IKr inhibition at 3
µM of Quizartinib or AC886, respectively. To place this in perspective, the Cmax concentration
in people administered 60 mg quizartinib is approximately 1 µM for quizartinib and 0.3 µM for
the AC886 metabolite. Therefore, although inhibition of both rectifying currents was observed,
it is thought that the predominant effect on cardiac repolarization would be due to the greater
inhibition of the IKs current.
Most drugs that prolong the QTc interval inhibit hERG channel and the IKr current. Therefore,
clinical experience to date in terms of QTc monitoring, level of risk relative to QTc interval
prolongation, and the combined effect of the polypharmacy of QTc prolonging medications on
the QTc interval are based on inhibition of IKr. It remains unclear how much of that experience
can be extrapolated to IKs inhibitors. At least two other approved drugs have shown inhibition
of the IKs current, arsenic trioxide and indapamide. Arsenic trioxide blocks both the Ikr and the
Iks currents and is well-known for its cardiac side effects including QT prolongation, Torsade de
Pointes, and sudden death. Given the dual blockade noted for arsenic trioxide, it is hard to
extrapolate the contribution toward the overall risk from IKs inhibition. Indapamide is a blood
pressure medication first approved in 1983 with dual thiazide type diuretic effects and direct
with vasodilatory effects. While preclinical studies have shown the IKs current inhibition and
case reports have described episodes of Torsade de Pointes in patients taking indapamide, it
seems to have been generally well tolerated, and the current USPI does not note a risk of QT
prolongation or TdP.
Insight concerning blockade of the IKs current is informed by patients with Long QT syndrome 1
(LQT1), a genetic condition where mutations in the channel responsible for IKs impair its
311
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
function. These patients have diminished shortening of the QT interval when the heart rate
increases and are at increased risk of TdP during times of elevated heart rate and when
recovering from elevated heart rate. Expert consensus recommendations for these patients
include avoidance of QT prolonging medications, identification and correction of electrolyte
abnormalities, and the use of beta blockers. While the first two recommendations were
incorporated into the quizartinib development program, the use of beta-blocker therapy to
treat acquired forms of Long QT syndrome is not standard. Based on the risk of greater QT
interval prolongation with heart rate increases, we recommend a post-marketing requirement
for an exercise test study in healthy volunteers and patients to evaluate the impact of heart
rate on the cardiac safety of quizartinib. The patient study should include patients on betablockers to observe for mitigating effects on the incidence of quizartinib-related adverse
reactions.
This preclinical evidence portended the increased rate of QTc prolonged AEs reported in the
above safety analysis in both the newly diagnosed patients and in the overall ISS population.
Indeed, dedicated response analysis of changes in QTcF during the continuation phase showed
dose dependent increase in the change in QTcF during this phase of treatment. Two caveats to
this analysis are that patients must have tolerated quizartinib through two prior phases of
treatment to reach the continuation phase, selecting out those patients that could not tolerate
quizartinib or had extreme QTcF responses to quizartinib. Secondly, dosing during this phase of
treatment was dependent on the change of QTcF. Therefore, these results do not reflect the
response that would be expected from a population randomly assigned to either dose level but
rather what might be expected if dose escalation was dependent on change in QTcF and after
disregarding patients that could not tolerate quizartinib treatment. The trend suggests
diminishing elevation of QTcF for the marginal increase in dose, when this trend reflects the
selection of patients to the higher dose that had less increase in QTcF change with the lower
dose.
We agree with the Applicant that most subjects Study AC220-A-U302 were on at least one QTc
prolonging medication at some time during the study. Fluconazole, ondansetron, levofloxacin
and ciprofloxacin were used in more than 10% of subjects. The Applicant investigated this
pharmacodynamic drug interaction on the QTc interval in their population exposure-response
analysis and concluded that concomitant use of QTc prolonging drugs with quizartinib did not
further increase the QTc interval. These negative findings are not persuasive because the
analysis has many limitations (e.g., Applicant lumped all QTc prolonging drugs together without
accounting for potency and dosage) and the sensitivity to detect an interaction was not
demonstrated. Therefore, the effects of coadministration of QTc prolongation drugs that are IKr
blockers with quizartinib have not been characterized.
Risk of Torsade de Pointes and Cardiac Arrest
As noted above, there were no reported cases of Torsade by PT analysis in Study AC220-A-U302
study. However, there were two cases of cardiac arrest on the quizartinib arm, one grade 4 and
312
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
one grade 5. There were no reported cases of cardiac arrest or Torsade de Pointes on the
placebo arm.
The Grade 4 event occurred in 63 year old female patient (Subject ID
) who
developed ventricular fibrillation and cardiac arrest on day 5 of quizartinib treatment. The
patient had personal history of Long QT syndrome diagnosed years prior to developing AML
and had a pretreatment QTcF of 463. She was started at a reduced dose of quizartinib of 20
mg daily due to being on a strong CYP3A inhibitor Posaconazole (which is also a QT
prolonging medication). During these days, the patient was also placed on standing
furosemide, which induced severe hypokalemia to 2.4 by the time of the cardiac arrest. She
was cardioverted and had resumption of spontaneous circulation. QTcF after the event was
reported as 587 ms. Tracing of the VF were not available for FDA review. She was
permanently discontinued from study treatment.
(b) (6)

FDA comment: This patient should have been excluded from the study due to her personal
history of Long QT syndrome and pretreatment QTcF being above the 450ms cutoff. However,
even with these risk factors, we see how concomitant QTc prolonging medications and severe
iatrogenic hypokalemia increased the chances of this event.
The Grade 5 event occurred in a 54 year old female patient (Subject
) who
developed cardiac arrest on day 79 of treatment. The subject was not in remission after a
first cycle of induction and underwent a second induction cycle followed by quizartinib. It
was noted during this second quizartinib cycle that treatment was interrupted on day 72 for
prolonged QTc of 493 but resumed the next day (day 73) at a reduced dose of 30 mg (40>30mg). On study day 78 the patient was noted to have severe hypokalemia with a
potassium of 2.6. Quizartinib dosing was not held and was continued with potassium
supplementation (including IV potassium). The next day (day 79), the patient was reported
in good clinical condition at 12:30 but had sudden cardiac arrest at 14:00. FDA review of the
tracing from the event was consistent with Torsade de Pointes. The medical team was
unable to restore normal rhythm, and the patient passed away. Other QTc prolonging
medications the patient was taking at the time of the event included ciprofloxacin, linezolid,
and piperacillin/tazobactam.
(b) (6)

FDA comment: Per the narrative, the patient had resolution of her initial QTc prolongation to a
QTc 407 the day prior (day 78) to her cardiac arrest. This is despite reports that her serum
potassium that day was severely low at 2.6.
The common features of these two cases include middle aged women, recovering from
induction chemotherapy, exposure to several QTc prolonging medications, and severe
hypokalemia. Avoiding QTc prolonging mediations and severe hypokalemia appear to be two
313
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
factors that can be addressed to mitigate the clinical risk of ventricular arrhythmia and cardiac
arrest.
While the Applicant contends that the rate of QTc prolongation was manageable and
asymptomatic, they do not satisfactorily address that 2/265 (0.8%) of quizartinib-treated
patients had a cardiac arrest during the first phase of treatment. FDA and the Applicant both
agree that additional factors including history of long QT syndrome, hypokalemia, and
concomitant QTc prolonging medications contributed to these two events. However, FDA views
this as disconcerting since it suggests that Study Investigators, despite protocol warnings and
contraindications about the risk of quizartinib treatment, still enrolled and treated a patient
with a history of Long QT syndrome, continued to prescribe concomitant QTc prolonging
medications with quizartinib and prolonged QTc, and continued quizartinib treatment despite
patients having severe hypokalemia (potassium levels <3 µM). Moreover, these events are
potentially fatal arrythmias that should be prevented from occurring. Once a patient develops
TdP, treatment is challenging even in the inpatient hospital setting. Also, as noted earlier,
although FLT3-ITD mutant AML is serious life-threatening disease, the availability of an
alternative FDA approved FLT3 inhibitor for this indication and line of therapy necessitates that
appropriate precaution be taken with the choice of patient and with respect to quizartinibspecific patient management. Lastly, these events occurred in younger patients during their
initial treatment with intention to cure. This means that possibly preventable deaths that occur
might be in patients that would otherwise be long-term survivors. Therefore, we conclude that
active measures to ensure provider education of these factors, including absolute
contraindications, temporary contraindications, unique mechanism of action, and patient
monitoring guidelines are necessary for the safe and effective use of quizartinib outside the
intensive monitoring of a clinical trial.
Lastly, in order to track the incidence of severe and fatal cardiac arrhythmias in patients treated
with quizartinib, with its unique mechanism of QT interval prolongation, we recommend a postmarketing requirement for a prospective, observational study to further assess the risk.

a

The Applicant’s Position:
In Study AC220-A-U302, 91 subjects (34.3%) in the quizartinib arm and 73 subjects (27.2%) in
the placebo arm experienced hepatic TEAEs in the reviewed SMQs. Most of the events
identified were non-serious and none resulted in a fatal outcome see (Table 108).
Treatment-emergent AEs and serious AEs in the quizartinib arm were mainly liver function test
(LFT) abnormalities and had alternative/contributing factors (such as sepsis, baseline LFT
abnormalities, concomitant hepatotoxic medication, and autoimmune hemolytic anemia),
improved on continued study drug administration, or had no recurrence on rechallenge. No
serious cases of hepatic failure or toxic hepatitis were observed in Study AC220-A-U302
(ISS Table 4.1.30).
The incidence of TESAEs of hepatic failure or drug-induced liver injury was low in the All AML
Pool. The reported cases of drug-induced liver injury, hepatic failure, or acute hepatic failure
had alternative etiologies and risk factors, including sepsis, cardiac failure, AML progression
with liver involvement, and GVHD.
In Study AC220-A-U302, elevations of AT were mostly limited to ≥3 to <10 × ULN and occurred
in numerically higher percentages of subjects in the quizartinib arm than in the placebo arm.
Elevations of TBL ≥2 × ULN occurred in similar percentages of subjects in both treatment arms.
Similar percentages of subjects in the quizartinib and placebo arms had concurrent AT ≥3 × ULN
with TBL ≥2 × ULN (see Table 107).
The LFT abnormalities in the 30 to 60 mg group of the All AML Pool were consistent with those
observed in Study AC220-A-U302 (see Table 107). Overall, subjects with concurrent AT ≥3 ×
ULN and TBL ≥2 × ULN had additional contributing factors identified, most frequently cosuspect concomitant medications, severe infection or sepsis, AML disease progression or GVHD,
or had combination liver enzyme elevations that were mild and transient, resolved on
continued study drug administration, or did not reoccur upon rechallenge. There were no

notable differences in findings across the dose groups. An evaluation of these subjects does
not reveal a pattern of liver injury associated with quizartinib therapy.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. FDA independently screened (both broadly and
narrowly) for Hy’s law cases in the laboratory data from Study AC220-A-U302. We found that
subjects that met criteria for screening had transient elevations of LFTs that resolved either
without change in study drug dosing or with study drug interruption. No patients on the
quizartinib arm of the study had to discontinue study drug due to elevated LFTs alone whereas
one patient on the placebo arm discontinued study drug for hyperbilirubinemia.

8.2.5.4 Differentiation Syndrome and Acute Febrile Neutrophilic Dermatosis
Of the 1081 subjects treated with quizartinib in the All AML Pool, 2 subjects (0.2%; both in the
>60 mg group) had investigator-reported TEAEs of differentiation syndrome (DS) in the setting
of R/R AML (Table 110).
Data from Study AC220-007 in subjects with R/R AML using the FDA criteria (Norsworthy, 2020)
for DS identified 67 (27.8%) quizartinib-treated subjects and 34 of the 94 subjects (36.2%)
treated with salvage chemotherapy. On Applicant medical review, no events were assessed as
definite or probable DS in Study AC220-007. A total of 12 quizartinib-treated subjects (5%)
were identified as having possible DS based on the clinical presentation, though a clear
alternative etiology may have been present. None had a fatal outcome due to possible DS. No
medical review of salvage chemotherapy cases was done.
No events of DS were reported in subjects with newly diagnosed AML from Study AC220-AU302 (Table 110).
Using the same FDA’s algorithmic approach for identification of potential cases of DS, 76 of the
265 subjects (28.7%) were identified from the AC220-A-U302 study dataset. Based on the
Applicant review, all the events identified from the search were assessed as ‘not a DS’. Of note,
79 of the 268 subjects (29.5%) in the placebo arm of Study AC220-A-U302 were also identified
as having potential DS using the FDA’s algorithm. Cases in placebo-treated subjects were not
further evaluated.

Source: ISS Table 4.1.24

The Applicant’s Position:
Quizartinib can cause terminal differentiation of AML blast cells (Sexauer, 2012), which can
potentially lead to the development of DS in subjects with R/R AML receiving quizartinib
monotherapy.
DS is dependent on differentiation of blasts cells and as such is considered unlikely to occur in
subjects with newly diagnosed AML receiving induction and consolidation chemotherapy,
where rapid disappearance of myeloid blasts occurs. Data from the AC220-A-U302 study has
not identified any occurrence of differentiation in quizartinib-treated subjects.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA reviewed the possible cases of DS identified from Study AC220-A-U302 and agreed that
they had alternative etiologies that were explained by the clinical context (culture positive
infections, neutropenic fever responding to empiric antibiotics, etc.) and responded quickly to
alternative treatments or were confirmed to be from alternative causes. FDA notes that in the
R/R setting, there were cases of DS identified by investigators and by screening of Study AC220007 for possible DS cases (12 possible cases noted above). Moreover, there were 20 cases of
acute febrile neutrophilic dermatosis identified in the ISS population but not within Study
AC220-A-U302.
Of note, FDA disagrees with the Applicant that there were no fatal cases of DS on Study AC220007. FDA adjudicated 3 cases of possible DS that contributed to the patients’ death and these
cases are detailed below.
•

USUBJID
was a 62-year-old male who met Montesinos criteria on D1
(on 30 mg quizartinib) when he presented with G1 febrile neutropenia and then G1

hypotension on D4, with no stop date. Initially treated with antibiotics, he subsequently
presented with neutropenic sepsis on D9 which was also treated with antibiotics; a blood
culture was positive for e. faecium on D8 supporting an infectious etiology for these
symptoms, but the neutropenic sepsis worsened on D23, when he developed multiple skin
lesions which were erythematous and painful. Of note a CT on D20 showed “diffuse swelling
of the subcutaneous tissues and platysma muscle on the left side of the jaw but no discrete
collection or mass lesion, which could mean inflammatory or infectious process.” On D24 a
CXR showed fluid overload and the patient developed hypoxemia, treated with diuretics. On
D25 his PICC line was removed as it was thought to be the source of the skin lesions.
Quizartinib was interrupted on D28 due to sepsis, but it was restarted on D37 at a reduced
dose (20 mg). On D46 the patient was noted to be hemodynamically stable with improving
inflammatory markers, and quizartinib was reescalated to 30 mg; the patient subsequently
deteriorated (unknown date) with sepsis, arm pain, swelling, and an MRI showing myositis
of the right abductor muscles, as well as fast AFib with refractory blood pressure, noted in
the narrative to be due to electrolyte abnormality. The quizartinib was discontinued on D55,
and on D64 the patient died of neutropenic sepsis. The patient’s WBC count decreased from
>293000 at screening to 500 at the end of treatment, the blast count decreased from
>287000 at screening to undetectable at the end of treatment, and the ANC peaked at 320
on D45, without much change throughout the treatment course. Also, on D30 the BM
showed no blasts, but a note showed that “there are a number of nucleated cells in the
trails which are mainly neutrophils.” The patient was treated with antibiotics and supportive
care, but no steroids.
Reviewer comment: Although it is possible that all of the symptoms above were due to infection,
the combination of fever, fluid overload, myositis and hypotension in the setting of
disappearance of blasts and a BM with predominantly neutrophils, that improved with
quizartinib interruption and dose reduction, and rapidly deteriorated with dose re-escalation,
and was not treated with steroids, must be considered at least possibly a case on the DS/AFND
spectrum due to the quizartinib. The restarting and dose-escalation of quizartinib and lack of
steroid administration in this case highlight the fact that an event on this spectrum was not in
the differential diagnosis for practitioners treating patients with quizartinib.

(b) (6)

Reviewer comment: The associated dyspnea, pyrexia and AFND in the setting of decreasing
blasts raise the possibility that this is a fatal event on the DS/AFND spectrum.
FDA agrees with Applicant’s supposition that DS is unlikely to occur within the treatment
regimen under consideration for newly diagnosed patients. This is likely due to debulking effect
of induction chemotherapy and the relative resistance of AML cells remaining after induction
chemotherapy to undergo differentiation from a 14-day course of quizartinib. FDA included DS
and AFND in labeling since these were serious events that might occur if quizartinib is used
outside of the indicated population. Labeling noted that these occurred within development
program but in a different clinical setting.
Clinical Outcome Assessment (COA) Analyses Informing Safety/Tolerability
Data:
321
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Refer to Section 8.1.2 for PRO results.
The Applicant’s Position:
Notwithstanding the caveats with respect to interpretation of the data discussed previously,
the subscales relevant for safety and tolerability included are fatigue and nausea/vomiting,
where improvement was noticeable in the Continuation Phase for both subscales. The results
for fatigue and nausea/vomiting suggest no detrimental impact from addition of quizartinib to
standard-of-care chemotherapy for newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that both nausea/vomiting and fatigue as
components of PRO results were improved from baseline. However, we note that the results
suggested greater PRO scale improvement with placebo than with quizartinib. Interestingly, this
difference is reflected in the higher rate of nausea (23% quizartinib vs. 9% placebo) and
vomiting (15% quizartinib vs. 8%) reported as TEAEs in quizartinib-treated patients during the
continuation phase than in the placebo treated patients (Table 95). However, fatigue reported
as a TEAE during this phase showed no difference (13% quizartinib vs. 13% placebo). This
suggests that PRO data might better capture patient fatigue than fatigue reported as a TEAE.
Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
Data:
Subgroup analyses of TEAEs by intrinsic factors (eg, age, sex, race, and ethnicity) and extrinsic
factors (eg, geographic region and concomitant medications) were performed for Study
AC220-A-U302 and the All AML Pool.
The analyses according to race, ethnicity, and geographic region were hampered by the limited
number of subjects in some of the subgroups (Black/African American, Hispanic/Latino), but in
general, the type and incidence of the TEAEs most frequently reported across the overall Safety
Analysis Set (gastrointestinal disorders, infections, pyrexia, cytopenias, skin disorders,
hypokalemia, ALT increased, and ECG QT prolonged) were similar in the different subgroups
(ISS Table 4.1.15).
There were some notable findings in subgroup analyses observed for QT interval prolongation,
with the TEAE of QT prolongation reported more frequently in females compared with males,
higher percentages of females than males who had QTcF >450 ms and QTcF >480 ms, and
increases in QTcF >30 ms or >60 ms in the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302. However,
no differences in QTcF >500 ms were observed between males and females in either treatment
arm (Table 10.27, CSR AC220-A-U302, eCTD 5.3.5.1).
Similarly, concomitant use of strong CYP3A inhibitor was shown to be associated with a higher
incidence of QTcF prolongation on ECG in Study AC220-A-U302 and the 30 to 60 mg group of
the All AML Pool.
322
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
In contrast, age appeared not to be a factor impacting the incidence of QT interval prolongation
based on reported TEAE or ECG data, with no clear trend for increased incidence with increased
age in Study AC220-A-U302 and the 30 to 60 mg dose group of the All AML Pool (ISS Table
7.1.2).
A subgroup analysis by use of QT-prolonging medication and by duration of treatment showed
that concomitant administration of QT-prolonging medications had no significant impact on the
incidence of QTcF prolongation (Module 2.7.4, Section 4.2). The incidence of QTcF
prolongation (>450 ms and change from baseline >60 ms) appeared to increase with longer
treatment duration, although this is likely a feature of event accrual during the longer follow-up
time in subjects with longer treatment duration.
As summarized in Section 19.4.3.4, the C-QTcF E-R analysis showed that age, daunorubicin,
hypokalemia, and calcium levels but not sex, body weight, or concomitant QT-prolonging
medications were as identified as significant covariates.
The Applicant’s Position:
Overall, the safety data with quizartinib were generally similar across different age, sex, and
concomitant use of strong CYP3A inhibitor subgroups in Study AC220-A-U302 and the 30 to
60 mg dose group of the All AML Pool. With the limitations outlined above, the type and
incidence of the TEAEs most frequently reported were generally similar in the different
subgroups of race, ethnicity, and geographic region.
Although some numerical differences in the incidence of some categories of TEAEs (eg, TESAEs,
TEAEs associated with death as outcome, and TEAEs associated with study drug discontinuation
or interruption) were observed these were assessed as not indicative of clinically meaningful
differences in the safety profile of quizartinib.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position. The FDA notes that multiple studies have indicated
that females are more susceptible to acquired long QT syndrome.

Additional Safety Explorations
Human Carcinogenicity or Tumor Development
Data:
No human carcinogenicity studies have been conducted.
The Applicant’s Position:
In Study AC220-A-U302, 7 subjects (2.6%) in the quizartinib arm and 8 subjects (3.0%) in the
placebo arm experienced a TEAE in the MedDRA SOC Neoplasms benign, malignant and
unspecified. No individual PT was reported by >1 subject in the quizartinib arm.
No specific trend could be identified in the nature of the neoplastic events reported from the
study, and based on the available information, no evidence of causal association with
quizartinib could be identified (ISS Table 4.1.3).
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. Events were balanced between arms and without
clear trend to occurrence.
Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
Data:
Women of childbearing potential were required to have a negative serum pregnancy test upon
entry into Study AC220-A-U302 and required to use highly effective birth control as defined in
the clinical study protocol upon enrollment, during the Treatment Period and for 6 months
following the last dose of study drug. Men were required to be surgically sterile or willing to
use highly effective birth control as defined in the clinical study protocol upon enrollment,
during the Treatment Period, and for 6 months following the last dose of study drug. There was
1 case of pregnancy in the placebo arm of Study AC220-A-U302 and no lactation-related events
were reported in the quizartinib development program (see Table 21: Subject Disposition in
Study AC220-A-U302).
The Applicant’s Position:
In an embryo-fetal development study in rats, fetotoxicity (lower fetal weights and effects on
skeletal ossification) and teratogenicity (high incidence of fetal malformations) were observed.
However, no clinical studies have been conducted that assess the use of quizartinib during
324
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
pregnancy or breastfeeding. While one case of pregnancy was reported in Study AC220-AU302, this case occurred on the placebo arm; no pregnancies have been reported in quizartinibtreated subjects in the clinical development program.
There are no human data on the effect of quizartinib on fertility. Based on findings in animals,
female and male fertility may be impaired with treatment with quizartinib.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment that embryo-fetal toxicity was observed in animal
studies and is a potential risk to humans. Therefore, embryo-fetal toxicity has been included in
warnings and precautions section of the USPI. Lastly, impaired fertility was observed in animal
studies and this information was also included in the USPI.
Pediatrics and Assessment of Effects on Growth
The Applicant’s Position:
Quizartinib is currently being investigated in the Phase 1/2 pediatric study, AC220-A-U202, in
R/R FLT3-ITD (+)AML.
Study AC220-A-U202 (NCT03793478) is ongoing to evaluate the safety, PK, PD, and efficacy of
quizartinib in combination with fludarabine and cytarabine (FLA) chemotherapy for re-induction
chemotherapy, and as a single agent continuation therapy in pediatric R/R FLT3-ITD (+) AML
subjects aged ≥1 month old to 18 years old (and young adults up to 21 years old). As of
September 2022, 9 subjects (aged between 3 and 17 years old) have been treated in the Phase
1 dose-escalation cohort with quizartinib 40 mg/m2 if body surface area (BSA) <1.5 m2, or 60 mg
if BSA ≥1.5 m2. There were no deaths during treatment; 4 subjects died during the long-term
follow-up (3 due to disease progression and 1 due to GVHD after bone marrow transplant). A
DLT occurred in 1 subject: delayed ANC and platelet recovery. TESAEs were reported by 7
subjects; none of the reported TESAE were fatal. Apart from SAEs of sepsis and febrile
neutropenia, which were both reported by 2 subjects each, the remaining events were
reported by single subjects and included: empyema, streptococcal sepsis, bilirubin conjugated
increased, sinus tachycardia, seizure, dyspnea, hypoxia, pulmonary edema, distributive shock,
herpes zoster reactivation, and anal fissure. Two subjects discontinued study treatment
because of an AE (ECG QT prolonged and thrombocytopenia). The most common Grade ≥3
TEAEs were blood cytopenias. No subjects experienced QTcF >500 ms, and 1 subject had QTcF
>480 ms.
No new safety signals have been detected and the safety profile is consistent with the known
safety profile from quizartinib experience in adult subjects.
Safety and PK continue to be evaluated to determine the recommended Phase 2 for the
expansion cohort (≥1 to ≤21 years) and the dose-escalation cohort for the younger age cohort
(≥1 month to <1 year old).

{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
The effects of quizartinib on growth have not been assessed and currently remains unknown.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. Study AC220-A-U302 is ongoing. The effects of
quizartinib on growth have not been assessed. Safety results in the 9 subjects treated to date
are comparable to the those observed in adult studies.
Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
Data:
Cumulatively, there have been 3 reports of accidental overdose in the All AML Pool, which were
reported as SAEs (ISS Table 4.1.11). Symptoms associated with overdose included vomiting, but
there were no effects on QTcF interval duration.
The Applicant’s Position:
No studies have been conducted that assess drug overdose, and there is limited clinical
experience with quizartinib overdose. Repeated doses up to 450 mg have been clinically
evaluated in subjects, and the DLT was prolongation of the QT interval. There are no specific
antidotes to quizartinib. For substantial overdose, supportive measures should be provided as
necessary, with the evaluation of hematology and ECG monitoring as well as attention to serum
electrolytes and concomitant medications that may predispose to QT interval prolongation
and/or TdP. Patients should be managed with symptomatic and supportive care.
No studies have been conducted that assess drug abuse. However, given the nature of
quizartinib, drug abuse is not anticipated. No studies have been conducted that assess
withdrawal or rebound effects of quizartinib. However, given the target patient population and
the nature of quizartinib, no such effects are anticipated.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. While higher exposure to the drug would be
expected to induce higher rates of adverse events, the fact that the development program
tested doses 10-fold greater than the proposed dose in the induction and consolidation setting
provides a measure of assurance that accidental overdose within this range of exposure would
at least be tolerated temporarily. We agree that supportive care and attention to electrolyte
and ECG monitoring would be appropriate based on recommended labeling in general. Lastly,
we concur that given the mechanism of action, adverse event profile, and the lack of euphoric
effects from use that quizartinib abuse is not anticipated.

Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
326
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Data:
Quizartinib was approved by the MHLW in Japan in June 2019 for the treatment of patients
with R/R FLT3-ITD (+) AML, as detected by an MHLW-approved assay based on the improved
response rates and OS in R/R FLT3-ITD (+) AML subjects in a randomized Phase 3 study, referred
to as Study AC220-007 (QuANTUM-R; Cortes, 2019).
Cumulatively as of 28 Oct 2021, an estimated 228 patients have received quizartinib in the
postmarketing setting, all of whom were in Japan. The safety profile of quizartinib in the
postmarketing setting is consistent with the safety profile as established in the development
program, and no new safety concerns have been identified. Reported SAEs were mostly events
related to AML disease progression and cytopenias (neutrophil count decreased, and febrile
neutropenia). Events related to AML disease progression were also the most common events
associated with fatal outcome. Reported AEs of ECG QT prolonged were mostly non-serious
and not associated with any ventricular arrhythmia events (eCTD 2.7.4, Section 8).
The Applicant’s Position:
Review of post marketing safety data did not reveal any new safety concerns and the reported
events were generally consistent with the safety profile established in the quizartinib
development program as summarized above.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the post marketing safety data from to date.
Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Data:
The safety and tolerability profile of quizartinib has been adequately characterized for use in
the intended population in Study AC220-A-U302. The safety profile seen is also consistent with
that observed throughout the quizartinib clinical development program as evidenced by data
for the 30 to 60 mg dose level in the All AML Pool. QT interval prolongation and the associated
potential risk of ventricular arrhythmias and blood cytopenias and infections are the main
safety risks for quizartinib in patients with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML. The risk of QT
prolongation/ventricular arrhythmia has been adequately managed with the risk mitigation
procedures implemented in Study AC220-A-U302, which comprise ECG monitoring for QT
prolongation, QTcF-based quizartinib dose interruption and reduction, and correction of
additional risk factors (eg, monitoring for and correction of electrolyte abnormalities,
quizartinib dose modification for concomitant strong CYP3A inhibitor use) and more frequent
monitoring of ECG during concomitant therapy with known QT prolongation.
The Applicant’s Position:
327
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Management of myelosuppression and associated risk of infections and bleeding is part of the
routine clinical practice for the treatment of patients with newly diagnosed AML. In the study,
events associated with myelosuppression could be appropriately managed by transfusion and
growth factor support, as well as through protocol-specified study drug dose modifications.
Overall, the majority of TEAEs of blood cytopenias were managed with study drug interruptions
or dose reductions, and few subjects discontinued quizartinib due to these events.
These measures are expected to adequately mitigate the risks conveyed in the proposed label
and are readily implemented within standard medical practice for the treatment of newly
diagnosed AML. The risk minimization measures for the risk of QT prolongation/ventricular
arrhythmias in the quizartinib label will be supplemented by additional communication to
prescribers and patients/caregivers.
Safety risks beyond those included in the proposed draft label are not anticipated. The risks of
DDIs with strong CYP3A inhibitors, strong and moderate CYP3A inducers, embryo-fetal toxicity,
and female and male fertility are also adequately conveyed in the proposed labeling.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA does not fully agree with the Applicant’s assessment. While the FDA does anticipate that
the most common AEs related to GI toxicity, cytopenia, and infections are standard in the
clinical setting of acute leukemia, the FDA has concluded that additional measures must be
implemented to further minimize the risk of ventricular arrhythmias in quizartinib-treated
patients. The Applicant has proposed a communication plan REMS to assist with provider and
patient education concerning this risk. However, the FDA clinical team concluded that
additional active and required education measures should be implemented to ensure the safe
use of quizartinib in clinical practice (i.e., REMS with Elements to Assure Safe Use [ETASU]).
Integrated Assessment of Safety
Data:
See data in Sections 8.2.4 and 8.2.5.
In the pivotal study, in both treatment arms, more than half of the deaths during the study
were attributed to AML disease progression or to AEs that were considered secondary to AML.
More early deaths (ie, deaths within 60 days of the initiation of study drug) occurred with
quizartinib compared with placebo. In total, 20 subjects (7.5%) and 13 subjects (4.9%),
respectively died within 60 days of the study drug initiation, of which 15 subjects (5.7%) and
9 subjects (3.4%), respectively, died within 30 days of study drug initiation. In both treatment
arms, the majority of early deaths were due to AEs (Table 72).
Treatment-emergent AEs associated with death as outcome occurred in 30 subjects (11.3%) in
the quizartinib arm and 26 subjects (9.7%) in the placebo arm during the overall study period.
In both treatment arms, the most frequently reported TEAEs with an outcome of death
328
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
(including early deaths) were due to infections, which occurred mainly in the Induction and
Consolidation Phases and were more common in the quizartinib arm (Table 74). Infections are a
known risk for subjects with AML receiving induction and consolidation chemotherapy.
More subjects in the quizartinib arm of Study AC220-A-U302 experienced TEAEs associated with
study drug discontinuation compared with placebo (54 subjects [20.4%] and 23 subjects [8.6%],
respectively). The most frequently reported TEAEs associated with discontinuation of
quizartinib were infections (septic shock, sepsis, and pneumonia) and cytopenias
(thrombocytopenia, neutropenia, and febrile neutropenia). In the placebo arm, the most
common TEAE associated with discontinuation were also infections (pneumonia).
The most frequently reported TEAEs in both treatment arms of Study AC220-A-U302 were
gastrointestinal disorders (diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting), infections and infestations
(pneumonia and sepsis), general disorders and administration site conditions (pyrexia, oedema
peripheral, and fatigue), and blood and lymphatic system disorders (febrile neutropenia,
neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, and anemia).
Among the most commonly reported TEAEs (≥20%), the events of nausea, vomiting, febrile
neutropenia, neutropenia, and headache, occurred more frequently (in ≥2% more subjects) in
the quizartinib arm than in the placebo arm.
The Applicant’s Position:
Safety data from Study AC220-A-U302 were consistent with the safety profile of quizartinib as
established over the prior development program and no new safety concerns were identified.
The most common types of TEAEs are those commonly seen in subjects with newly diagnosed
AML receiving standard intensive chemotherapy and generally occurred at comparable rates in
the quizartinib and placebo arms. Blood cytopenias (neutropenia, thrombocytopenia, and
anemia) and infections were the most common severe and serious TEAEs and the most
common categories of events associated with quizartinib discontinuation, interruption, and
dose reduction.
The incidence of TEAEs associated with fatal outcome was low, and the most common fatal
TEAEs (including those occurring within 30 and 60 days of the start of quizartinib) were
infections, which are a known risk for subjects with AML receiving induction and consolidation
chemotherapy.
There was no clinically relevant variability of the safety profile across subpopulations and no
evidence of increasing toxicity with long-term treatment with quizartinib for up to 36 cycles
(3 years). No new event types were observed among subjects receiving quizartinib >12 cycles
(12 months), and the most frequently reported TEAEs occurred at similar incidence rates
regardless of length of treatment duration, with the exception of blood disorders, which
increased with longer treatment duration. Quizartinib was not associated with clinically
apparent drug-induced liver injury.
329
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
Quizartinib causes QT interval prolongation in a dose-dependent and exposure-dependent
manner, and QT interval prolongation on ECG was commonly observed in Study AC220-A-U302.
However, most of the QT prolongation events were of mild or moderate severity and were
manageable by quizartinib dose modification and correction of additional risk factors (eg,
monitoring for and correction of electrolyte abnormalities, quizartinib dose modification for
QTcF prolongation, and for concomitant strong CYP3A inhibitor use) and more frequent
monitoring of ECG during concomitant therapy with known QT prolongation. QT prolongation
is a risk factor for the occurrence of ventricular arrythmias, including TdP, which were observed
at very low incidence at the recommended doses of quizartinib and generally occurred in
subjects with other significant risk factors for cardiac arrhythmia. This further demonstrates
that the clinical impact of QT prolongation can be appropriately managed with QTc-based dose
modifications, correction of electrolyte abnormalities, and dose adjustments for concomitant
use with strong CYP3A inhibitors.
As a substrate of CYP3A, quizartinib should not be administered concomitantly with strong or
moderate CYP3A inducers.
Although no data in humans are available that assess the use of quizartinib during pregnancy or
breastfeeding or its effect on fertility, nonclinical safety data indicate that quizartinib can be
associated with fetotoxicity and teratogenicity if administered during pregnancy and that
impaired fertility could occur in treated patients.
Overall, QT interval prolongation and the associated potential risk of ventricular arrhythmias
and blood cytopenias and infections are the main safety risks for quizartinib in subjects with
newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML. Results from Study AC220-A-U302 have demonstrated an
acceptable safety and tolerability profile of quizartinib in the target population when given in
combination with standard induction and consolidation chemotherapy and then continued as
monotherapy for up to 36 cycles with the proposed dosing regimen.
The safety data across the quizartinib development program in subjects with AML are
consistent with the findings from the pivotal study AC220-A-U302. Apart from DS, which is
considered a potential risk for quizartinib monotherapy for the treatment of patients with R/R
AML, but is not anticipated to occur in patients with newly diagnosed AML receiving quizartinib
in combination with chemotherapy, the safety profile of quizartinib is consistent across
different AML indications and patient populations. Similarly, available post marketing safety
data for quizartinib in patients with R/R AML have not revealed any additional safety concerns.
The safety results and efficacy observed in Study AC220-A-U302 establish that quizartinib is an
effective and well-tolerated treatment option when combined with standard chemotherapy
then as continuation monotherapy, for adults with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML.
Therefore, the Applicant concludes that the data from Study AC220-A-U302 support a positive
overall benefit-risk profile for quizartinib for the proposed indication.

Total deaths were higher on the placebo arm than the quizartinib arm, which is reflective of the
OS analysis. On-treatment deaths and grade 5 TEAEs were similar between arms but slightly

higher on the quizartinib arm, suggesting increased toxicity with quizartinib treatment.
Increased toxicity is also reflected in the higher rate of TEAEs with discontinuation and serious
TEAEs seen on the quizartinib arm. However, the rate of all-grade TEAEs and Grade 3-5 TEAEs
(90% vs 92%) was essentially the same between arms. The increase in toxicity over the placebo
arm is not unexpected for an add-on trial design where the backbone therapy is known to carry
significant toxicity.
In the ISS AML population, total deaths, on-treatment deaths, Grade 5 TEAEs, and serious TEAEs
were higher, likely reflecting the higher acuity of the R/R AML patients; although, higher
quizartinib exposure could have contributed. The rate of TEAEs with discontinuation, however,
was similar.
The safety profile of the most common TEAEs in Study AC220-A-U302 were generally consistent
with those that occur in AML patients undergoing intensive induction and consolidation
chemotherapy treatment. It is notable that only a subset of the most frequently occurring
TEAEs were increased on the quizartinib arm with others being the same between both arms or
higher on the placebo arm. The general category for these common TEAEs increased over
placebo were GI toxicity, cytopenias, and infections. It is important to note, as seen by the
results of the placebo arm, that these events are common in the clinical context and clinicians
that treat AML are experienced with their general management.
Analysis of the safety profile of quizartinib during the maintenance phase of treatment allowed
a comparison between quizartinib and placebo without the high background rate of TEAEs that
occurred with induction and consolidation chemotherapy. As with the first two phases of
treatment, the most common TEAEs that were increased with quizartinib treatment over
placebo were cytopenias, infections, and GI toxicity. Interestingly, when these common TEAEs
were further assessed in patient based on their transplant status, we saw that patients that had
undergone HSCT during consolidation had higher rates of cytopenias and infection TEAEs during
the maintenance phase than patients that patients that had only undergone chemotherapy
consolidation. This showed that maintenance therapy was less well tolerated post-HSCT
consolidation than in patients that had only undergone chemotherapy consolidation.
The toxicity that occurs with quizartinib treatment that is not common in AML or from standard
treatment is QT prolongation and risk of ventricular arrhythmias. Indeed, 2/265 (0.8%) of
quizartinib treated patients had a cardiac arrest during the first phase of treatment on Study
AC220-A-U302. While the Applicant notes that rate of QT prolongation seems manageable
compared to other QT prolonging medications, the risk of cardiac arrest is not accurately
reflected from this rate. FDA has concluded that the novel mechanism of QT prolongation
increases the risk of cardiac arrest beyond the risk that would be otherwise suggested by more
traditional QT prolonging medications (IKr inhibitors). This might be due to concomitant QT
prolonging medications synergizing with quizartinib or to baseline QTc changes incompletely
capturing the risk of ventricular arrythmia for IKs inhibitors. Therefore, FDA concludes that

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
additional risk mitigations measures that ensure clinician education of these unique aspects of
quizartinib mechanism of QT prolongation and mitigation measures are required for safe use in
clinical practice.
Therefore, to mitigate the risks of serious and fatal cardiac toxicities, we recommend a REMS
with ETASU to 1) ensure prescribers are able to identify the unique QT prolonging mechanism
of quizartinib, 2) sure prescribers are able to identify the risk factors that are associated with
Torsades de Pointes and cardiac arrest with quizartinib, and 3) ensure prescribers are able to
identify the importance of providing risk mitigation measures including QTc interval monitoring,
electrolyte monitoring and repletion, avoidance of concomitant QTc prolonging medications,
and dose modifications/dose interruptions when indicated. With these additional safety
measures, FDA agrees with the Applicant that based on the totality of efficacy and safety data,
the benefit/risk profile of quizartinib in the treatment of patients with newly diagnosed FLT3ITD AML is favorable.

SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS

Statistical Issues

The FDA’s Assessment:

QuANTUM-First demonstrated a statistically significant effect on OS, with HR 0.78 (95% CI: 0.62,
0.98). No improvement was demonstrated for the key secondary endpoint of EFS. Of note, the
EFS results are sensitive to choice of window for induction failure (defined per protocol as
failure to achieve CR within 42 days of the start of the last cycle of induction chemotherapy).
Exploratory analyses suggest that long duration of CR is observed with quizartinib (median
duration of CR 38.6 months).
Based on our current analyses of data from QuANTUM-First, we note that patients may not
benefit from quizartinib continuation/maintenance therapy post-HSCT. In an exploratory
subgroup analysis of the 89/208 (43%) of patients who received maintenance therapy with
VANFLYTA or placebo following consolidation chemotherapy, the OS HR was 0.40 (95% CI: 0.19,
0.84). Of 119/208 (57%) of patients who received maintenance therapy with VANFLYTA or
placebo following HSCT, the OS HR was 1.62 (95% CI: 0.62, 4.22). For RFS, the corresponding
hazard ratios were 0.93 (95% CI: 2.00) and 0.71 (95% CI: 0.35, 1.43). As the unadjusted analyses
do not account for the unbalance in post-randomization variables and/or baseline
characteristics between treatment arms among patients who entered Continuation, additional
analyses attempted to account for such features. Results from these adjusted analyses were
similar. Thus, these analyses suggest inconclusive evidence regarding use of quizartinib as
continuation/maintenance therapy after HSCT.
While QuANTUM-First demonstrated a statistically significant effect on OS, questions remain
about which patients in the intended population have a positive benefit-risk. Early OS (<6
months) was lower in the quizartinib arm due in part to increased infections. No single
subgroup appears to be driving this effect. Likewise, it is unclear whether quizartinib
continuation provides any benefit to patients who receive HSCT. Though this conclusion is
based on exploratory post-hoc analyses, a more formal analysis is not possible since QuANTUMFirst was inadequately designed to isolate the effect of continuation therapy. To date, no FLT3
TKI has definitively demonstrated improved efficacy for continued treatment following HSCT
(Levis, 2019).

Conclusions and Recommendations

The FDA’s Assessment:

334
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
The review team was able to verify or clarify the efficacy and safety endpoints as provided by
the Applicant. Substantial evidence of effectiveness was established based on improvement in
overall survival demonstrated on the quizartinib treatment arm of the adequate and well
controlled clinical Study AC220-A-U302 and on the supporting evidence provided by Study
AC220-007 demonstrating achievement of CR+CRh with quizartinib monotherapy in the R/R
setting. The review team noted that while maintenance appeared to benefit patients who had
not undergone HSCT, those that had undergone HSCT did not benefit from quizartinib
maintenance. Ultimately, there was inadequate demonstration of favorable benefit-risk for
quizartinib maintenance post-HSCT. See Sections 1 and 8 for more information. This led to the
limitations of use statement that quizartinib is not indicated as maintenance monotherapy
following allogenic hematopoietic stem cell transplant as improvement in OS with quizartinib in
this setting has not been demonstrated. Lastly, the review team did not find the original
management plan proposed by the Applicant sufficient to mitigate quizartinib-induced QTc
prolongation and the risk of fatal cardiac arrythmia. The review team asked the Applicant to
develop an adequate REMS program to mitigate these risks, which the Applicant assented to at
the end of the review cycle. Review of the REMS program follows below in section 12.

Advisory Committee Meeting and Other External Consultations

The FDA’s Assessment:
Meetings with two separate Special Government Employees (SGEs) were conducted on
February 27, 2023 and March 6, 2023. Noting the lack of statistical significance of the secondary
efficacy endpoints of EFS and CR rate on QuANTUM-First, the lack of an OS benefit in patients
treated with quizartinib maintenance post-HSCT, and the unique risk of QTc prolongation, FDA
requested a discussion of benefit-risk for the proposed indication, as well as the need for
additional education and risk mitigation measures to prevent serious and potentially fatal
arrhythmias. The Agency requested that the SGE comment on the following:
1. Whether quizartinib has a favorable benefit-risk profile for its proposed indication “…in
combination with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and standard cytarabine
consolidation chemotherapy, and as continuation monotherapy following consolidation, for
the treatment of adult patients with newly diagnosed acute myeloid leukemia (AML) that is
FMS-like tyrosine kinase 3 internal tandem duplication (FLT3-ITD) positive as detected by an
FDA-approved test.”
2. If the SGE concludes that the benefit-risk profile is favorable, the Agency requests
commentary on the following items:
a. Whether the benefit-risk profile is favorable for quizartinib continuation
monotherapy following consolidation chemotherapy and/or HSCT.
b. Whether additional education and risk mitigation measures are needed (e.g.,
communication plan, certification of healthcare providers and pharmacists, etc.), in
addition to a boxed warning, to convey information and management guidelines on
quizartinib’s unique, serious, and potentially fatal risk of arrhythmias.
The two SGEs concluded that quizartinib has a favorable benefit-risk for use in combination
with standard cytarabine and anthracycline induction and standard cytarabine consolidation
chemotherapy. Both SGEs, however, noted concerns with the benefit of post-HSCT
maintenance therapy with quizartinib. SGE #1 expressed concerns regarding isolation of the
impact of maintenance without having randomization just prior to initiation of maintenance.
They conveyed that there is no evidence to support the efficacy of post-HSCT maintenance and
only weak evidence of benefit post-consolidation chemotherapy in the absence of HSCT. They
thought, however, that physicians would likely use the product for maintenance in patients
tolerating the drug in the absence of HSCT. SGE #2 noted an unlikely benefit of maintenance
therapy in the post-HSCT setting, but that without HSCT, patients appeared to benefit from
post-consolidation therapy. They noted reservations with over-interpretation of the subgroup
336
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
data, which may lead physicians to prescribe quizartinib the way it was administered on the
study. They encouraged conveying the additional data on the impact of maintenance to
physicians so that they could justify variations from how the study was conducted.
Regarding the unique, serious and potentially fatal risk of arrhythmias, the two SGEs concluded
that the risk demonstrated in the trial and the unique mechanism of QT prolongation
necessitated measures beyond labeling to effectively educate the relevant providers. SGE#1
noted that with number of drugs now reported to prolong the QT interval, that physicians have
become blasé about QT prolongation and would not appreciate the additional risk incurred by
blockade of the slow potassium channel. SGE#2 also noted that required education that clearly
conveys the unique and serious risks of blockage of this alternative potassium channel would be
critical to the safe use of quizartinib. They both noted that a required REMs program that
provided a brief overview of the novel mechanism of action would be essential to mitigate this
potentially fatal risk. The REMS program should also educate the providers on the important
factors common to TdP cases (females, severe hypokalemia, concomitant QT prolonging
medications) in addition to appropriate management of quizartinib when these risks occur.
They both noted that required REMS training for various products have become a requirement
of attending on inpatient leukemia services and that these trainings have not been overly
burdensome.

10 Pediatrics
The Applicant’s Position:
The Applicant and FDA reached agreement on an agreed initial Pediatric Study Plan (Ref. ID
4895995). Per this agreement, the Applicant is including in this application a waiver request for
the conduct of studies in pediatric subjects <1 month with FLT3-ITD (+) AML on the grounds
that such studies would be highly impractical due to the rarity of FLT3-ITD mutation in this age
group. Per the same agreement, the Applicant is including in this application a request for
deferral of submission of results from agreed upon pediatric studies (ie, the study reports) of
quizartinib for all ages ≥1 month because the drug product is anticipated to be ready for
approval for use in adult subjects with FLT3-ITD (+) newly diagnosed AML before the clinical
pediatric studies agreed upon are complete as provided for under 21 U.S.C. 355c(4)(A)(i)(I).
Pursuant to the agreed upon initial Pediatric Study Plan, the Applicant is conducting Study
AC220-A-U202 (NCT03793478) in addition to a modeling and extrapolation study to generate
efficacy data from the adult newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML Study AC220-A-U302 that is
supporting this application.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of their pediatric studies. Study AC220-A-U202 has
been open for several years and has been slowly accruing patients. Despite being ongoing, the
study has not yet achieved its objective to complete assessment of quizartinib in pediatric
patients. Therefore, the Applicant’s request for deferral is permissible. Moreover, FDA agrees
that partial waiver for study of pediatric patients less than one year of age is also permissible.
Lastly, completion and submission of a final CSR for Study AC220-A-U202 will be included as a
PMR for quizartinib approval as detailed in section 13.

12 Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategies (REMS)
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Division of Risk Management (DRM) in the Office of Surveillance and Epidemiology (OSE)
reviewed this Application and concurs with the review team that additional risk evaluation and
mitigation strategies (REMS) are required to ensure the risks of quizartinib can be adequately
managed in the post-marketing setting.
The specific goal of the REMS with elements to assure safe use (ETASU) is to mitigate the risk of
342
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
severe and fatal cardiac arrhythmias by ensuring that prescribers are able to identify the unique
QT prolonging mechanism of quizartinib, risk factors associated with Torsades de Pointes and
cardiac arrest with quizartinib, and the importance of providing risk mitigation measures
including QTc interval monitoring, electrolyte monitoring and repletion, avoidance of
concomitant QTc prolonging medications, and dose modification/dose interruptions when
indicated.
Components of the REMS for quizartinib will include ETASU A (certification of prescribers) and
ETASU B (certification of pharmacies that dispense quizaritnib). Under ETASU A, prescribers
must obtain certification by enrolling and completing training including a knowledge
assessment regarding the risks of cardiac arrhythmias and agree to counsel patients on the risks
and provide them with a patient wallet card. Under ETASU B, pharmacies must be certified and
verify that prescribers are certified before dispending quizartinib.
As part of the REMS, the Sponsor must submit REMS Assessments annually from the date of the
initial approval. Depending on the findings from formal assessment of the REMS, FDA may
modify the REMS or consider other regulatory actions. In the future, if the REMS assessments
and/or data from other sources indicates that prescribers have gained familiarity with the risks
of cardiac arrhythmias with quizartinib and are taking appropriate actions to reduce and
manage the risks, FDA may re-evaluate the REMS to determine if continuation of REMS is
necessary.
Refer to REMS review from DRM for additional details.

13 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
The FDA’s Assessment:
The following PMRs/PMCs will be issued to the Applicant:
1. PMR #1: Conduct a clinical study to confirm the appropriate dose of quizartinib, and to
assess safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of quizartinib in
combination with fludarabine and cytarabine, in pediatric patients ages ≥1 month to <18
years with relapsed/refractory FLT3-ITD positive AML. Patients should be followed for at
least 12 months (52 weeks). Include at least 16 patients ≥1 month to <18 years old,
including a minimum of 4 patients < 12 years old.
2. PMR #2: Conduct an exercise test study in healthy subjects to further evaluate the
serious risk of QTc prolongation with quizartinib. Evaluate the impact of rapid
acceleration in heart rate on the cardiac safety of quizartinib using a standardized test
protocol such as Bruce protocol, modified Bruce protocol, or graded-intensity bicycle
exercise test. Identify QT/QTc and RR intervals at rest, peak exercise, and recovery, and
capture the incidence of arrhythmias and other adverse reactions.
3. PMR #3: Conduct a sub-maximal exercise test study in patients with AML to further
evaluate the serious risk of QTc prolongation with quizartinib. Evaluate the impact of
rapid acceleration in heart rate on the cardiac safety of quizartinib using a 24-Hour
Holter study with sub-maximal exercise (e.g., postural provocation – i.e., supine to
standing). Identify QT/QTc and RR intervals at rest and sub-maximal exercise and
capture the incidence of arrhythmias and other adverse reactions. Include patients on
beta-blockers in order to observe for mitigating effects on the incidence of quizartinibrelated adverse reactions.
4. PMR #4: Conduct a randomized trial of quizartinib maintenance therapy that compares
the recommended dosage of 53 mg daily to a lower dosage (e.g., 26.5 mg daily) to
further characterize serious adverse reactions including but not limited to the rates of
Grade ≥3 adverse reactions, Grade ≥ 3 neutropenia, QTc interval prolongation, and dose
reductions, interruptions, and discontinuations due to adverse reactions and provide an
analysis of dose- and exposure-response relationships for safety. Incorporate
systematically assessed patient-reported outcome assessments to further characterize
safety and tolerability, including patient-reported symptoms, function, and overall side
effect impact. Eligible patients will include newly diagnosed, FLT3-ITD positive AML
patients in complete remission following consolidation and exclude patients who
underwent allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation. The study should also
provide an analysis of dose- and exposure-response relationships for efficacy, including
overall survival and relapse-free survival.
344
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
5. PMR #5: Conduct an observational study using electronic health records (EHR) to further
assess the risk of severe (Grades 3-4) and fatal ventricular arrhythmia events in adult
patients treated with quizartinib for the indication of acute myeloid leukemia (AML) that
is FLT3-ITD positive as detected by an FDA-approved test. The selected EHR data source
should contain access to clinical data elements including ECG results, laboratory results,
concomitant medications, and clinical data to allow for outcome validation (i.e., via
chart review). Evaluate the incidence of severe and fatal arrhythmia events and collect
detailed clinical features of the adverse reactions, to investigate associations and
temporal relationships between the incidence and severity of arrhythmia events and
other potential associated risk factors. Specify case definitions, measurement, validation
methods, and procedures for all study outcomes.
6. PMR #6: Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to assess the magnitude of change in
exposure of quizartinib and its metabolite AC886 and to determine appropriate dosing
recommendations of quizartinib in patients with severe hepatic impairment. Design and
conduct the trial in accordance with the FDA Guidance for Industry: Pharmacokinetics
in Patients with Impaired Hepatic Function: Study Design, Data Analysis, and Impact on
Dosing and Labeling.
7. PMC #1: Conduct exploratory analyses aimed at identifying potential mechanisms of
primary and acquired resistance to quizartinib using longitudinal samples for
cytogenetic and mutational analyses collected at baseline and at the end of treatment
or time of relapse from patients treated with quizartinib in QuANTUM-First. Include a
discussion of the results in the context of the available published literature.
8. PMC #2: Conduct a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to assess the magnitude of decreased
exposure of quizartinib and its metabolite AC886, and to determine appropriate dosing
recommendations for quizartinib, with coadministration of a weak CYP3A inducer.
Design and conduct the trial in accordance with the FDA Guidance for Industry: Clinical
Drug Interaction Studies – Cytochrome P450 Enzyme- and Transporter-Mediated Drug
Interactions.

OCP Appendices (Technical Documents Supporting OCP
Recommendations)
Bioanalytical Methods Section
In the AC220-A-U302 trial, a validated HPLC/MS/MS methods was used to quantitate
quizartinib and its major, active, metabolite AC886. The method is summarized below.
Bioanalytical
method
validation report
name,
amendments,
and hyperlinks

Quantification of quizartinib (AC220) and AC886 in K2EDTA human plasma by LCMS/MS (Module 5.3.1.4 Report 1000-141851-1)

Method description

Quizartinib (AC220) and AC886 were quantitatively measured from human K2 EDTA
plasma using an LC-MS/MS method. Plasma samples containing analytes were
mixed with deuterated internal standards (IS) and deproteinized by organic solvents
and filtered, followed by evaporation to dryness of the eluate and then
reconstitution of the residue. Extracted samples were analyzed by reverse-phase
chromatography with detection using a triple quadrupole mass spectrometer with
an electrospray ion source operated in positive ionization mode.
Matrix: Human K2EDTA plasma
Analytes: Quizartinib, AC886
Internal Standards: quizartinib-d4, AC886-d4
Calibration Curve concentrations: 0.500 (LLOQ), 1.00, 5.00, 25.0, 150, 300, 450, and
500 ng/mL (ULOQ)

concentration

Interference &
specificity

Hemolysis effect a

Interference & specificity were evaluated in blank matrix, LQC and HQC spiked with
the interfering drugs (and some of their metabolites) and common OTCs.
The blank matrix samples were measured to be below LLOQ. For spiked QC
responses:
Quizartinib: Bias% ranged from -8.3 to 2.7% with CV%≤2.9%.
AC886: Bias% ranged from -4.0 to -2.0% with CV%≤4.3.
No impact from co-administered drugs or common OTCs to the quantitation of either
analyte (furosemide, lansoprazole, propranolol, spironolactone and rifaximin).
Hemolytic effect was tested at LQC and HQC using plasma with 2% (v/v) of lysed
blood.
Quizartinib: Bias% ranged from -4.0 to -3.3% with CV%≤8.3%
AC886: Bias% ranged from -2.3 to 0.7% with CV%≤3.4%.
No hemolysis effect observed.

Lipemic effect a

Dilution linearity &
hook effect

Lipemic effect was tested with 6 lots of blank hyperlipidemic (2% lipid) matrix and
spiked with LLOQ level. The responses from blank hyperlipidemic matrix relative to
LLOQ were measured to be below 5% for quizartinib and below 4% for AC886.
For 6 different lipidemic blank matrix samples spiked at LLOQ level:
Quizartinib: Mean response Bias% is 0.8% with CV% ≤3.9%. AC886: Mean response
Bias% is 5.8% with CV%≤5.7%.
No lipemic effect observed.
Dilution linearity was established at 1500 and 5000 ng/mL (n = 6) using a factor of 20.
Back-calculated mean values of 6 replicates against nominal for:
Quizartinib: Bias% ranged from -3.3% to 2.6% with %CV ≤3.5% AC886: Bias% ranged
from -6.0% to 1.0 % with %CV ≤2.6%
The hook effect evaluate is not applicable for LC/MS/MS assay.

Bench-top/process
stability

Bench-top stability (short term) was established by using LQC and HQC for 24 hours
at RT.
Quizartinib: %Bias from -3.3% to -2.8% with %CV ≤5.3%
AC886 %Bias from -2.0% to 5.3% with %CV ≤4.7%
Process stability was established by using LQC and HQC for 84 hours at 2 to 8°C
Quizartinib: %Bias from -2.7% to 0.0% with %CV ≤3.0%
AC886 %Bias from -1.3% to 7.3% with %CV≤8.0%

Freeze-Thaw stability

5 freeze-thaw cycles at -20°C and -80°C were established for both analytes at LQC
(1.50 ng/mL) and HQC (400 ng/mL).

Population PK Analysis
19.4.1.1

Executive Summary

The FDA’s Assessment:
The proposed induction and consolidation dosage and dose adjustments for concomitant
strong CYP3A4 inhibitor administration are supported by the population PK (PPK) analysis.
Exposure is expected to be significantly higher following the proposed dosage during
maintenance compared to the proposed dosage during induction and consolidation. The
acceptability of exposure during maintenance treatment warranted further assessment
detailed in Section 19.4.3.2. and additional data is required to determine an appropriate
maintenance dose as described by the PMR in Section 6.2.2.3.
PPK analysis did not identify any clinically relevant differences in quizartinib or AC886 exposure
according to age, weight, sex, race, weak or moderate CYP3A4 inhibitors, mild or moderate
renal impairment, and mild or moderate hepatic impairment. The effect of severe renal
impairment and severe hepatic impairment on quizartinib and AC886 PK has not been studied.

Final Model
Software and
Version
Model Structure

Model Parameter
Estimates

Summary
NONMEM version 7.4.4
Quizartinib: a three-compartment model with sequential
zero- and first-order absorption and a first-order
elimination from the central compartment. Quizartinib PK
varied during the different treatment phases in newly
diagnosed AML subjects which was captured by including
a phase effect on the relative bioavailability (Frel) in the
final model.
AC886: a two-compartment model with first-order
elimination from the central compartment and first-order
formation. The final model included a phase effect on the
fraction metabolized from quizartinib (fMET) to correct for
the phase effect of quizartinib (Frel).

{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
The FDA’s Assessment:
The final quizartinib and AC886 population PK models are acceptable for the purpose of
characterizing the average PK profile and predicting exposure in subjects with newly diagnosed
AML for use in exposure-response (E-R) analyses.
The population PK dataset contained quizartinib and AC886 concentration data from a total of
931 subjects, which included 273 healthy volunteers (29%), 293 subjects with newly-diagnosed
AML (31%), and 365 subjects with R/R AML (39%). Continuous covariates are summarized in
non-AML subjects and for subjects with AML in the Applicant’s Table 8 and Table 9 above,
respectively. Categorical covariates are summarized for non-AML subjects and for subjects with
AML above in the Applicant’s Table 11 and Table 12 above, respectively.
The Reviewer was able to verify the Applicant’s final quizartinib and AC886 PPK models without
significant discrepancies in final parameter estimates. The Applicant’s Table 20 and Table 25 in
the PPK Assessment Summary above report the standard deviations for inter-individual
variability (IIV) and residual unexplained variability (RUV) instead of the coefficient of variation
(CV%). Additionally, relative standard error (RSE) percentages in the Applicant’s Table 20 and
Table 25 were calculated incorrectly. The summary of the Reviewer’s final parameter estimates,
including corrected statistics for IIV and RUV, are presented in Table 112 for the final quizartinib
model and in Table 113 for the final AC886 model.
