Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
The final AC886 model included typical values of quizartinib PK parameters that were fixed to
the final estimates from the final quizartinib model. Individual values of IIV for quizartinib
parameters were set to the individual posthoc IIV from the quizartinib PPK model. However, the
AC886 PPK model structure did not include quizartinib TLAG or quizartinib relative bioavailability
(F1). Although the AC886 model estimated the fraction of quizartinib that was metabolized into
AC886 (fMET) and included covariate effects of treatment phase on fMET, the AC886 model did
not incorporate the effects of moderate and strong CYP3A inhibitors on F1 which were included
in the final quizartinib model. Therefore, the AC886 model assumed that subjects receiving
concomitant CYP3A inhibitors would have the same proportion of quizartinib metabolized into
AC886 compared with subjects without concomitant CYP3A inhibitors.
The quizartinib and AC886 goodness of fit plots (Figures A7-5, A7-6, A7-7 for quizartinib and
Figures A7-19, A7-20, and A7-21 for AC886 in the Applicant’s PPK Assessment Summary)
indicate adequate model fit. Although the relatively even distribution of CWRES around zero in
both models indicates relatively small bias, some observed quizartinib and AC886 observed
concentrations had relatively high absolute values of CWRES. The range of CWRES was -5.4 to
5.1, and no significant associations between any covariates and CWRES were identified. The
final PPK models are adequate for characterizing average quizartinib and AC886 concentrations
but may not accurately characterize individual concentrations.
Covariate Effects
The predicted impacts of covariates on exposure are displayed in the Applicant’s Figure 43 and
Figure 44 in the PPK Assessment Summary for quizartinib and AC886, respectively. Figure 31
and Figure 32 display the median exposure values corresponding to the Applicant’s Figure 43
and Figure 44, respectively. Of note, the Applicant’s forest plot of the sum of quizartinib and
AC886 exposure (Figure 45 in the PPK Assessment Summary) was generated by adding
quizartinib and AC886 exposure together without accounting for differences in molecular
weight, which may result in errors in relative exposure difference according to covariates.

Body Weight
In the final quizartinib PPK model, higher body weight was associated with higher clearance and
higher volume with allometric scaling (see Table 112). The median quizartinib AUCss, Cmax,ss, and
Cmin,ss are 22 to 23% higher in a typical patient weighing 65 kg compared to a typical patient
weighing 85 kg, as shown in Figure 31.
The observed OS appeared better in the quizartinib arm compared to the placebo arm across all
weight quartiles, as displayed in Figure 33. The difference in exposure according to body weight
is not expected to have clinically relevant impact on clinical outcomes, and no dose adjustments
based on body weight are warranted based on current data.
Figure 33. Study AC220-A-U302 Overall Survival versus Treatment Arm Across Baseline Body
Weight Quartiles

Weight quartile 1 = 36.8 to 61.6 kg (n=67 quizartinib; n=65 placebo). Weight quartile 2 = 61.8 to 70.2 kg (n=68
quizartinib; n=64 placebo). Weight quartile 3 = 70.3 to 82 kg (n=66 quizartinib; n=66 placebo). Weight
quartile 4 = 82.1 to 151.6 kg (n=58 quizartinib; n=73 placebo). Q = quartile.
Source: Reviewer’s analysis of Applicant’s exposure-response efficacy dataset

Renal Impairment
After accounting for body weight, CLcr was not associated with any clinically significant
383
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the

Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
differences in quizartinib or AC886 exposure. The PPK analysis supports the recommendation
that no dose adjustment is needed in patients with mild to moderate renal impairment. The
effect of severe renal impairment (CLcr <30 mL/min) on quizartinib pharmacokinetics has not
been studied.
Hepatic Impairment
In the PPK dataset, 139/931 (15%) of subjects had baseline mild hepatic dysfunction and 16/931
(1.7%) subjects had baseline moderate hepatic dysfunction according to NCI-ODWG
classification. Quizartinib exposure did not differ significantly according to hepatic dysfunction
category. In Study AC220-A-U302, subjects with moderate hepatic dysfunction had 25% lower
AC886 AUCss following planned dosing of 35.4 mg quizartinib daily during induction compared
to subjects with normal hepatic function. The difference in AC886 exposure is not expected to
be clinically significant.
The PPK analysis of hepatic function is consistent with the dedicated hepatic impairment
studies AC220-016 and AC220-A-U105, which are described in Section 6.3.2.3. No dose
adjustment is needed in patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment. The
pharmacokinetics of quizartinib have not been studied in patients with severe hepatic
impairment.
Race and Ethnicity
In the PPK analysis, PK parameters did not significantly differ according to Caucasian race
category or Asian race category (Caucasian [n=603] compared to not Caucasian [n=328]; Asian
[n=169] compared to not Asian [n=762]). However, the PPK model identified statistically
significant impacts of Black or African American race (Black or African American [n=79]
compared to not Black or African American [n=852]) on quizartinib and AC886 clearance. Black
or African American subjects had 35% higher median quizartinib AUCss (95% CI = 9 to 83%
higher AUCss), 30% higher median quizartinib Cmax,ss (95% CI = 7 to 70% higher Cmax,ss), 33%
lower median AC886 AUCss (95% CI = 23 to 40% lower AUCss), and 33% lower median AC886
Cmax,ss (95% CI = 23 to 42% lower Cmax,ss) compared to subjects who did not identify as Black or
African American (see Figure 31 and Figure 32).
Study AC220-A-U302 only included 2 Black or African American subjects in the quizartinib arm
and so the subgroup analysis could not adequately evaluate whether identifying as Black or
African American race was associated with differences in clinical safety due to potentially
higher exposure. However, E-R analyses did not find any E-R safety associations where 30 to
35% higher quizartinib exposure would result in higher risk of TEAEs (see Section 19.4.3.4).
Therefore, Black and African American patients with previously untreated AML are not
expected to have clinically significant differences in quizartinib exposure compared to patients

of other races.
Regarding ethnicity, the PPK analysis did not identify any differences in PK parameters in Asian
patients according to Japanese, Korean, Chinese, Taiwanese, or Hongkongese subgroups.
Potential PK parameter differences in subjects with Hispanic/Latino ethnicity were not
evaluated due to lack of Hispanic/Latino ethnicity data.
Treatment Phase
The model fit was improved when a Study AC220-A-U302 treatment phase effect was included.
The Applicant included a treatment phase effect on F1 in the final quizartinib model, which
resulted in a statistically significant improvement in objective function value (OFV) compared to
a treatment phase effect on CL or no treatment phase effect at all.
The increase in quizartinib dose-normalized concentrations from induction to consolidation and
then from consolidation to continuation may be due to changes in disease characteristics over
the course of treatment. Although the PPK model adequately fits the current data, the apparent
effect of treatment phase on F1 is likely explained by a correlated mechanism that is more
physiologically plausible than an impact on F1. For instance, time-dependent clearance or a
time-varying disease characteristic effect on quizartinib clearance may characterize PK
differences more accurately than the model’s treatment phase effect on F1. The
model-predicted F1 of quizartinib should not be used in place of absolute bioavailability
determined from clinical bioavailability data.
Median exposure following 35.4 mg once daily is 1.4-fold higher during consolidation compared
to induction (AUCss = 4290 versus 3086 ng∙hr/mL). Cmax,ss is strongly impacted by F1 even
though the treatment phase effect on F1 is physiologically unlikely, and so the PPK model may
over-predict the impact of treatment phase on Cmax,ss.
Quizartinib and AC886 exposure is higher during maintenance treatment compared to
induction or consolidation due to lower clearance during maintenance as well as the higher
proposed dose during maintenance. Median AUCss is 3.7-fold higher following the proposed
53 mg dose during continuation versus the proposed 35.4 mg dose during induction
(AUCss = 11286 versus 3086 ng∙hr /mL).
Based on E-R analysis of safety (Section 19.4.3.4) and E-R analysis of efficacy according to
maintenance exposure (Section 19.4.3.2), the proposed quizartinib maintenance dose of 53 mg
daily may result in worse QTcF prolongation and other safety events without improving survival
compared to lower quizartinib maintenance dosages. The PMR in Section 6.2.2.3 addresses
these concerns with the quizartinib maintenance dose and exposure.

{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
CYP3A Inhibitor Coadministration
The PPK analysis supports the conclusion that no dosage adjustment is needed for concomitant
weak or moderate CYP3A inhibitor coadministration. The PPK analysis also supports the
proposed dosage reductions in patients with concomitant use of CYP3A inhibitors (see Section
6.3.2.4).
The quizartinib PPK model predicted 12% higher median quizartinib exposure (95% CI = 9 to
15%) with coadministration of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor (see Figure 31). This difference in
quizartinib exposure is consistent with the results of Study AC220-015, which found that
moderate CYP3A inhibitor coadministration resulted in 11% higher quizartinib Cmax and 20%
higher quizartinib AUC in healthy subjects. The difference in quizartinib exposure following
coadministration with a moderate CYP3A inhibitor is ≤20% and is not expected to be clinically
relevant.
The quizartinib PPK model predicted that coadministration of a strong CYP3A inhibitor will
result in 74% higher (95% CI = 67 to 80% higher) median quizartinib Cmax,ss and 83% higher (95%
CI = 75 to 90% higher) median quizartinib AUCss. The predicted difference in AUC is consistent
with the results of Study AC220-015, which found that strong CYP3A inhibitor coadministration
resulted in 17% higher quizartinib Cmax and 94% higher quizartinib AUC in healthy subjects. The
PPK model may overpredict the impact on Cmax because the model included strong CYP3A
inhibitor administration impacts on both CL and F1. Although this may have resulted in better
model fit, there is no clear physiologic mechanism explaining why CYP inhibitor administration
would impact quizartinib F1. However, the model-predicted impact on AUC supports the
degree of exposure difference observed in Study AC220-015.
CYP3A Inducer Coadministration
Only 36/931 (3.9%) subjects with PPK data had any recorded coadministration of a weak CYP3A
inducer, and 1/931 (0.1%) subject had recorded coadministration of a moderate or strong
CYP3A inducer. PPK data is inadequate to assess the impact of weak, moderate, or strong
CYP3A inducer coadministration on quizartinib or AC886 PK. Due to the potential for
concomitant CYP3A inducer administration to decrease quizartinib exposure and impact
effectiveness, the recommendation to avoid coadministration of quizartinib with moderate or
strong CYP3A inducers is reasonable.

Study AC220-A-U302 quizartinib (Table 114) and AC886 (Table 115) exposures following
planned dosage were calculated from individual predicted PK parameters for each treatment
phase. Study AC220-A-U302 elimination half-lives and accumulation ratios during maintenance

Table 116. Summary statistics of half-life and accumulation ratios in the maintenance phase
for quizartinib and AC886 in study AC220-A-U302.

The effective half-life (t1/2eff) was calculated as 24 • ln(2) / ( ln (Rac / Rac - 1)).
The accumulation ratio (Rac) was calculated as AUCss / AUC0-24. Individual values of AUCss and AUC0-24 were
calculated following 53 mg quizartinib daily during maintenance treatment without dose reductions or
interruptions, as well as no concomitant administration of CYP3A inhibitors. Steady state is achieved at Day 15.
AUC0-24 = area under the concentration-versus-time curve from time zero to 24 hours following a single dose of
quizartinib; AUCss = steady state area under the concentration-versus-time curve during one dosing interval;
h = hours; ln=natural log; Rac = accumulation ratio; SD = standard deviation; t1/2eff = effective half-life.

Exposure-Response Analysis
19.4.3.1. E-R Efficacy Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
E-R analysis identified that improvement in OS may be associated with treatment arm
(quizartinib versus placebo) or with higher quizartinib induction exposure (AUCss) following
planned dosing of 35.4 mg daily. The data do not adequately support the conclusion that there
is an E-R association with OS due to the high level of uncertainty. No E-R associations were
identified between quizartinib induction exposure and EFS or CR rate.
The Reviewer also assessed OS and EFS according to maintenance exposure in subjects who
received at least one maintenance dose. Maintenance exposure was not associated with any
clear differences in OS or EFS. Due to confounding factors such as a potential carryover effect
from quizartinib induction and consolidation as well as the QTcF-dependent maintenance
dosage in the protocol, E-R analysis cannot support the conclusion that quizartinib maintenance
389
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
treatment has a clinical benefit.
Importantly, E-R analysis indicates that the proposed 53 mg daily maintenance dosage may
expose patients to unnecessary toxicity such as QT prolongation and other safety events
without an improvement in survival compared to lower maintenance doses. The need for
additional data to determine an appropriate quizartinib maintenance dose is addressed by the
PMR in Section 6.2.2.3.

Multivariate E-R modeling did not identify any clear associations between EFS or CR rate and
quizartinib AUCss following planned dosage of 35.4 mg once daily.
Observed OS according to quizartinib exposure quartile is displayed in Figure 34. The lowest
quizartinib AUCss quartile did not appear to have significantly different OS compared to
placebo. Quizartinib quartiles Q2, Q3, and Q4 generally had better OS than placebo or Q1.
However, the 95% CIs for all quartiles overlap with each other and there is no clear association
between AUCss and OS.

Several confounding factors may impact OS, such as age or baseline disease characteristics, and
so multivariate E-R modeling was performed to account for potential confounding. The final OS
model used a time-varying hazard function according to a Gompertz distribution, and both
lower age and increased induction AUCss were associated with better OS. The final model
parameter estimates from the Reviewer’s run of the final model are summarized in Table 119.
The value of the estimated AUCss slope is slightly different than the value in the Applicant’s
Table 20 in the E-R Efficacy Assessment Summary above due to discovery of a calculation error;
however, conclusions from the model did not differ.

The VPC plots in the Applicant’s Figure 23 and Figure 24 in the E-R Efficacy Assessment
Summary display the variability in OS according to the Applicant’s final model. However, VPC
plots do not display uncertainty of model parameters or predictions, which is a critical
consideration in model selection. Although the Applicant’s final OS model included a
statistically significant effect of quizartinib exposure on OS, reviewer analysis found that the
exposure effect could be replaced by a treatment arm effect (i.e., quizartinib versus placebo)
without a significant difference in model fit (OFV 4467.8 versus 4471.4, difference of 3.6
points). Therefore, it is uncertain whether the apparent effect on overall survival is truly due to
quizartinib AUCss or due to treatment cohort (i.e., quizartinib or placebo). The use of exposure
following planned dosing rather than actual dosing records also contributed to the uncertainty
of an exposure effect on OS. The data do not adequately support the conclusion that higher
quizartinib exposure is associated with better OS.
Maintenance E-R Efficacy in Subjects who Received at least one Maintenance Dose
The maintenance E-R analysis contained data for 116 subjects who received at least one
maintenance dose of quizartinib and 92 subjects who received at least one maintenance dose
of placebo in Study AC220-A-U302.
Subjects receiving quizartinib were less likely to receive a maximum individual dose of 53 mg,
which is the full proposed once daily maintenance dose, compared to placebo. Subjects who
received quizartinib had significantly higher rates of dose modifications during the maintenance
phase than subjects who received placebo. The maximum individual maintenance dose and
rates of dose modifications are summarized in Table 120 and Table 121, respectively.

OS and maintenance Cavg,TTE were also compared after stratifying by HSCT status, as shown in
Figure 36, due to the potential impact of HSCT on OS. Observed OS did not differ significantly
across maintenance Cavg,TTE quartiles in subjects without HSCT or in subjects with HSCT, which is
consistent with the overall analysis.
In subjects with HSCT, observed OS in the placebo arm also overlapped greatly with the
quizartinib arm which suggests that quizartinib maintenance treatment may not provide a
survival benefit in subjects with HSCT. In subjects without HSCT, all quartiles of maintenance
Cavg,TTE appeared to have generally better OS compared to placebo although this finding is

uncertain due to overlapping confidence intervals between treatment arms. Additionally, all
subjects who received quizartinib maintenance also received quizartinib induction and

Q3 and Q4) compared to the two lowest quartiles (i.e., Q1 and Q2), confidence intervals
overlapped across all exposure quartiles as shown in Figure 37. The Kaplan-Meier plot does not
adequately support an association between Cavg,TTE and EFS. Additionally, EFS in maintenance
Cavg,TTE Q1 and Q2 overlap with EFS in the placebo arm. The confidence intervals of all Cavg,TTE
quartiles overlap with the placebo arm as well.

Observed EFS was also compared across maintenance Cavg,TTE quartiles after stratifying by HSCT
status, as shown in Figure 38. Maintenance Cavg,TTE Q3 and Q4 still appeared to have generally
better EFS compared to Q1 and Q2, although the overlap in confidence intervals indicates
uncertainty of this difference. In the subgroup with HSCT, observed EFS did not appear to differ
significantly across Cavg,TTE exposure quartiles or the placebo arm which suggests that quizartinib
maintenance treatment may not provide an EFS benefit in subjects with HSCT.

Because the potential impact of quizartinib Cavg,TTE on survival may be confounded by other
patient characteristics, the Reviewer conducted multivariate Cox proportional hazard modeling
for the primary efficacy endpoint of OS in subjects with at least one maintenance dose. Actual
maintenance dosage records were used to calculate maintenance Cavg,TTE.
Potential effects from the following covariates were investigated: treatment arm, quizartinib
maintenance Cavg,TTE, AC886 maintenance Cavg,TTE, quizartinib induction AUCss following nominal
dosing of 35.4 mg QD, baseline body weight, baseline ECOG score, sex, age, race, maintenance
dose escalation status, AML cytogenetic risk score (favorable versus intermediate versus
unfavorable), NPM1 mutation status, baseline white blood cell count ( <40 x 10^9/L versus
≥40 x 10^9/L), HSCT status, choice of anthracycline during induction, and region.
The model with the best fit, presented in Table 123, included effects from age and HSCT. Lower
age and HSCT were associated with improved OS.

Evaluation of effectiveness associated with the maintenance dosage is complicated by several
issues. Due to the study design of Study AC220-A-U302 and lack of re-randomization prior to
maintenance treatment, the effects of quizartinib exposure and treatment during induction and
consolidation phases could not be isolated from the effect of quizartinib exposure and
treatment during maintenance. Subjects with higher maintenance Cavg,TTE tended to have higher
induction exposure as well. It is not certain how survival after initiation of maintenance
treatment is impacted by quizartinib maintenance independent of the impact of quizartinib
induction and consolidation. Additionally, QTcF and related patient characteristics may
confound the analysis because of the QTcF-dependent dose escalation during maintenance
treatment.
After accounting for age and HSCT, no clear association was identified between OS and
maintenance exposure or maintenance treatment (quizartinib versus placebo). The E-R
406
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
maintenance analysis did not find evidence that quizartinib maintenance treatment has a
clinical benefit. Clinical analyses determined that quizartinib maintenance was beneficial (see
Section 8), but the E- E-R maintenance analysis did not find evidence that higher maintenance
exposure is associated with improved effectiveness. Given the significantly higher exposure in
maintenance treatment compared to induction or consolidation (refer to Section 19.4.1.2) and
the potential for higher exposures to result in worse QTcF prolongation (refer to Section
19.4.3.4) and worse tolerability, the proposed quizartinib maintenance dosage of 53 mg QD
may exposure patients to toxicity without providing a survival benefit compared to lower
maintenance dosages. Evaluation of lower maintenance dosages is warranted in order to
evaluate this significant safety concern. The PMR in Section 6.2.2.3 addresses the need for
additional data to adequately evaluate maintenance quizartinib dosage.
19.4.3.3. E-R Safety Executive Summary
The FDA’s Assessment:
Worse QTcF prolongation was associated with higher quizartinib concentrations. Individual
observed QTcF prolongations varied widely in subjects receiving quizartinib, and QTcF
measurements over +100 ms were observed in Study AC220-A-U302. The median QTcF
prolongation for Cmax,ss in the typical subject is +18.4 ms following 26.5 mg daily maintenance
treatment and +24.1 ms following 53 mg daily maintenance treatment.
Higher quizartinib induction exposure may result in higher incidence of TEAE leading to dose
interruption. Although E-R safety analysis was limited by use of exposure calculated with
planned dosing rather than actual dosage records, no E-R associations were identified with
infection, TEAE leading to dose reduction, TEAE leading to drug withdrawn, or Grade ≥3
cytopenia incidence from the lab dataset.

The FDA’s Assessment:
QTcF Prolongation
The E-R analysis of QTcF prolongation included data from 531 subjects with newly-diagnosed
AML in Study AC220-A-U302, which included 256 subjects with at least one pair of
time-matched QTcF and PK measurements after first treatment dose.
The change in QTcF was characterized by an Emax model which included effects from clock
time, age, hypokalemia, and individual predicted induction AUCss following planned dosage of
35.4 mg once daily as described in the Applicant’s Table 28 in the E-R Safety Assessment
Summary above and with 95% confidence intervals in Table 126. Of note, the Applicant’s Table
28 in the E-R Safety Assessment contains an error in CV and RSE calculation; see final parameter
estimates with corrected variance and RSE values in Table 126. The model fit was adequate for
the purpose of predicting median change in QTcF according to quizartinib concentration.

Source: Table 28 in Applicant’s Exposure-Response Report and Reviewer’s analysis of Applicant’s run39-mod-cqt

Higher quizartinib concentration was associated with greater change in QTcF. The median
predicted change in QTcF according to quizartinib concentration is compared to observed data
in the Applicant’s Figure 31 in the E-R Safety Assessment Summary, which shows that variability
in QTcF prolongation with quizartinib treatment is relatively wide. QTcF prolongations over
100 ms were observed in subjects receiving quizartinib. Both the predicted and observed QTcF
in the VPC plots (Applicant’s Figure 32 in the E-R Safety Assessment Summary) show the 95th
percentile of QTcF exceeding 450 ms even at quizartinib concentrations below 100 ng/mL.
The model did not identify any significant associations between time-varying concomitant QT
prolonging drugs and QTcF prolongation. The model also did not identify any association
between time-varying concomitant beta blocker use and QTcF prolongation, but the lack of
apparent association may be due to the small number of subjects with any recorded beta
blocker use (49 out of 531 subjects total).
Quizartinib exposure is expected to be higher during maintenance treatment compared to
induction or consolidation (see Section 19.4.1.2). Therefore, exposure during maintenance
treatment is likely to result in the highest QTcF prolongation. Table 127 summarizes the median
model-predicted QTcF prolongation at the Cmax,ss of a typical Study AC220-A-U302 patient (i.e.,
70.3 kg, 56 year old male) following the proposed maintenance dosages of 26.5 mg or 53 mg
once daily.
The Cmax,ss in the typical patient (293 ng/mL following nominal maintenance dosing of 26.5 mg
QD; 586 ng/mL following nominal maintenance dosing of 53 mg QD) was slightly higher than
the median Cmax,ss of the Study AC220-A-U302 population with at least one maintenance dose
of quizartinib (n=115) (270 ng/mL following nominal maintenance dosing of 26.5 mg QD;
541 ng/mL following nominal maintenance dosing of 53 mg QD). The predicted Cmax,ss in the
typical subject during maintenance treatment adequately represents average exposure in Study
AC220-A-U302.

Overall, the E-R analysis characterizes the median QTcF prolongation with quizartinib
treatment. The median QTcF prolongation for Cmax,ss in the typical subject is +18.4 ms following
26.5 mg daily and +24.1 ms following 53 mg daily during maintenance.
Incidence of Adverse Events
The E-R safety analysis of TEAE incidence included data from 258 subjects with newlydiagnosed AML who received quizartinib and 267 subjects with newly-diagnosed AML who
received placebo from Study AC220-A-U302
As summarized in the Applicant’s Table 31 in the E-R Safety Assessment Summary, the
Applicant’s E-R analysis did not identify any associations between quizartinib AUCss following
planned dosage of 35.4 mg once daily during induction and incidence of serious TEAE, TEAE
leading to dose reduction or interruption, TEAE leading to discontinuation, infection,
hemorrhage, hepatotoxicity, or myelosuppression in the adverse event dataset (“adae.xpt”). In
the Applicant’s Table 31, the term myelosuppression refers to one or more of the following
adverse events in the adverse event dataset (adae.xpt): febrile neutropenia, anemia,
thrombocytopenia, neutropenia, leukopenia, platelet count decreased, white blood cell count
decreased, neutrophil count decreased, pancytopenia, lymphocyte count decreased, hemoglobin
decreased, hematocrit decreased, lymphopenia, or red blood cell count decreased.
Table 128 summarizes the Reviewer’s additional E-R safety analysis of select TEAEs of interest
across quizartinib induction AUCss quartiles. Higher induction AUCss was associated with a
higher incidence of TEAE leading to drug interruption. No other TEAEs in Table 128 appeared to
have higher incidence in higher quizartinib AUCss quartiles. Overall, subjects who received
quizartinib had higher rates of TdP/QT prolongation, TEAE leading to drug interruption, TEAE
leading to dose reduction, TEAE leading to drug discontinuation, and TEAE leading to any dose
modification compared to placebo in all exposure quartiles.

Although rates of cytopenias and other TEAEs were not associated with induction quizartinib
AUCss, the E-R analysis did not investigate TEAE incidence according to exposure following
actual dosage records in induction or other treatment phases. E-R analysis using actual
exposure instead of planned exposure may be more likely to identify safety associations, and so
the current E-R analysis is relatively limited. The exposure-response relationships for the safety
of quizartinib have not been fully characterized.
19.4.3.5. Overall Benefit-risk Evaluation Based on E-R Analyses
The Applicant’s Position:
The E-R analyses support the overall favorable benefit-risk for quizartinib. The E-R analysis on
OS predicted a median (95% CI) HR for quizartinib compared to the placebo of 0.790 (0.690,
0.933) at the median quizartinib exposure and thus further demonstrated survival benefit from
quizartinib treatment in combination with standard chemotherapy followed by continuation
monotherapy in newly diagnosed AML subjects with FLT3-ITD (+) AML. Quizartinib prolongs
QTcF in a concentration-dependent manner, which was appropriately managed in Study AC220A-U302 through dose modification, including QTcF-based dosing, correction of electrolyte
abnormalities, and dose adjustments for concomitant use with strong CYP3A inhibitors.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Overall, the concentration-dependent increase in QTcF interval has been characterized and the
proposed dose modifications for QTcF prolongation are appropriate. The PPK and E-R analyses
support a positive benefit-risk profile for the proposed dosage of 35.4 mg once daily for
induction and consolidation treatment in subjects with newly diagnosed FLT3-ITD (+) AML.
The E-R analyses identified significant known risk and unclear benefit with the proposed
maintenance dosage of 53 mg once daily. In subjects who received at least one maintenance
dose, quizartinib exposure during maintenance treatment was not associated with
improvements in OS or EFS after accounting for the effects of age and HSCT. Higher quizartinib
concentrations are associated with greater QTcF prolongation, and quizartinib treatment can
increase risk of safety events. Therefore, the E-R analysis indicates that 53 mg once daily during
maintenance may expose patients to unnecessary toxicity without an improvement in survival.
Additional data is required to determine an appropriate maintenance dose of quizartinib, and
the known risk of quizartinib maintenance is addressed by the PMR in Section 6.2.2.3.
Additionally, E-R analysis did not provide supportive evidence of the effectiveness of quizartinib
maintenance treatment.

Physiology Based Pharmacokinetics (PBPK) Analyses
19.4.4.1. PBPK Executive Summary
The aim of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s PBPK report titled
“development of a PBPK model for quizartinib in Simcyp population-based simulator and
evaluation of DDI risk as a perpetrator of UGT1A1” to support the intended uses. Specifically,
the Applicant applied the PBPK modeling approach to assess the DDI potential of quizartinib as
an inhibitor of UGT1A1 and specifically, the effects of quizartinib on the exposure of raltegravir
(a UGT1A1 substrate), following single dose administration of raltegravir.
The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the PBPK report, supporting modeling files and
concluded the following:
1) The submitted PBPK models are adequate to assess the inhibitory potential of quizartinib on
the exposure of raltegravir (a UGT1A1 substrate) in the healthy volunteer.
2) The effect of quizartinib on a UGT1A1 substrate (such as raltegravir) is expected to be not
clinically significant.
19.4.4.2. Methods
The Applicant’s Position:
PBPK model structure
The PBPK analyses were performed using the population based PBPK software Simcyp® (V20
Simcyp Ltd., a Certara Company, Sheffield, United Kingdom). The PBPK models for quizartinib
were developed based on in vitro and in vivo information and clinical PK data. The final input
parameters and the method to obtain the values of input parameters for PBPK model of
quizartinib are summarized in Table 129.

The FDA’s Assessment:
In the PBPK model of quizartinib, the first-order absorption model was used. The fugut value
was estimated using the build-in function provided by Simcyp. The upper bound and lower
bound of fu is likely between 0.0088 and 1. Permeability of quizartinib was estimated by
comparing the simulated and observed DDI between quizartinib and dabigatran etexilate.
Thus, the confidence in the estimated permeability value is subjected, not limited to, to the
PBPK model of dabigatran etexilate and the assigned ki value of P-gp for quizartinib. Sensitivity
of Qgut on the effect of quizartinib on exposure of raltegavir should be conducted.
PBPK model refinement and validation
The Applicant’s Position:
The PBPK model was validated against the adult PK studies following single oral dose of 30 mg,
60 mg and 90 mg Quizartinib respectively in healthy subjects (AC220-014, AC220-015, AC220018, AC220-019 and AC220-A-U106) and multiple oral doses of 30 mg and 60 mg quizartinib QD
respectively in AML patients (2689-CL-2004).
The FDA’s Assessment:
The Applicant’s PBPK model were verified with the PK dataset relevant to clinical conditions.
The proposed initial dosing for quizartinib is 35.4 mg QD for two weeks for patients taking
standard chemotherapy. Different dosing regiments are proposed for patients at different
stages (for example, patients who achieved complete remission (CR)). The proposed doses
range from 26.5 mg QD to 53 mg QD. Ideally, the PBPK model should be verified with a wide
range of clinical PK dataset which covered the full range of clinical dosing regiments. Given the
PPK analysis concludes that quizartinib showed linear pharmacokinetics in the dose range of
26.5 mg to 79.5 mg in healthy subjects and 17.7 mg to 53 mg QD in AML patients. The
Applicant’s modeling development and verification approach is acceptable.
Raltegravir PBPK model
The Applicant’s Position:
The default compound library file for raltegravir was used. Prediction performance of SVRaltegravir model was verified with clinically observed data following 400 mg SD and 400 mg
BID raltegravir treatment, respectively. A developer’s summary of development and validation
of the PBPK model of raltegravir is provided by the applicant.
The FDA’s Assessment:

The applicability of raltegravir PBPK model as a UGT1A substrate was discussed in PBPK review
for asciminib (NDA 215358). The Reviewer noted there is a limited validation of the DDI
predictive performance of raltegravir model as it was conducted using clinical DDI data of a
single inhibitor, atazanavir. Raltegravir PBPK model is considered acceptable as a UGT1A
substrate for purpose of risk assessment.
19.4.4.3. Results
Can the PBPK model adequately describe the PK profiles of quizartinib?
The Applicant’s Position:
The final PBPK model for quizartinib, based on both in vitro and in vivo data was able
to generate plasma concentration-time profiles and exposure levels of quizartinib after
a single oral dose of quizartinib 60 mg and an intravenous dose of 14C-quizartinib
50 μg in healthy male subjects that were within 1.5-fold of observed data, respectively.
The model was validated against 7 sets of PK data following a single oral dose of
quizartinib (30 - 90 mg). Plasma concentration-time profiles were recovered and
AUCinf and Cmax were within 2-fold of the observed data. In addition, 2 sets of PK data in AML
patients (30 mg QD and 60 mg QD) were used to validate the model to simulate multiple
dosing. The ability of PBPK modeling to describe the clinical PK dataset at various dosing levels
is presented in Figure 39.

The FDA’s Assessment:
The submitted PBPK model can describe the PK profiles of quizartinib and adequate to support
the DDI analysis with a substrate of enzyme pathway (such as UGT1A1). The fa of quizartinib
was calculated to be 0.737 by dividing the observed F (0.7147) by FG (0.992) and FH (0.977).
However, to recover the lower plasma exposure of quizartinib in AML patients, the fa was
modified by using the ratio of simulated and observed Cmax (1.86) on the first day of the 60 mg

original fa value of 0.737 was used. Thus, a higher plasma exposure of quizartinib at steady
state compared to those observed in clinical were used to conduct PBPK DDI assessment.
Is the inhibitory effect of quizartinib on Raltegravir (an UGT1A1 substrate) is expected to be
clinically significant?
The Applicant’s Position:
Sensitivity analysis of quizartinib Ki for UGT1A1 inhibition and fugut were performed by the
Applicant respectively. Briefly, plasma raltegravir PK following a single oral dose of 400 mg in the
absence of quizartinib (Day 1) and on the 18th day of 20 days of dosing of quizartinib (60 mg QD)
to healthy subjects were simulated using 10-fold and 20-fold reduced Ki values of quizartinib for
UGT1A1 (0.078 and 0.039 μM). The impacts of different Ki values on the simulated AUCinf and
Cmax ratios of raltegravir are shown in Table 130.

approximately 1.5.

The FDA’s Assessment:
As discussed in the Methods section (19.4.4.2), permeability of quizartinib was estimated by
comparing the simulated and observed DDI between quizartinib and dabigatran etexilate. The
estimated permeability was then used to derive the Qgut value. Thus, the reviewer conducted
additional sensitivity analysis to evaluate the effect of Qgut on the predicted inhibitory effect of
quizartinib on raltegravir. The results of sensitivity analysis (not presented) showed that there
are minimal changes in the predicted AUC ratio for raltegravir (<2% change) when different
Qgut (1-20 L/hr) were used in the simulations.
Reviewer also conduct a simulation of worst-case scenario using Ki of 0.039 µM, fugut of 1 and
Qgut of 20 L/h. The predicted AUC ratio for raltegravir was approximately 1.5 (Table 131).
Reviewer concluded that the effect of quizartinib on a UGT1A1 substrate (such as raltegravir) is
expected to be not clinically significant or weak.

Additional Analyses of Efficacy in the Continuation Phase
Applicant’s Analyses
To address the potential confounding issues arising from the unbalance in the prognostic
factors between two treatment arms during the Continuation Phase, the sponsor provided
additional analysis based on propensity scores per FDA’s request. By accounting for any
differences in measured baseline characteristics, the propensity score analysis mimics what
would have been achieved through randomization in a randomized clinical trial. In contrast to
true randomization, it should be emphasized that the propensity score can only account for
measured confounders, not for any unmeasured confounders. The variables that the Applicant
started with for the propensity score analysis are listed below:
• (at study baseline) age, sex, race, geographical region, ECOG performance status, choice
of anthracycline, AML cytogenetic risk score, FLT3-ITD allelic burden, NPM1 mutational
status, percentage of bone marrow blasts, and WBC count at the time of diagnosis of
AML.
• (at the initiation of the Continuation Phase): number of induction cycles, time from
randomization to start of Continuation Phase, receipt of HSCT, best response in the
Induction phase, ECOG performance status, WBC count (as a continuous variable) and
choice of conditioning regimen.
The Applicant chose two sets of covariates from above by selecting any unbalanced variables
between two arms (1) at study baseline or the initiation of the Continuation Phase, or (2) at the
initiation of the Continuation Phase, and then applied four commonly used propensity score
methods to evaluate the treatment effect in the Continuation Phase for OS and RFS:
stratification, matching, inverse probability treatment weighting (IPTW) and covariate
adjustment based on propensity score. The corresponding results of OS overall and by HSCT
status are displayed in Table 133 based on unbalanced variables between two arms at study
baseline or initiation of Continuation. HR estimations for OS in the Continuation Phase from
propensity score methods range from 0.72 to 0.77, which are slightly greater than the
unadjusted HR of 0.68. Among 119 patients who initiated continuation with HSCT, the
425
Version date: July 2021 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.
Reference ID: 5211984

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation {NDA 216993}
{VANFLYTA; quizartinib}
estimated HR from propensity score methods range from 1.61 to 1.83 in favor of placebo.
Among 89 patients who initiated continuation without HSCT, the estimated HR from propensity
score methods range from 0.4 to 0.46 in favor of quizartinib. These results are supportive of the
previous conclusion that the benefit of quizartinib after HSCT as continuation therapy is
questionable.

Additional FDA Analyses
Landmark analyses are survival analyses which consider only a subset of patients who have
lived at least a specified amount of time. These analyses help to address immortal time bias and
allow for comparisons to patients who did not experience the post-randomization event of

interest. FDA conducted a landmark analysis in which only patients who survived at least 1 year
after randomization are included. The choice of 1 year as the landmark time is due to (1) most
patients in Continuation Analysis Set entered Continuation within 1 year after randomization,
and (2) most patients with HSCT received it within 1 year after randomization. Figure 41 shows
the comparison of OS between treatment arms for the subgroup of patients who entered
continuation by 1 year versus not and those who received HSCT by 1 year versus not. Figure
41(d) suggests the quizartinib treatment improves OS for patients who receive continuation
therapy without HSCT; in contrast, in Figure 41(b) the placebo curve stays on top of the
quizartinib curve, making it unclear if patients derive benefit from continuation after HSCT.

While the above analyses correct for immortal time bias by removing patients who did not live
long enough to reach HSCT or continuation therapy, they do not account for granular
differences in times to these events. To address these limitations, an expanded Cox model was
implemented. The expanded model includes a time-dependent indicator for HSCT and another
time-dependent indicator for initiation of continuation therapy. Results from the expanded
model are presented in Table 135, where the treatment effect of quizartinib during
Continuation in terms of HR is estimated to 1.67 (95% CI: 0.64, 4.33) in favor of placebo among
patients who received HSCT and continuation, and 0.42 (0.20, 0.88) in favor of quizartinib
among patients who did not receive HSCT and continuation. These results are similar to the
unadjusted results.
Table 135: Estimation of treatment effect of quizartinib on OS in the continuation phase
based on the expanded Cox model (Continuation Analysis Set)

Similar analyses were conducted on RFS. As for the adjusted OS analyses, the adjusted analyses
of RFS were similar to the unadjusted RFS analyses.
As documented above, FDA requested the Applicant to conduct their own independent analysis
of the effect of quizartinib 1) in the continuation setting and 2) with emphasis on possible
differential effects for HSCT vs. non-HSCT patients. The results are similar to those presented
above. These analyses defined the HSCT subgroup as patients who received protocol-specified
HSCT rather than any HSCT.
While the analysis above suggests benefit for patients who receive quizartinib continuation
without HSCT, this analysis is speculative and makes various untestable, strong assumptions.
One such assumption is that the treatment effect (i.e., HR) due to any treatment period
(induction/consolidation, continuation) is constant. For instance, the above analysis estimates
the effect of induction/consolidation quizartinib therapy on OS to be 0.87 (95% CI: 0.66, 1.15).
Though it is possible that continued quizartinib continuation increases this treatment effect for
patients who do not receive HSCT, it is also possible that this treatment effect would increase
on its own over time without further treatment. Similar logic applies to patients who receive
HSCT and subsequent quizartinib continuation therapy. Because QuANTUM-First was not rerandomized during the continuation phase, more rigorous comparisons cannot be made.
Effect of MRD in Continuation
Among 124 patients in MRD- Continuation Analysis Set, 70 (56%) and 54 (44%) started the
continuation therapy with and without HSCT respectively. The OS analysis results among these
patients and a breakdown by the HSCT status are provided in Figure 44. There is a separation of
Kaplan-Meier curves is in favor of quizartinib (HR = 0.43 [95% CI: 0.18, 1.03]), although such an
effect is mostly driven by the patients without HSCT prior to continuation (HR = 0.28 [95% CI:
0.09, 0.89]). Whereas among the patients with HSCT prior to continuation, the survival curves
overlap for most of the time, which suggests lack of benefit of the quizartinib treatment as
continuation therapy in this subgroup.
